| `9112 8-9-10` | informative | HTTP/1.1 has no request identifier; response association relies entirely on responses arriving in the order requests were made on the connection. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | it relies on the order of response arrival to correspond exactly to the order in which requests are made on the same connection. | Response demux must be strictly FIFO per connection; 1xx responses are additional messages preceding the final response for the same request. |
| `9112 8-9-11` | MUST | A client with more than one outstanding request on a connection must maintain a list of those requests in the order sent. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client that has more than one outstanding request on a connection MUST maintain a list of outstanding requests in the order sent | If pipelining is ever enabled, keep an ordered FIFO queue of in-flight requests per connection. |
| `9112 8-9-12` | MUST | A client must associate each received response to the first outstanding request that has not yet received a final (non-1xx) response. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | MUST associate each received response message on that connection to the first outstanding request that has not yet received a final (non-1xx) response. | Match responses to queue head; a 1xx does not pop the queue — only the final non-1xx response does. |
| `9112 8-9-13` | MUST NOT | Data received on a connection with no outstanding requests must not be treated as a valid response. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | If a client receives data on a connection that doesn't have outstanding requests, the client MUST NOT consider that data to be a valid response | Bytes arriving on an idle pooled connection must never be parsed as a response for a later request; mark the connection unusable. |
| `9112 8-9-14` | SHOULD | On unsolicited data the client should close the connection (delimitation is ambiguous) unless the data is only one or more CRLF, which may be discarded. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | the client SHOULD close the connection, since message delimitation is now ambiguous, unless the data consists only of one or more CRLF | Idle-connection reader: discard bare CRLFs; on any other unsolicited bytes, evict and close the connection instead of reusing it. |
| `9112 8-9-15` | SHOULD | HTTP implementations should support persistent connections. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | HTTP implementations SHOULD support persistent connections. | Client must implement keep-alive/connection reuse as the default mode, not close-per-request. |
| `9112 8-9-16` | informative | Persistence is determined from the protocol version and Connection header field of the most recently received message. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A recipient determines whether a connection is persistent or not based on the protocol version and Connection header field (Section 7.6.1 of [HTTP]) in the most recently received message | After each response, re-evaluate connection reusability from that response's version + Connection options (algorithm in 8-9-17..20). |
| `9112 8-9-17` | informative | If the "close" connection option is present, the connection will not persist after the current response. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | If the "close" connection option is present (Section 9.6), the connection will not persist after the current response | Response with Connection: close → do not return connection to pool; close after reading the response. |
| `9112 8-9-18` | informative | Otherwise, if the received protocol is HTTP/1.1 or later, the connection persists after the current response. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | If the received protocol is HTTP/1.1 (or later), the connection will persist after the current response | HTTP/1.1 response without close option → connection is reusable by default (no keep-alive header needed). |
| `9112 8-9-19` | informative | Otherwise, an HTTP/1.0 message persists only if the "keep-alive" option is present, the recipient is not a proxy or the message is a response, and the recipient wishes to honor HTTP/1.0 keep-alive. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | If the received protocol is HTTP/1.0, the "keep-alive" connection option is present, either the recipient is not a proxy or the message is a response | HTTP/1.0 response is reusable only when Connection: keep-alive is present and the client opts into HTTP/1.0 keep-alive; otherwise close. |
| `9112 8-9-20` | informative | In all remaining cases the connection will close after the current response. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The connection will close after the current response. | Default branch of the persistence algorithm: treat connection as non-reusable and close after the response is read. |
| `9112 8-9-21` | MUST | A client that does not support persistent connections must send "close" in every request. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client that does not support persistent connections MUST send the "close" connection option in every request message. | If a non-pooling / one-shot mode exists, it must emit Connection: close on every request it sends. |
| `9112 8-9-22` | MUST | A server that does not support persistence must send "close" in every non-1xx response. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A server that does not support persistent connections MUST send the "close" connection option in every response message that does not have a 1xx (Informational) status code. | Server-only; client side is covered by honoring the received close option (8-9-58). |
| `9112 8-9-23` | MAY | A client may keep sending requests on a persistent connection until it sends or receives "close", or receives an HTTP/1.0 response without "keep-alive". <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client MAY send additional requests on a persistent connection until it sends or receives a "close" connection option or receives an HTTP/1.0 response without a "keep-alive" connection option. | Pool reuse gate: stop reusing a connection once close is sent/received or an HTTP/1.0 response lacked keep-alive. |
| `9112 8-9-24` | informative | To remain persistent, every message on the connection needs a self-defined length (not delimited by connection close). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | In order to remain persistent, all messages on a connection need to have a self-defined message length (i.e., one not defined by closure of the connection) | A response delimited by close (no Content-Length/chunked) makes the connection non-reusable; requests the client sends must always carry Content-Length or chunked. |
| `9112 8-9-25` | MUST | A server must read the entire request body or close the connection after responding. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A server MUST read the entire request message body or close the connection after sending its response | Server-only; client analogue is 8-9-26. |
| `9112 8-9-26` | MUST | A client must read the entire response body before reusing the connection for a subsequent request. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | a client MUST read the entire response message body if it intends to reuse the same connection for a subsequent request. | Before returning a connection to the pool, fully drain (or verify fully read) the response body; if the caller abandoned the body, close instead of reuse. |
| `9112 8-9-27` | MUST NOT | A proxy must not maintain a persistent connection with an HTTP/1.0 client. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | A proxy server MUST NOT maintain a persistent connection with an HTTP/1.0 client | Proxy-only; no action for a pure client. |
| `9112 8-9-28` | informative | Implementations ought to anticipate recovering from asynchronous close events at any time. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Connections can be closed at any time, with or without intention. Implementations ought to anticipate the need to recover from asynchronous close events. | Treat unexpected close on a pooled connection as a normal event: detect, evict, and (where permitted) transparently retry on a fresh connection. |
| `9112 8-9-29` | informative | The conditions for automatically retrying outstanding requests are those of RFC 9110 Section 9.2.2 (idempotency rules). <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | The conditions under which a client can automatically retry a sequence of outstanding requests are defined in Section 9.2.2 of [HTTP]. | Auto-retry logic must gate on RFC 9110 §9.2.2: retry idempotent requests; do not auto-retry non-idempotent ones that may have been processed. |
| `9112 8-9-30` | MAY | A client supporting persistent connections may pipeline requests. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client that supports persistent connections MAY "pipeline" its requests (i.e., send multiple requests without waiting for each response). | Pipelining is optional; if implemented, all pipelining constraints (8-9-11/12, 8-9-33..35) apply. |
| `9112 8-9-31` | MAY | A server may process pipelined requests in parallel only if all have safe methods. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A server MAY process a sequence of pipelined requests in parallel if they all have safe methods | Server-only. |
| `9112 8-9-32` | MUST | A server must send responses in the same order the requests were received. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | it MUST send the corresponding responses in the same order that the requests were received. | Server obligation the client's FIFO association (8-9-12) depends on; nothing extra to implement client-side. |
| `9112 8-9-33` | SHOULD | A pipelining client should retry unanswered requests if the connection closes before all responses arrive. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client that pipelines requests SHOULD retry unanswered requests if the connection closes before it receives all of the corresponding responses. | On premature close of a pipelined connection, requeue the unanswered tail (subject to 8-9-29 idempotency conditions). |
| `9112 8-9-34` | MUST NOT | When retrying pipelined requests after a failed connection, the client must not pipeline immediately after establishing the new connection. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | When retrying pipelined requests after a failed connection (a connection not explicitly closed by the server in its last complete response), a client MUST NOT pipeline immediately after connection establishment | On the retry connection, send only the first remaining request and wait for its response before pipelining again (TCP reset can erase an error response). |
| `9112 8-9-35` | SHOULD NOT | A user agent should not pipeline requests after a non-idempotent method until that method's final response arrives, unless it can recover from partial failure. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | A user agent SHOULD NOT pipeline requests after a non-idempotent method, until the final response status code for that method has been received | Pipeline scheduler must stall the queue behind any non-idempotent (e.g., POST) request until its final response, absent explicit recovery support. |
| `9112 8-9-36` | MAY | An intermediary may pipeline received pipelined requests when forwarding inbound. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | An intermediary that receives pipelined requests MAY pipeline those requests when forwarding them inbound | Intermediary-only. |
| `9112 8-9-37` | MAY | On inbound connection failure, a pipelining intermediary may retry the unanswered sequence only if all requests are idempotent. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | the pipelining intermediary MAY attempt to retry a sequence of requests that have yet to receive a response if the requests all have idempotent methods | Intermediary-only. |
| `9112 8-9-38` | SHOULD | Otherwise the pipelining intermediary should forward received responses and close the corresponding outbound connections so user agents can recover. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | the pipelining intermediary SHOULD forward any received responses and then close the corresponding outbound connection(s) | Intermediary-only. |
| `9112 8-9-39` | informative | A client ought to limit the number of simultaneous open connections it maintains to a given server. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client ought to limit the number of simultaneous open connections that it maintains to a given server. | Pool must have a configurable per-host connection cap; do not open unbounded parallel connections. |
| `9112 8-9-40` | informative | No specific maximum connection count is mandated; clients are encouraged to be conservative when opening multiple connections. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | this specification does not mandate a particular maximum number of connections but, instead, encourages clients to be conservative when opening multiple connections. | Choose a modest default per-host limit; make it tunable rather than hardcoding the old 2-connection rule. |
| `9112 8-9-41` | informative | Persistent connections place no requirements on the length or existence of idle timeouts for client or server. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | The use of persistent connections places no requirements on the length (or existence) of this timeout for either the client or the server. | Client may choose any idle-connection timeout policy, but must tolerate servers closing idle connections at any time (8-9-44). |
| `9112 8-9-42` | SHOULD | A client or server wishing to time out should issue a graceful close on the connection. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | A client or server that wishes to time out SHOULD issue a graceful close on the connection. | Idle-reaper should close connections gracefully (and for TLS, send close_notify per 8-9-71), not abort/RST. |
| `9112 8-9-43` | SHOULD | Implementations should constantly monitor open connections for a received closure signal and respond appropriately. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Implementations SHOULD constantly monitor open connections for a received closure signal and respond to it as appropriate | Keep a reader (or readable-event check) on idle pooled connections so peer close/EOF is detected promptly and the connection is evicted before reuse. |
| `9112 8-9-44` | MAY | A client, server, or proxy may close the transport connection at any time. <br>_(client,server,proxy [CLIENT])_ | A client, server, or proxy MAY close the transport connection at any time. | Design for the idle-close race: a request written to a just-closed connection must be detected and retried per 8-9-29 rather than surfaced as failure. |
| `9112 8-9-45` | SHOULD | A server should sustain persistent connections and use transport flow control to resolve overloads rather than closing and expecting client retries. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A server SHOULD sustain persistent connections, when possible, and allow the underlying transport's flow-control mechanisms to resolve temporary overloads | Server-only. |
| `9112 8-9-46` | SHOULD | A client transmitting a message body should monitor the connection for an error response while transmitting. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client sending a message body SHOULD monitor the network connection for an error response while it is transmitting the request. | While streaming a request body, concurrently read the connection so an early error response (e.g., 4xx) is noticed before the upload finishes. |
| `9112 8-9-47` | SHOULD | If the response indicates the server rejects the body and is closing, the client should immediately stop transmitting and close its side. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | the client SHOULD immediately cease transmitting the body and close its side of the connection. | On early final response with Connection: close during upload, abort the body write and close the client side instead of finishing the upload. |
| `9112 8-9-48` | informative | The "close" connection option signals that the sender will close the connection after completing the response. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The "close" connection option is defined as a signal that the sender will close this connection after completion of the response. | Parse the Connection header's option list case-insensitively and recognize "close" semantics in both directions. |
| `9112 8-9-49` | SHOULD | A sender should send a Connection header with the "close" option when it intends to close the connection. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A sender SHOULD send a Connection header field (Section 7.6.1 of [HTTP]) containing the "close" connection option when it intends to close a connection. | When the client decides a request is its last on a connection, emit Connection: close on that request. |
| `9112 8-9-50` | informative | The field name "Close" is reserved because it could conflict with the "close" connection option. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | the field name "Close" is reserved, since using that name as a header field might conflict with the "close" connection option. | Never emit a header field literally named "Close"; reject/avoid it in the request-building API. |
| `9112 8-9-51` | MUST NOT | A client that sends "close" must not send further requests on that connection. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client that sends a "close" connection option MUST NOT send further requests on that connection (after the one containing the "close") | Once a request with Connection: close is written, mark the connection non-reusable so the pool never schedules another request on it. |
| `9112 8-9-52` | MUST | A client that sends "close" must close the connection after reading the final response to that request. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | MUST close the connection after reading the final response message corresponding to this request. | After the final (non-1xx) response to a close-flagged request is fully read, actively close the connection (for TLS, per 8-9-71/72). |
| `9112 8-9-53` | MUST | A server receiving "close" must initiate connection closure after sending the final response to that request. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A server that receives a "close" connection option MUST initiate closure of the connection (see below) after it sends the final response to the request that contained the "close" connection option. | Server-only; client should expect the connection to close after such a response. |
| `9112 8-9-54` | SHOULD | A server receiving "close" should echo a "close" option in its final response on the connection. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | The server SHOULD send a "close" connection option in its final response on that connection. | Server-only; client must not rely on the echo — closing is required regardless (8-9-52). |
| `9112 8-9-55` | MUST NOT | A server that received "close" must not process any further requests on that connection. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | The server MUST NOT process any further requests received on that connection. | Server-only; reinforces client rule 8-9-51 (further requests would be ignored). |
| `9112 8-9-56` | MUST | A server that sends "close" must initiate closure after sending the response containing it. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A server that sends a "close" connection option MUST initiate closure of the connection (see below) after it sends the response containing the "close" connection option. | Server-only; client-side counterpart is 8-9-58. |
| `9112 8-9-57` | MUST NOT | A server that sent "close" must not process further requests received on that connection. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | The server MUST NOT process any further requests received on that connection. | Server-only; means pipelined requests already in flight past a close response are dead (client rule 8-9-59). |
| `9112 8-9-58` | MUST | A client receiving "close" must stop sending requests on that connection and close it after reading the close-bearing response. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client that receives a "close" connection option MUST cease sending requests on that connection and close the connection after reading the response message containing the "close" connection option | On Connection: close in a response: immediately bar new requests on that connection, finish reading that response body, then close and evict from pool. |
| `9112 8-9-59` | SHOULD NOT | After receiving "close", the client should not assume already-pipelined requests will be processed by the server. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | if additional pipelined requests had been sent on the connection, the client SHOULD NOT assume that they will be processed by the server. | Treat pipelined requests queued behind a close-bearing response as unanswered: requeue/retry per idempotency rules, never assume success. |
| `9112 8-9-60` | informative | An immediate server close risks a TCP reset erasing the client's unread buffers, so the client may fail to read the last response. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | If a server performs an immediate close of a TCP connection, there is a significant risk that the client will not be able to read the last HTTP response. | Explains 8-9-34: after ECONNRESET, the final response may be lost; drain the receive buffer before closing your own side and be conservative on retry. |
| `9112 8-9-61` | informative | Servers avoid the reset problem by closing in stages: half-close the write side, keep reading until the client closes or the last response is acknowledged, then fully close. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | To avoid the TCP reset problem, servers typically close a connection in stages. | Server technique; client should still promptly close its side after reading the final response so the server's staged close completes. |
| `9112 8-9-62` | informative | A client half-close does not delimit a request message and does not mean the client no longer wants the response. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | a TCP connection that is half-closed by the client does not delimit a request message, nor does it imply that the client is no longer interested in a response. | Never use shutdown(write) as an end-of-request signal; request framing must come from Content-Length or chunked encoding only. |
| `9112 8-9-63` | informative | For HTTPS, the HTTP client acts as the TLS client, initiating the handshake with ClientHello and sending the first request only after the handshake finishes. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | When the TLS handshake has finished, the client may then initiate the first HTTP request. | Complete the TLS handshake before writing any HTTP bytes; no early data unless separately negotiated (out of scope here). |
| `9112 8-9-64` | MUST | All HTTP data over TLS must be sent as TLS application data; the connection is otherwise treated normally, including persistent reuse. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | All HTTP data MUST be sent as TLS "application data" | Write HTTP bytes only through the TLS record layer as application data; keep-alive/pooling rules apply identically to TLS connections. |
| `9112 8-9-65` | informative | Receipt of a valid TLS closure alert assures that no further data will be received on that connection. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | When a valid closure alert is received, an implementation can be assured that no further data will be received on that connection. | Treat close_notify as authoritative clean EOF; distinguish it from transport-level EOF/reset when judging response completeness (8-9-69). |
| `9112 8-9-66` | informative | An implementation that has all the data it cares about might generate an "incomplete close": send a closure alert and close without waiting for the peer's alert. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | it might generate an "incomplete close" by sending a closure alert and then closing the connection without waiting to receive the corresponding closure alert from its peer. | Understand and support the incomplete-close pattern in both directions (client may generate it per 8-9-72; must tolerate it per 8-9-70). |
| `9112 8-9-67` | informative | Since TLS is unaware of HTTP framing, HTTP data itself must be examined to determine whether messages are complete after an incomplete close. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | it is necessary to examine the HTTP data itself to determine whether messages are complete. | Completeness after TLS close is decided by HTTP framing (Content-Length/chunked), never by the TLS layer alone. |
| `9112 8-9-68` | SHOULD | On an incomplete close, a client should treat as completed all requests for which it received the full Content-Length of data or the terminal zero-length chunk. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | When encountering an incomplete close, a client SHOULD treat as completed all requests for which it has received either | After incomplete TLS close: responses satisfying their Content-Length, or chunked responses that reached the zero-length chunk, count as complete; others are incomplete. |
| `9112 8-9-69` | informative | A response with neither chunked coding nor Content-Length is complete only if a valid closure alert was received; treating incomplete as complete invites truncation attacks. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A response that has neither chunked transfer coding nor Content-Length is complete only if a valid closure alert has been received. | For read-until-close HTTPS bodies, require close_notify before marking complete; plain TCP EOF without it = incomplete (security-relevant). |
| `9112 8-9-70` | SHOULD | A client detecting an incomplete close should recover gracefully. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client detecting an incomplete close SHOULD recover gracefully. | Missing peer close_notify must not crash or hang the client; classify per 8-9-68/69 and continue (retry/evict as appropriate). |
| `9112 8-9-71` | MUST | Clients must send a TLS closure alert before closing the connection. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | Clients MUST send a closure alert before closing the connection. | Connection close path for TLS must call CloseWrite/close_notify (Go crypto/tls Conn.Close does this) — never bare TCP close on a TLS connection. |
| `9112 8-9-72` | MAY | Clients expecting no more data may close without waiting for the server's closure alert, generating an incomplete close on the server side. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | Clients that do not expect to receive any more data MAY choose not to wait for the server's closure alert and simply close the connection | After sending close_notify, the client need not block waiting for the server's alert; immediate close is permitted. |
| `9112 8-9-73` | SHOULD | Servers should be prepared to receive an incomplete close from the client. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | Servers SHOULD be prepared to receive an incomplete close from the client, since the client can often locate the end of server data. | Server-only; justifies the client's 8-9-72 shortcut. |
| `9112 8-9-74` | MUST | Servers must attempt to initiate an exchange of closure alerts before closing the connection. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | Servers MUST attempt to initiate an exchange of closure alerts with the client before closing the connection. | Server-only; client benefit: absence of close_notify is abnormal and marks read-until-close responses incomplete (8-9-69). |
| `9112 8-9-75` | MAY | Servers may close immediately after sending the closure alert, producing an incomplete close on the client side. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | Servers MAY close the connection after sending the closure alert, thus generating an incomplete close on the client side. | Server-only permission; client must therefore handle incomplete close on its side gracefully (8-9-68/70). |

### RFC 9112 10-11 - Enclosing Messages as Data; Security Considerations (response splitting, request smuggling, integrity, confidentiality)

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|--------------|---------------------|
| `9112 10-11-1` | informative | The message/http media type encloses exactly one HTTP request or response and must obey MIME 'message' type restrictions on line length and encodings. <br>_(all [not-client])_ | The "message/http" media type can be used to enclose a single HTTP request or response message, provided that it obeys the MIME restrictions for all "message" types regarding line length and encodings. | N/A for the wire client; only applies if the library ever serializes captured messages as message/http (it does not today). |
| `9112 10-11-2` | informative | Field values inside a message/http payload are permitted to use obsolete line folding (obs-fold) to span multiple lines, unlike on-wire HTTP/1.1. <br>_(all [not-client])_ | field values within "message/http" are allowed to use line folding (obs-fold), as described in Section 5.2, to convey the field value over multiple lines | Only if the library ever parses message/http bodies: its parser must tolerate obs-fold there even though the wire parser rejects/handles it per Section 5.2. |
| `9112 10-11-3` | MUST | Any recipient consuming message/http data must replace each obsolete line fold with one or more SP characters before using the field value. <br>_(all [not-client])_ | A recipient of "message/http" data MUST replace any obsolete line folding with one or more SP characters when the message is consumed. | The client treats response bodies (including TRACE's message/http) as opaque bytes, so no obligation; if a message/http parser is ever added, it must rewrite obs-fold to SP. |
| `9112 10-11-4` | informative | message/http defines optional media-type parameters 'version' and 'msgtype'; when absent, version and message type are determined from the first line of the enclosed body. <br>_(all [not-client])_ | Optional parameters: version, msgtype | Only if parsing message/http payloads: honor version/msgtype parameters, falling back to the enclosed start-line when they are absent. |
| `9112 10-11-5` | informative | message/http content may only be transferred with 7bit, 8bit, or binary content-transfer encodings. <br>_(all [not-client])_ | only "7bit", "8bit", or "binary" are permitted | N/A for the wire client; MIME-embedding concern only. |
| `9112 10-11-6` | informative | The application/http media type encloses a pipeline of one or more HTTP messages that are all requests or all responses, never intermixed. <br>_(all [not-client])_ | The "application/http" media type can be used to enclose a pipeline of one or more HTTP request or response messages (not intermixed). | Only if the library ever parses or emits application/http pipelines: enforce homogeneous message type within one payload. |
| `9112 10-11-7` | informative | application/http defines the same optional 'version' and 'msgtype' parameters, determinable from the first line of the body when absent. <br>_(all [not-client])_ | Optional parameters: version, msgtype | Same as 10-11-4, for application/http payloads. |
| `9112 10-11-8` | informative | application/http payloads are binary; an appropriate Content-Transfer-Encoding is required if transmitted via email. <br>_(all [not-client])_ | HTTP messages enclosed by this type are in "binary" format; use of an appropriate Content-Transfer-Encoding is required when transmitted via email. | N/A — email-transport concern outside the HTTP client. |
| `9112 10-11-9` | informative | Response splitting (CRLF injection) attacks work by exploiting HTTP's line-based framing and the ordered pairing of requests to responses on persistent connections, so recipients must not be lax about framing. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | exploits the line-based nature of HTTP message framing and the ordered association of requests to responses on persistent connections | Keep the response parser strict: exact CRLF line framing, reject stray/bare CR-LF and control octets in the header section, and associate responses to outstanding requests strictly in order — never resync mid-stream after a framing anomaly (check http1 response reader in http1/). |
| `9112 10-11-10` | informative | The effective defense against splitting is that only the core protocol library may emit CR or LF into the header section — header output must go through APIs that filter bad octets. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | A more effective mitigation is to prevent anything other than the server's core protocol libraries from sending a CR or LF within the header section | Mirror the principle on the request-emit path: the client's header-serialization API must reject or filter CR, LF (and NUL) in caller-supplied field names and values so an application cannot inject extra fields or requests (request splitting). |
| `9112 10-11-11` | informative | Request smuggling exploits parser disagreements between recipients to hide extra requests inside an apparently harmless request; implementations must avoid producing or tolerating ambiguously framed messages. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | exploits differences in protocol parsing among various recipients to hide additional requests (which might otherwise be blocked or disabled by policy) within an apparently harmless request | Never emit a request with ambiguous framing: exactly one framing mechanism per message (no Transfer-Encoding + Content-Length together, no duplicate/differing Content-Length), so no two parsers can disagree about where the client's request ends. |
| `9112 10-11-12` | informative | RFC 9112's stricter message-framing rules in Section 6.3 exist specifically to defeat request smuggling and must be implemented exactly, not loosely. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | This specification has introduced new requirements on request parsing, particularly with regard to message framing in Section 6.3, to reduce the effectiveness of request smuggling. | Implement the Section 6.3 message-body-length algorithm verbatim on the response path (TE overrides CL, invalid/conflicting CL is unrecoverable, close-delimited fallback) and treat violations as connection-fatal — this is the smuggling defense the RFC points at. |
| `9112 10-11-13` | informative | HTTP has no built-in integrity mechanism; completeness detection depends entirely on transport error detection plus length- or chunk-delimited framing, so framing checks are the integrity check. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | relying on the error-detection ability of underlying transport protocols and the use of length or chunk-delimited framing to detect completeness | Verify each received body against its framing: exactly Content-Length octets, or chunked stream terminated by a last-chunk + trailer/CRLF; any mismatch is an incomplete/invalid message error, never silently truncated success. |
| `9112 10-11-14` | informative | The https scheme's authenticated encryption protects messages from modification in transit; clients get that protection only by actually using it. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The mechanisms provided with the "https" scheme, such as authenticated encryption, provide protection against modification of messages. | Ensure https-scheme requests always run over the TLS transport (authenticated encryption); no code path may downgrade an https request to plaintext. |
| `9112 10-11-15` | informative | Implementations must take care that connection closure cannot be used to truncate messages undetected (ties to Section 9.8 close handling). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Care is needed, however, to ensure that connection closure cannot be used to truncate messages (see Section 9.8). | A peer close arriving before the framed end (Content-Length not yet satisfied, or no last-chunk seen) must surface as a typed incomplete-message error from Do/DoStream — never as a clean EOF that looks like a complete response. |
| `9112 10-11-16` | informative | User agents are permitted to refuse incomplete messages or handle them specially (e.g., flagging truncation to the user). <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | User agents might refuse to accept incomplete messages or treat them specially. | Expose incompleteness as a distinct, detectable condition (typed error or response flag) so callers can implement refuse/special-handling policy; do not fold it into generic I/O errors. |
| `9112 10-11-17` | informative | The plain http scheme provides no protection whatsoever against accidental or malicious message modification. <br>_(all [not-client])_ | The "http" scheme provides no protection against accidental or malicious modification of messages. | No code obligation; document that http-scheme traffic has no integrity guarantee and integrity-sensitive callers should use https. |
| `9112 10-11-18` | informative | Message confidentiality is delegated to the underlying transport, selected via the URI scheme (or user-agent configuration). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | HTTP relies on underlying transport protocols to provide message confidentiality when that is desired. | The client's scheme-to-transport mapping is the confidentiality mechanism: https must deterministically select the encrypted (TLS) connection type at dial time. |
| `9112 10-11-19` | informative | The https scheme identifies resources that require a confidential connection, per RFC 9110 Section 4.2.2. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The "https" scheme can be used to identify resources that require a confidential connection, as described in Section 4.2.2 of [HTTP]. | Treat https as a hard requirement for a confidential connection: refuse to send an https-scheme request over any non-TLS connection, including on pooled-connection reuse. |

### RFC 9110 2-3 - RFC 9110 §§2-3 — Conformance (syntax notation, requirements, lengths, error handling, protocol version) and Terminology/Core Concepts

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|--------------|---------------------|
| `9110 2-3-1` | informative | The grammar of the spec is expressed in RFC 5234 ABNF extended with RFC 7405 case-sensitive string notation; parsers/emitters must interpret productions accordingly. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | This specification uses the Augmented Backus-Naur Form (ABNF) notation of [RFC5234], extended with the notation for case-sensitivity in strings defined in [RFC7405]. | Implement every quoted production per RFC 5234 ABNF semantics; treat unmarked literal strings as case-insensitive, %s-prefixed as case-sensitive. |
| `9110 2-3-2` | informative | The spec defines a '#' list operator (Section 5.6.1) for compact comma-separated list productions; Appendix A gives the expanded standard ABNF. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | It also uses a list extension, defined in Section 5.6.1, that allows for compact definition of comma-separated lists using a "#" operator | Field-value parsers must expand #rule productions to comma-separated-list ABNF (with the §5.6.1 empty-element tolerances) when validating or emitting list-typed fields. |
| `9110 2-3-3` | informative | ABNF rule names prefixed 'obs-' are obsolete grammar retained for historical reasons. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | ABNF rule names prefixed with "obs-" denote obsolete grammar rules that appear for historical reasons. | Parser may need to accept obs- forms where grammar references them (e.g., obs-fold, obs-text elsewhere); emitter must never produce them (follows from 2-3-9 since obs- forms are generation-restricted). |
| `9110 2-3-4` | informative | Core ABNF rules ALPHA, CR, CRLF, CTL, DIGIT, DQUOTE, HEXDIG, HTAB, LF, OCTET, SP, VCHAR are incorporated by reference from RFC 5234 Appendix B.1. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The following core rules are included by reference, as defined in Appendix B.1 of [RFC5234] | Byte-classification tables in the parser (e.g., tchar/VCHAR/CTL checks) must match the RFC 5234 core-rule definitions exactly (VCHAR = %x21-7E, CTL = %x00-1F / %x7F, etc.). |
| `9110 2-3-5` | informative | BCP 14 keywords are normative only when they appear in all capitals. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | are to be interpreted as described in BCP 14 [RFC2119] [RFC8174] when, and only when, they appear in all capitals | When mapping spec text to conformance tests, treat only all-caps MUST/SHOULD/MAY etc. as binding; lowercase 'must'/'should' is prose. |
| `9110 2-3-6` | informative | Requirements are scoped by participant role (sender, recipient, client, server, user agent, intermediary, origin server, proxy, gateway, cache). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | requirements are placed on senders, recipients, clients, servers, user agents, intermediaries, origin servers, proxies, gateways, or caches, depending on what behavior is being constrained by the requirement. | The client library must satisfy the union of sender, recipient, client, and user-agent requirement classes; server/proxy/cache-scoped rules apply only if those roles are added. |
| `9110 2-3-7` | informative | 'generate' scopes a requirement to the implementation that creates the protocol element, unlike 'send' which also covers forwarding. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The verb "generate" is used instead of "send" where a requirement applies only to implementations that create the protocol element | When reading later requirements: 'generate' rules bind the client for elements it originates (request line, its own headers) but not for caller-supplied values merely relayed verbatim only if the library is a forwarder — as an originator, poseidon is bound by generate-rules. |
| `9110 2-3-8` | informative | An implementation is conformant iff it complies with all requirements of every role it takes on. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | An implementation is considered conformant if it complies with all of the requirements associated with the roles it partakes in HTTP. | Conformance checklist for the client must cover all sender+recipient+client+user-agent rows; partial compliance in any held role breaks conformance. |
| `9110 2-3-9` | MUST NOT | A sender must not emit any protocol element that fails its ABNF grammar. <br>_(all,client [CLIENT])_ | A sender MUST NOT generate protocol elements that do not match the grammar defined by the corresponding ABNF rules. | Validate everything written to the wire — method token, request-target, version, field names (token), field values (field-content), chunk sizes — against the governing ABNF before emission; reject or sanitize caller input that violates it (header-injection guard). |
| `9110 2-3-10` | MUST NOT | A sender must not emit protocol elements or syntax alternatives reserved for other roles in that message. <br>_(all,client [CLIENT])_ | a sender MUST NOT generate protocol elements or syntax alternatives that are only allowed to be generated by participants in other roles | Client must never emit server-only constructs (status lines, response-only fields as originator semantics) or proxy-only syntax alternatives (e.g., absolute-form target on a non-proxy connection, per RFC 9112 §3.2). |
| `9110 2-3-11` | informative | Conformance requires both correct messaging syntax of the version in use and correct semantics of elements sent; a client claiming HTTP/1.1 must recognize features required of HTTP/1.1 recipients. <br>_(all,client [CLIENT])_ | Conformance to HTTP includes both conformance to the particular messaging syntax of the protocol version in use and conformance to the semantics of protocol elements sent. | Before emitting 'HTTP/1.1' in the request line the client must implement all mandatory 1.1 recipient features (chunked decoding, 1xx handling, persistent-connection semantics); the spec text explicitly warns a client that fails this will fail to interoperate. |
| `9110 2-3-12` | MAY | A recipient may implement workarounds for known-broken sender implementations while staying conformant, provided the workarounds are limited to the faulty implementations. <br>_(all,client [CLIENT])_ | A recipient MAY employ such workarounds while remaining conformant to this protocol if the workarounds are limited to the implementations at fault. | Any server-quirk handling (e.g., keyed on Server field value) must be gated to the identified faulty peer, never applied as blanket lenient behavior. |
| `9110 2-3-13` | SHOULD | A recipient should parse received protocol elements defensively, expecting grammar violations and unreasonable sizes. <br>_(all,client [CLIENT])_ | A recipient SHOULD parse a received protocol element defensively, with only marginal expectations that the element will conform to its ABNF grammar and fit within a reasonable buffer size. | Response parser must use bounded buffers with explicit limits (status line, field section, chunk-size line) and fail gracefully on malformed or oversized input — never trust peer input for allocation sizing (matches repo Peer-input policy). |
| `9110 2-3-14` | MUST | A recipient must be able to parse protocol elements at least as long as the ones it itself generates. <br>_(all,client [CLIENT])_ | a recipient MUST be able to parse and process protocol element lengths that are at least as long as the values that it generates for those same protocol elements | Check that every parse-side limit (max URI length, max field-line size, max field-section size) is >= the maximum the client will emit for the same element; enforce emission caps <= parse caps. |
| `9110 2-3-15` | MUST | A recipient must interpret received protocol elements per the defined semantics (including extensions), unless it has determined the specific sender implements them incorrectly. <br>_(all,client [CLIENT])_ | A recipient MUST interpret a received protocol element according to the semantics defined for it by this specification, including extensions to this specification | No bespoke reinterpretation of status codes or header semantics in the response path; deviations only as targeted per-sender workarounds (see 2-3-12). |
| `9110 2-3-16` | MAY | Unless the spec notes otherwise, a recipient may attempt to recover a usable protocol element from an invalid construct. <br>_(all,client [CLIENT])_ | Unless noted otherwise, a recipient MAY attempt to recover a usable protocol element from an invalid construct. | Error recovery is optional and forbidden where the spec explicitly requires rejection (security-impacting cases like Content-Length/Transfer-Encoding conflicts); a load-gen client may legitimately choose strict-fail everywhere. |
| `9110 2-3-17` | informative | Certain requests can be automatically retried by a client after an underlying connection failure, under the conditions of Section 9.2.2. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | Some requests can be automatically retried by a client in the event of an underlying connection failure, as described in Section 9.2.2. | The client's retry layer must gate automatic retry on the §9.2.2 conditions (idempotency / no-data-processed) — cross-check the pool/retry logic against that section, not just transport errors. |
| `9110 2-3-18` | informative | The HTTP version number is two decimal digits separated by a period. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | HTTP's version number consists of two decimal digits separated by a "." (period or decimal point). | Emit exactly 'HTTP/1.1' (DIGIT '.' DIGIT); parser must accept single-digit major.minor in the status line and treat anything else as malformed. |
| `9110 2-3-19` | informative | Major version indicates messaging syntax; minor version indicates the highest minor version within that major to which the sender is conformant. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The first digit (major version) indicates the messaging syntax, whereas the second digit (minor version) indicates the highest minor version within that major version to which the sender is conformant | Sending minor '1' is a conformance claim to all of HTTP/1.1, not a per-request feature flag; the client must interpret a server's 'HTTP/1.0' vs 'HTTP/1.1' response version when deciding keep-alive/chunked expectations. |
| `9110 2-3-20` | informative | The protocol version as a whole indicates conformance with that version's corresponding specification set (for HTTP/1.1: RFC 9110 + Caching + RFC 9112). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The protocol version as a whole indicates the sender's conformance with the set of requirements laid out in that version's corresponding specification(s). | Conformance target for the http1 package is RFC 9110 + RFC 9112 together (RFC 9111 only if a cache is added); the checklist must span both documents. |
| `9110 2-3-21` | informative | The minor version advertises sender capabilities even when only a backwards-compatible subset is being used, letting servers use more advanced features in responses. <br>_(all,client [CLIENT])_ | The minor version advertises the sender's communication capabilities even when the sender is only using a backwards-compatible subset of the protocol | Always send HTTP/1.1 (never downgrade to 1.0 for 'simple' requests) and be prepared for the server to respond with any 1.1 feature (chunked, trailers, 1xx) on every exchange. |
| `9110 2-3-22` | informative | A major version with no defined minor versions has implied minor version '0', used where an element requires a minor version identifier. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | When a major version of HTTP does not define any minor versions, the minor version "0" is implied. | When formatting versions of HTTP/2 or HTTP/3 in elements demanding major.minor (e.g., Via), use '2.0'/'3.0'; affects only such contexts, not ALPN tokens. |
| `9110 2-3-23` | informative | A resource cannot treat a request inconsistently with the semantics of its method; e.g., a safe-method request must not trigger unsafe action. <br>_(origin,server [not-client])_ | A resource cannot treat a request in a manner inconsistent with the semantics of the method of the request. | Binds resources/servers, not the client; it is the guarantee underpinning client-side safe-method assumptions (automatic retry, prefetch) in §9.2.1. |
| `9110 2-3-24` | informative | The 'selected representation' supplies the data/metadata for conditional-request evaluation and for the content of 200, 206, and 304 responses to GET. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | This "selected representation" provides the data and metadata for evaluating conditional requests (Section 13) and constructing the content for 200 (OK), 206 (Partial Content), and 304 (Not Modified) responses | Client-side conditional/range logic must interpret 200/206/304 metadata as describing the one selected representation — relevant when validators (ETag/Last-Modified) are compared across responses. |
| `9110 2-3-25` | informative | An HTTP client is a program establishing a connection to a server to send one or more HTTP requests; a server accepts connections to service requests with responses. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | An HTTP "client" is a program that establishes a connection to a server for the purpose of sending one or more HTTP requests. | Definitional scope: every 'client' requirement in the spec binds this library because it initiates connections and sends requests. |
| `9110 2-3-26` | informative | Client and server denote per-connection roles; the same program can hold either role on different connections. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | The terms client and server refer only to the roles that these programs perform for a particular connection. | Role determination is per connection — a process embedding poseidon as its outbound half is bound by client requirements on those connections regardless of also being a server elsewhere. |
| `9110 2-3-27` | informative | HTTP is stateless: each request message's semantics can be understood in isolation, and the connection/message relationship has no interpretive impact. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | HTTP is defined as a stateless protocol, meaning that each request message's semantics can be understood in isolation | Every emitted request must be self-contained (full target, Host, credentials as headers); never rely on prior requests on the same pooled connection to carry semantic state — CONNECT/Upgrade may legally occur at any point. |
| `9110 2-3-28` | MUST NOT | A server must not assume two requests on the same connection come from the same user agent unless the connection is secured and specific to that agent. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | a server MUST NOT assume that two requests on the same connection are from the same user agent unless the connection is secured and specific to that agent. | Server-side rule; consequence for the client pool is that servers may not tie identity to a connection, so multiplexing different logical users over one pooled connection is protocol-legal (auth must ride in each request). |
| `9110 2-3-29` | informative | A request message consists of a method and request target, optionally with header fields, content, and trailer fields. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client sends requests to a server in the form of a "request" message with a method (Section 9) and request target (Section 7.1). | Request builder must always produce method + request-target; header fields, body content, and trailers are the only other permitted request components. |
| `9110 2-3-30` | informative | A server responds with one or more response messages per request, each carrying a status code. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | A server responds to a client's request by sending one or more "response" messages, each including a status code (Section 15). | Response reader must handle multiple response messages per request — i.e., any number of 1xx interim responses before the final one — and extract a status code from each. |
| `9110 2-3-31` | informative | 'User agent' covers any client program that initiates a request, not just browsers; user-agent requirements bind non-interactive tools. <br>_(user-agent,client [CLIENT])_ | The term "user agent" refers to any of the various client programs that initiate a request. | All user-agent-scoped requirements in the spec apply to this library (a load generator is explicitly in scope: 'command-line tools' are listed as user agents). |
| `9110 2-3-32` | informative | Where the spec requires reporting errors to the user, reporting observable only in an error console or log file is acceptable. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | it is acceptable for such reporting to only be observable in an error console or log file. | User-notification requirements (e.g., around security warnings) are satisfiable by returning errors / logging — no UI needed; ensure such conditions surface as Go errors or log entries rather than being swallowed. |
| `9110 2-3-33` | informative | Requirements that a user confirm an automated action can be met via advance configuration, run-time options, or simply avoiding the unsafe action. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | requirements that an automated action be confirmed by the user before proceeding might be met via advance configuration choices, run-time options, or simple avoidance of the unsafe action | Confirmation-type requirements (e.g., redirecting an unsafe method) can be implemented as config flags or by refusing the action by default — document the chosen policy per such requirement. |
| `9110 2-3-34` | informative | Directional terminology: messages flow upstream to downstream; 'inbound' means toward the origin server, 'outbound' toward the user agent. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | inbound means "toward the origin server", whereas outbound means "toward the user agent". | Needed to read later requirements correctly (e.g., Via, Connection handling): the client's requests travel inbound/downstream; responses it receives are outbound-directed. |
| `9110 2-3-35` | informative | A proxy is a client-chosen message-forwarding agent receiving requests for some types of absolute URI and satisfying them via HTTP translation. <br>_(proxy,client [CLIENT])_ | A "proxy" is a message-forwarding agent that is chosen by the client, usually via local configuration rules, to receive requests for some type(s) of absolute URI | Only if proxy support is added: proxy selection is client configuration, and requests to a proxy carry absolute URIs (form details in RFC 9112 §3.2.2); poseidon currently has no proxy support — mark N/A until then. |
| `9110 2-3-36` | informative | A gateway (reverse proxy) acts as an origin server on its outbound connection while translating and forwarding requests inbound. <br>_(gateway [not-client])_ | A "gateway" (a.k.a. "reverse proxy") is an intermediary that acts as an origin server for the outbound connection but translates received requests and forwards them inbound | From the client's perspective a gateway is indistinguishable from an origin server; no special client behavior. |
| `9110 2-3-37` | informative | All origin-server requirements also apply to a gateway's outbound communication. <br>_(gateway,origin [not-client])_ | All HTTP requirements applicable to an origin server also apply to the outbound communication of a gateway. | Gateway-side rule; no client action. |
| `9110 2-3-38` | informative | An HTTP-to-HTTP gateway that wants to interoperate with third-party servers needs to conform to user agent requirements on its inbound connection. <br>_(gateway,user-agent [CLIENT])_ | an HTTP-to-HTTP gateway that wishes to interoperate with third-party HTTP servers needs to conform to user agent requirements on the gateway's inbound connection. | If poseidon is embedded as the upstream-fetch half of a gateway, the full user-agent conformance checklist still applies to it — same requirements, no relaxation. |
| `9110 2-3-39` | informative | A tunnel is a blind relay between two connections, not a party to HTTP communication once active. <br>_(tunnel [not-client])_ | A "tunnel" acts as a blind relay between two connections without changing the messages. | Relevant only if CONNECT-initiated tunneling is added; after tunnel establishment the client must treat the connection as opaque bytes, not HTTP. |
| `9110 2-3-40` | MAY | Any client or server may employ a cache, but a cache cannot be used while acting as a tunnel. <br>_(client,server,cache,tunnel [CLIENT])_ | Any client or server MAY employ a cache, though a cache cannot be used while acting as a tunnel. | Client-side caching is optional (poseidon has none — conformant); if ever added it must follow RFC 9111 and must be bypassed entirely on tunneled (CONNECT) connections. |
| `9110 2-3-41` | informative | A response is 'cacheable' if a cache is allowed to store a copy for answering subsequent requests; cache behavior is governed by the Caching spec. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | A response is "cacheable" if a cache is allowed to store a copy of the response message for use in answering subsequent requests. | Applies only if a client cache is implemented; then storage/reuse rules come from RFC 9111 [CACHING], out of scope for the current cacheless client. |

### RFC 9110 4 - Identifiers in HTTP (RFC 9110): URI references, http/https schemes, normalization, userinfo deprecation, authoritative access, certificate verification

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|--------------|---------------------|
| `9110 4-1` | informative | HTTP adopts the RFC 3986 ABNF productions URI-reference, absolute-URI, relative-part, authority, port, host, path-abempty, segment, and query for all URI parsing. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The definitions of "URI-reference", "absolute-URI", "relative-part", "authority", "port", "host", "path-abempty", "segment", and "query" are adopted from the URI generic syntax. | URI parser must implement RFC 3986 grammar for these components; do not invent a looser or stricter syntax. |
| `9110 4-2` | informative | Grammar: absolute-path is one or more "/"-prefixed segments, i.e. a non-empty path that may not be empty (unlike path-abempty). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | absolute-path = 1*( "/" segment ) | Where a protocol element requires absolute-path (e.g. origin-form request-target), emit at least "/" and reject empty paths. |
| `9110 4-3` | informative | Grammar: partial-URI is a relative-part with optional query and no fragment component. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | partial-URI = relative-part [ "?" query ] | Elements typed partial-URI must never carry a "#fragment"; strip/reject fragments when emitting such elements. |
| `9110 4-4` | informative | Unless a protocol element says otherwise, URI references in it are resolved relative to the target URI of the request. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Unless otherwise indicated, URI references are parsed relative to the target URI (Section 7.1). | Resolve relative references (e.g. Location) against the request's target URI per RFC 3986 reference resolution. |
| `9110 4-5` | RECOMMENDED | All senders and recipients should support URIs of at least 8000 octets in protocol elements. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | It is RECOMMENDED that all senders and recipients support, at a minimum, URIs with lengths of 8000 octets in protocol elements. | Do not cap target URIs, request-lines, or received URI-valued fields (Location etc.) below 8000 octets. |
| `9110 4-6` | informative | Grammar: an http URI is "http" "://" authority path-abempty with optional query. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | http-URI = "http" "://" authority path-abempty [ "?" query ] | Parse/emit http URLs against this production; authority is mandatory, path may be empty, fragment is not part of the scheme. |
| `9110 4-7` | informative | For http URIs, an empty or absent port subcomponent defaults to TCP port 80. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | If the port subcomponent is empty or not given, TCP port 80 (the reserved port for WWW services) is the default. | Dial port 80 when the http URI has no port or an empty port ("http://host:/"); use 80 in origin/pool keys. |
| `9110 4-8` | MUST NOT | A sender must never generate an http URI whose host identifier is empty. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A sender MUST NOT generate an "http" URI with an empty host identifier. | Validate before emitting: refuse to build a request (or absolute-form target) from an http URI with empty host. |
| `9110 4-9` | MUST | A recipient processing an http URI reference with an empty host must reject it as invalid. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A recipient that processes such a URI reference MUST reject it as invalid. | When parsing URIs from responses (e.g. Location redirects) or config, reject "http://" with empty host with an error. |
| `9110 4-10` | informative | Grammar: an https URI is "https" "://" authority path-abempty with optional query. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | https-URI = "https" "://" authority path-abempty [ "?" query ] | Parse/emit https URLs against this production, identical structure to http-URI. |
| `9110 4-11` | informative | For https URIs, an empty or absent port subcomponent defaults to TCP port 443. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | If the port subcomponent is empty or not given, TCP port 443 (the reserved port for HTTP over TLS) is the default. | Dial port 443 when the https URI has no port or an empty port; use 443 in origin/pool keys. |
| `9110 4-12` | MUST NOT | A sender must never generate an https URI whose host identifier is empty. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A sender MUST NOT generate an "https" URI with an empty host identifier. | Refuse to build a request from an https URI with empty host. |
| `9110 4-13` | MUST | A recipient processing an https URI reference with an empty host must reject it as invalid. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A recipient that processes such a URI reference MUST reject it as invalid. | Reject "https://" with empty host wherever URIs are parsed (redirect targets, user input). |
| `9110 4-14` | MUST | A client must secure its requests for https resources before sending them and must accept only secured responses to those requests. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client MUST ensure that its HTTP requests for an "https" resource are secured, prior to being communicated, and that it only accepts secured responses to those requests. | Never send an https-target request over a plaintext connection; complete TLS handshake first and read responses only from that secured connection. |
| `9110 4-15` | informative | http and https resources share no identity: they are distinct origins with separate namespaces even on the same host. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Resources made available via the "https" scheme have no shared identity with the "http" scheme. They are distinct origins with separate namespaces. | Include scheme in every connection-pool and cache key; never serve/reuse an http connection or entry for an https URI or vice versa. |
| `9110 4-16` | informative | http/https URIs are normalized and compared using RFC 3986 Section 6 methods together with the scheme default ports. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | URIs with an "http" or "https" scheme are normalized and compared according to the methods defined in Section 6 of [URI], using the defaults described above for each scheme. | Any URI equivalence logic (pool keys, redirect-loop detection) must follow RFC 3986 §6 plus the port-80/443 defaults. |
| `9110 4-17` | informative | Normal form omits the port subcomponent when it equals the scheme's default port. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | If the port is equal to the default port for a scheme, the normal form is to omit the port subcomponent. | Treat "http://h", "http://h:80", and "http://h:" as equivalent when comparing; drop default port when normalizing. |
| `9110 4-18` | informative | Except as the target of an OPTIONS request, an empty path is equivalent to "/" and the normal form is "/". <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | an empty path component is equivalent to an absolute path of "/", so the normal form is to provide a path of "/" instead. | When building origin-form request-targets from a URI with empty path, send "/" (except OPTIONS asterisk-form case); compare empty path and "/" as equal. |
| `9110 4-19` | informative | Scheme and host compare case-insensitively (lowercase normal form); all other URI components compare case-sensitively. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The scheme and host are case-insensitive and normally provided in lowercase; all other components are compared in a case-sensitive manner. | Lowercase scheme and host before comparison/pooling; never case-fold path, query, or userinfo. |
| `9110 4-20` | informative | Characters outside the reserved set are equivalent to their percent-encoded octets; normal form leaves them unencoded. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Characters other than those in the "reserved" set are equivalent to their percent-encoded octets: the normal form is to not encode them | URI comparison must decode percent-encoded unreserved characters (e.g. %7E vs ~) before comparing; do not encode unreserved characters when emitting. |
| `9110 4-21` | MAY | URIs equivalent after normalization can be assumed to identify the same resource, and any HTTP component may perform normalization. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Two HTTP URIs that are equivalent after normalization (using any method) can be assumed to identify the same resource, and any HTTP component MAY perform normalization. | The client is permitted to normalize URIs (e.g. for pool keys); normalization must not change resource identity. |
| `9110 4-22` | SHOULD NOT | Distinct resources should not be identified by URIs that are equivalent after normalization. <br>_(origin,server [not-client])_ | distinct resources SHOULD NOT be identified by HTTP URIs that are equivalent after normalization | Server-side resource design constraint; no client action beyond being allowed to normalize (4-21). |
| `9110 4-23` | informative | The "user:password" format inside the userinfo subcomponent is deprecated. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | In that subcomponent, the use of the format "user:password" is deprecated. | Do not accept or propagate user:password userinfo except possibly in local configuration; never on the wire (see 4-24). |
| `9110 4-24` | MUST NOT | A sender must not include the userinfo subcomponent or its "@" delimiter when generating an http(s) URI as a target URI or field value in a message. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A sender MUST NOT generate the userinfo subcomponent (and its "@" delimiter) when an "http" or "https" URI reference is generated within a message as a target URI or field value. | Strip userinfo before serializing any URI into a request-target (absolute-form), Host, Referer, or other field; never emit "user@host". |
| `9110 4-25` | SHOULD | Before using an http(s) URI received from an untrusted source, a recipient should parse for userinfo and treat its presence as an error (phishing defense). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | a recipient SHOULD parse for userinfo and treat its presence as an error | When following redirects or other server-supplied URIs, detect "@" userinfo in the authority and fail the request (or refuse the redirect). |
| `9110 4-26` | informative | Whether a protocol element permits a fragment is determined by its ABNF rule; the http/https scheme productions themselves exclude fragments. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | They are distinguished by use of the ABNF rule for elements where fragment is allowed; otherwise, a specific rule that excludes fragments is used. | Never send a fragment in a request-target; only accept fragments where the element's grammar is URI-reference (e.g. Location allows fragment). |
| `9110 4-27` | informative | The origin of a URI is the triple of scheme, host, and port after lowercasing scheme/host and stripping leading zeros from the port. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The "origin" for a given URI is the triple of scheme, host, and port after normalizing the scheme and host to lowercase and normalizing the port to remove any leading zeros. | Compute origin keys as (lowercased scheme, lowercased host, zero-stripped numeric port) for pooling, authority, and cache decisions. |
| `9110 4-28` | informative | When the port is elided from a URI, the origin uses the scheme's default port. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | If port is elided from the URI, the default port for that scheme is used. | Fill in 80/443 in the origin triple when the URI omits the port, so "https://h" and "https://h:443" map to one origin. |
| `9110 4-29` | informative | Two origins are distinct if they differ in scheme, host, or port. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Two origins are distinct if they differ in scheme, host, or port. | Connection reuse and authority decisions must compare full (scheme, host, port); any difference means a different origin. |
| `9110 4-30` | informative | Even when one entity controls two distinct origins, their namespaces remain distinct unless a server authoritative for the origin explicitly aliases them. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | the two namespaces under those origins are distinct unless explicitly aliased by a server authoritative for that origin. | Never infer cross-origin equivalence from shared ownership/IP; only explicit mechanisms (redirects, ORIGIN frame) may alias. |
| `9110 4-31` | informative | If the http URI host is an IP address, the origin server is the listener on the indicated TCP port at that address (no name resolution). <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | If the host identifier is provided as an IP address, the origin server is the listener (if any) on the indicated TCP port at that IP address. | Skip DNS for IP-literal hosts; connect directly to the literal address and port. |
| `9110 4-32` | informative | If the http URI host is a registered name, it is an indirect identifier to be resolved via a name resolution service such as DNS. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | the registered name is an indirect identifier for use with a name resolution service, such as DNS, to find an address for an appropriate origin server. | Resolve registered names via the configured resolver before connecting; the name, not the resolved IP, remains the identity. |
| `9110 4-33` | MAY | For an http URI, a client may attempt access by resolving the host, opening TCP to the indicated port, and sending a request whose target matches the URI. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | a client MAY attempt access by resolving the host identifier to an IP address, establishing a TCP connection to that address on the indicated port | Standard http dial path: resolve, connect to (addr, port), send request-target matching the client's target URI. |
| `9110 4-34` | informative | A non-interim (final, non-1xx) response to such an http request is considered the authoritative answer to the client's request. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | If the server responds to such a request with a non-interim HTTP response message, as described in Section 15, then that response is considered an authoritative answer to the client's request. | Treat the first final (non-1xx) response on the connection as authoritative; interim 1xx responses are not the answer. |
| `9110 4-35` | informative | The https scheme associates authority with a server able to use the private key of a certificate the client trusts for the identified origin. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | associates authority based on the ability of a server to use the private key corresponding to a certificate that the client considers to be trustworthy | Authority for https rests entirely on TLS certificate verification (4-42..4-45), not on IP, DNS answer, or port. |
| `9110 4-36` | informative | In HTTP/1.1 and earlier a client attributes authority only over a secured connection established specifically to that URI origin's host. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | In HTTP/1.1 and earlier, a client will only attribute authority to a server when they are communicating over a successfully established and secured connection specifically to that URI origin's host. | No cross-host connection coalescing for HTTP/1.1: an h1 TLS connection serves only the exact host it was established to. |
| `9110 4-37` | informative | In HTTP/2 and HTTP/3 a client may attribute authority to an existing secured connection if the URI origin's host is in the server's certificate and the client believes it could open a connection to that host. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | the URI origin's host matches any of the hosts present in the server's certificate and the client believes that it could open a connection to that host for that URI | H2/H3 coalescing is allowed only with cert SAN coverage plus reachability belief (in practice a DNS check for the same server IP), or a received ORIGIN frame. |
| `9110 4-38` | informative | The request target's host and port are passed within each HTTP request, identifying the origin and distinguishing it from other namespaces on the same server. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | The request target's host and port value are passed within each HTTP request, identifying the origin and distinguishing it from other namespaces that might be controlled by the same server | Every request must convey the target host:port (Host header in h1, :authority in h2/h3) matching the target URI's origin. |
| `9110 4-39` | informative | The origin is responsible for ensuring services sharing its certificate's private key manage the corresponding https namespaces or reject misdirected requests. <br>_(origin,server [not-client])_ | It is the origin's responsibility to ensure that any services provided with control over its certificate's private key are equally responsible for managing the corresponding "https" namespaces | Server-side obligation; no client code path. |
| `9110 4-40` | MAY | For an https URI, a client may attempt access by resolving the host, opening TCP, securing the connection end-to-end with TLS, and sending a request whose target matches the URI. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | a client MAY attempt access by resolving the host identifier to an IP address, establishing a TCP connection to that address on the indicated port, securing the connection end-to-end | Standard https dial path: resolve, connect, complete TLS with confidentiality and integrity, then send the request. |
| `9110 4-41` | informative | A non-interim response to such an https request is considered the authoritative answer to the client's request. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | If the server responds to such a request with a non-interim HTTP response message, as described in Section 15, then that response is considered an authoritative answer to the client's request. | Same as 4-34, over the secured connection: the final non-1xx response is the authoritative answer. |
| `9110 4-42` | MUST | To establish a secured connection to dereference a URI, the client must verify the service's identity is an acceptable match for the URI's origin server. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | a client MUST verify that the service's identity is an acceptable match for the URI's origin server. | TLS handshake must include hostname/identity verification against the target URI's host before any request is sent. |
| `9110 4-43` | MUST | In general the client must verify service identity using the RFC 6125 Section 6 verification process. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | a client MUST verify the service identity using the verification process defined in Section 6 of [RFC6125]. | Use RFC 6125-conformant certificate name matching (in Go, crypto/tls default VerifyHostname behavior satisfies this). |
| `9110 4-44` | MUST | The client must construct the reference identity from the URI host: IP-ID for a literal IP address, DNS-ID for a name. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | The client MUST construct a reference identity from the service's host: if the host is a literal IP address (Section 4.3.5), the reference identity is an IP-ID | Branch on host form: IP literal verifies against SAN iPAddress (IP-ID); registered name verifies against SAN DNS names (DNS-ID). |
| `9110 4-45` | MUST NOT | Clients must not use a CN-ID (certificate Common Name) reference identity. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A reference identity of type CN-ID MUST NOT be used by clients. | Never fall back to the certificate Subject CN for hostname matching; SAN-only verification (Go crypto/tls already ignores CN). |
| `9110 4-46` | informative | A client might be specially configured to accept an alternative form of server identity verification (e.g. a pinned certificate). <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client might be specially configured to accept an alternative form of server identity verification. | Alternative verification (pinning, external reference identity) is permitted only as explicit opt-in configuration, never default. |
| `9110 4-47` | informative | Ignoring server identity may be appropriate in special cases but leaves the connection open to active attack. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | it might be appropriate for a client to simply ignore the server's identity, but it must be understood that this leaves a connection open to active attack. | Any InsecureSkipVerify-style knob must be explicit opt-in and documented as vulnerable to active attackers. |
| `9110 4-48` | MUST | If the certificate is not valid for the target URI's origin, a user agent must either obtain user confirmation before proceeding or terminate with a bad certificate error. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | a user agent MUST either obtain confirmation from the user before proceeding (see Section 3.5) or terminate the connection with a bad certificate error. | Interactive-UA rule; for a library client the applicable branch is terminate-on-invalid-certificate (see 4-49/4-50 for automated clients). |
| `9110 4-49` | MUST | On an invalid certificate, automated clients must log the error to an appropriate audit log if one is available. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | Automated clients MUST log the error to an appropriate audit log (if available) | Surface certificate-verification failures through the client's logging/metrics/hooks path, not silently. |
| `9110 4-50` | SHOULD | On an invalid certificate, automated clients should terminate the connection with a bad certificate error. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | SHOULD terminate the connection (with a bad certificate error) | Default behavior on cert failure: abort the handshake/connection and return the error to the caller. |
| `9110 4-51` | MAY | Automated clients may provide a configuration setting that disables certificate checking. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | Automated clients MAY provide a configuration setting that disables this check | An opt-in disable-verification setting is permitted (matches the repo's intentional gosec G402 opt-in stance). |
| `9110 4-52` | MUST | If a disable setting exists, automated clients must also provide a setting that enables the certificate check. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | but MUST provide a setting which enables it | Verification must be available (and sensibly the default); a client with verification permanently off is non-conformant. |
