| `9110 4-53` | informative | A server identified by an IP address literal in an https URI's host has a reference identity of type IP-ID. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A server that is identified using an IP address literal in the "host" field of an "https" URI has a reference identity of type IP-ID. | For https://<ip-literal> targets, certificate matching uses IP-ID semantics, not DNS name matching. |
| `9110 4-54` | informative | Grammar: IPv4 hosts use the IPv4address ABNF rule; IPv6 hosts use the IP-literal production with the IPv6address option (RFC 3986 Section 3.2.2). <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | An IP version 4 address uses the "IPv4address" ABNF rule, and an IP version 6 address uses the "IP-literal" production with the "IPv6address" option | Parse IPv6 hosts only in bracketed IP-literal form; classify host as IP literal per these productions before choosing IP-ID vs DNS-ID. |
| `9110 4-55` | informative | An IP-ID reference identity consists of the decoded bytes of the IP address. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A reference identity of IP-ID contains the decoded bytes of the IP address. | Compare the binary address bytes (not the textual form) against certificate iPAddress values. |
| `9110 4-56` | informative | IPv4 IP-IDs are 4 octets and IPv6 IP-IDs are 16 octets; IP-ID is undefined for any other IP version. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | An IP version 4 address is 4 octets, and an IP version 6 address is 16 octets. Use of IP-ID is not defined for any other IP version. | Distinguish v4/v6 by decoded length (4 vs 16 bytes); reject any other length as unverifiable. |
| `9110 4-57` | informative | An IP-ID matches only if the address is byte-identical to an iPAddress value in the certificate's subjectAltName extension. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A reference identity of type IP-ID matches if the address is identical to an iPAddress value of the subjectAltName extension of the certificate. | IP-literal verification requires exact match against SAN iPAddress entries; no wildcarding, no DNS-name fallback. |

### RFC 9110 5 - Fields: names, field lines/combined value, order, limits, values, common rules (lists, tokens, whitespace, quoted-string, comments, parameters, date/time)

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|--------------|---------------------|
| `9110 5-1` | informative | A field name is syntactically a token. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | field-name = token | Validate emitted header names against the token charset; reject/never emit names with delimiters, whitespace, or non-VCHAR. |
| `9110 5-2` | informative | Field names are case-insensitive. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Field names are case-insensitive and ought to be registered within the "Hypertext Transfer Protocol (HTTP) Field Name Registry" | Header-name comparison and lookup in the client must be case-insensitive (e.g., canonicalize on receive). |
| `9110 5-3` | informative | Host and Connection ought to be recognized by all HTTP implementations regardless of advertised HTTP/1.1 conformance. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | the Host and Connection fields ought to be recognized by all HTTP implementations whether or not they advertise conformance with HTTP/1.1. | Client must implement Host and Connection semantics even when talking to HTTP/1.0 peers. |
| `9110 5-4` | MUST | A proxy must forward unrecognized header fields unless listed in Connection or explicitly configured to block/transform them. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | A proxy MUST forward unrecognized header fields unless the field name is listed in the Connection header field | Proxy-only; no client action. |
| `9110 5-5` | SHOULD | Non-proxy recipients should ignore unrecognized header and trailer fields. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Other recipients SHOULD ignore unrecognized header and trailer fields. | Client response parser must not error on unknown header/trailer names; skip them and expose them opaquely to the caller. |
| `9110 5-6` | informative | When a field name repeats in a section, its combined field value is the field line values concatenated in received order, separated by commas. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | its combined field value consists of the list of corresponding field line values within that section, concatenated in order, with each field line value separated by a comma | Any client API returning a single value per header must join repeated lines with commas in wire order. |
| `9110 5-7` | MAY | A recipient may merge same-name field lines into one line by appending values in order, separated by comma and optional whitespace (comma SP for consistency). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A recipient MAY combine multiple field lines within a field section that have the same field name into one field line, without changing the semantics of the message | If the client combines repeated headers, it must append in received order using ", " and must not do so for Set-Cookie (see 5-10). |
| `9110 5-8` | MUST NOT | A proxy must not reorder same-name field line values when forwarding. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | a proxy MUST NOT change the order of these field line values when forwarding a message. | Proxy-only; the client analogue is preserving order when combining (5-6/5-7). |
| `9110 5-9` | MUST NOT | A sender must not generate multiple field lines with the same name (or append to an existing one) unless the field's definition allows comma-separated list recombination. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | a sender MUST NOT generate multiple field lines with the same name in a message (whether in the headers or trailers) | When emitting requests, reject or merge duplicate singleton headers; allow duplicates only for list-based fields. |
| `9110 5-10` | informative | Set-Cookie violates the same-name rules and cannot be comma-combined; recipients ought to special-case it. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | recipients ought to handle "Set-Cookie" as a special case while processing fields. | Client must keep multiple Set-Cookie response lines as separate values, never comma-join them. |
| `9110 5-11` | informative | Order of field lines with differing names is not significant; sending control data (e.g., Host) first is good practice. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The order in which field lines with differing field names are received in a section is not significant. | Client may store headers in any structure keyed by name, but should emit Host first in requests. |
| `9110 5-12` | MUST NOT | A server must not act on a request until the entire request header section has been received. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A server MUST NOT apply a request to the target resource until it receives the entire request header section | Server-only; no client action. |
| `9110 5-13` | informative | HTTP defines no predefined limit on field line length, field value length, or total header/trailer section size; limits found in practice are ad hoc. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | HTTP does not place a predefined limit on the length of each field line, field value, or on the length of a header or trailer section as a whole | Client-side header size limits are local policy (DoS defense), not protocol; document them and pair with 5-15 handling. |
| `9110 5-14` | MUST | A server receiving request fields larger than it wishes to process must respond with an appropriate 4xx status. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A server that receives a request header field line, field value, or set of fields larger than it wishes to process MUST respond with an appropriate 4xx (Client Error) status code. | Server-only; client should merely expect 4xx (e.g., 431) when sending oversized headers. |
| `9110 5-15` | MAY | A client may discard or truncate received field lines that exceed its limits, but only if dropping them cannot change message framing or response semantics. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client MAY discard or truncate received field lines that are larger than the client wishes to process if the field semantics are such that the dropped value(s) can be safely ignored | If enforcing header limits, never drop framing-relevant fields (Content-Length, Transfer-Encoding, Connection); otherwise fail the response. |
| `9110 5-16` | informative | Grammar: field-value = *field-content; field-content is field-vchar optionally followed by a run of SP/HTAB/field-vchar ending in field-vchar; field-vchar = VCHAR / obs-text. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | field-content = field-vchar [ 1*( SP / HTAB / field-vchar ) field-vchar ] | Emit only values matching this grammar (no leading/trailing SP/HTAB, no CTLs); validate outgoing header values against it. |
| `9110 5-17` | informative | Grammar: obs-text covers the high octets %x80-FF permitted in field content. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | obs-text = %x80-FF | Parser must accept 0x80-0xFF octets inside field values without decoding them (see 5-20). |
| `9110 5-18` | MUST | A field parsing implementation must strip leading/trailing whitespace permitted by the messaging syntax before evaluating the field value. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | a field parsing implementation MUST exclude such whitespace prior to evaluating the field value. | Client header parser must trim OWS around the field line value before storing/interpreting it. |
| `9110 5-19` | SHOULD | Specifications defining new fields should restrict values to VCHAR, SP, and HTAB. <br>_(all [not-client])_ | Specifications for newly defined fields SHOULD limit their values to visible US-ASCII octets (VCHAR), SP, and HTAB. | Binds field-definition authors, not the client runtime; relevant only if the project defines custom fields. |
| `9110 5-20` | SHOULD | A recipient should treat obs-text octets in field content as opaque data. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A recipient SHOULD treat other allowed octets in field content (i.e., obs-text) as opaque data. | Do not charset-decode or reject 0x80-0xFF in received header values; pass bytes through unchanged. |
| `9110 5-21` | MUST | A recipient of CR, LF, or NUL within a field value must either reject the message or replace each such character with SP before further processing/forwarding. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | a recipient of CR, LF, or NUL within a field value MUST either reject the message or replace each of those characters with SP | Response parser must detect bare CR, LF, NUL inside field values and either fail the message or substitute SP — never pass them through (response-splitting defense). |
| `9110 5-22` | MAY | Field values containing other CTL characters are invalid, but recipients may retain them for robustness when they appear in a safe context. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | recipients MAY retain such characters for the sake of robustness when they appear within a safe context | Client may tolerate non-CR/LF/NUL CTLs inside e.g. quoted-strings it will not re-parse; must still never emit them. |
| `9110 5-23` | informative | Members that can contain commas (HTTP-date, URI-reference) ought to be delimited so data commas are distinguishable from list separators. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Fields that expect to contain a comma within a member, such as within an HTTP-date or URI-reference element, ought to be defined with delimiters around that element | List-splitting code in the client must be quote-aware: do not split on commas inside quoted-strings. |
| `9110 5-24` | informative | Grammar: the #rule ABNF extension defines comma-delimited lists; <n>#<m>element means at least n and at most m elements separated by a comma and OWS. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The full form is "<n>#<m>element" indicating at least <n> and at most <m> elements, each separated by a single comma (",") and optional whitespace | Implement one shared list parser/serializer for all #rule fields (comma + OWS separation). |
| `9110 5-25` | MUST NOT | A sender must not generate empty list elements; emitted lists must match element *( OWS "," OWS element ). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | In any production that uses the list construct, a sender MUST NOT generate empty list elements. | When serializing list-based headers, never produce ",,", leading/trailing commas, or whitespace-only members. |
| `9110 5-26` | MUST | A recipient must parse and ignore a reasonable number of empty list elements, bounded to avoid denial-of-service. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A recipient MUST parse and ignore a reasonable number of empty list elements | Client list parser must skip empty members (e.g., "foo , ,bar") without error, with a bounded tolerance limit. |
| `9110 5-27` | MUST | Grammar: recipients must accept lists matching [ element ] *( OWS "," OWS [ element ] ); empty elements do not count toward element cardinality. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | #element => [ element ] *( OWS "," OWS [ element ] ) | Implement the lenient recipient grammar for parsing and count only non-empty members when enforcing 1# minimums. |
| `9110 5-28` | informative | Grammar: token = 1*tchar, where tchar is any VCHAR except delimiters: ! # $ % & ' * + - . ^ _ ` \| ~ plus DIGIT and ALPHA. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | tchar = "!" / "#" / "$" / "%" / "&" / "'" / "*" / "+" / "-" / "." / "^" / "_" / "`" / "\|" / "~" / DIGIT / ALPHA | Provide a tchar lookup table used for validating field names, method names, and token-valued parameters. |
| `9110 5-29` | informative | Delimiters are the US-ASCII visual characters excluded from token: DQUOTE and (),/:;<=>?@[\]{}. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Delimiters are chosen from the set of US-ASCII visual characters not allowed in a token (DQUOTE and "(),/:;<=>?@[\]{}"). | Tokenizer must stop tokens at these delimiter octets; anything needing them must be quoted. |
| `9110 5-30` | SHOULD | Where optional whitespace is preferred for readability, a sender should generate OWS as a single SP. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | a sender SHOULD generate the optional whitespace as a single SP | Serializer emits exactly one SP at OWS positions where whitespace is customary (e.g., after list commas). |
| `9110 5-31` | SHOULD NOT | Otherwise a sender should not generate optional whitespace except when overwriting invalid elements during in-place filtering. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | a sender SHOULD NOT generate optional whitespace except as needed to overwrite invalid or unwanted protocol elements during in-place message filtering. | Serializer emits no gratuitous SP/HTAB at other OWS positions. |
| `9110 5-32` | SHOULD | A sender should generate required whitespace (RWS) as a single SP. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A sender SHOULD generate RWS as a single SP. | Where the grammar requires whitespace between tokens, emit exactly one SP. |
| `9110 5-33` | MAY | OWS and RWS have the semantics of a single SP; content known to be OWS/RWS may be replaced with a single SP before interpreting or forwarding. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Any content known to be defined as OWS or RWS MAY be replaced with a single SP before interpreting it or forwarding the message downstream. | Parser may normalize runs of SP/HTAB at OWS/RWS positions to one SP without changing meaning. |
| `9110 5-34` | MUST NOT | A sender must not generate bad whitespace (BWS) in messages. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A sender MUST NOT generate BWS in messages. | Serializer must never emit whitespace at BWS positions (e.g., around "=" in transfer/auth params where grammar shows BWS). |
| `9110 5-35` | MUST | A recipient must parse for bad whitespace and remove it before interpreting the protocol element. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A recipient MUST parse for such bad whitespace and remove it before interpreting the protocol element. | Parser must tolerate and strip whitespace at BWS positions in received values before evaluation. |
| `9110 5-36` | MAY | BWS has no semantics; content known to be BWS may be removed before interpreting or forwarding. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Any content known to be defined as BWS MAY be removed before interpreting it or forwarding the message downstream. | Client may normalize BWS away when re-serializing values it forwards to application code. |
| `9110 5-37` | informative | Grammar: OWS = *( SP / HTAB ); RWS = 1*( SP / HTAB ); BWS = OWS. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | OWS = *( SP / HTAB ) | Linear whitespace recognized inside field values is exactly SP and HTAB — no other characters. |
| `9110 5-38` | informative | Grammar: quoted-string = DQUOTE *( qdtext / quoted-pair ) DQUOTE, where qdtext = HTAB / SP / %x21 / %x23-5B / %x5D-7E / obs-text. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | quoted-string = DQUOTE *( qdtext / quoted-pair ) DQUOTE | Implement a shared quoted-string parser accepting qdtext ranges (excludes DQUOTE and backslash) plus escapes. |
| `9110 5-39` | MUST | Recipients processing a quoted-string value must treat each quoted-pair as if replaced by the octet following the backslash. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Recipients that process the value of a quoted-string MUST handle a quoted-pair as if it were replaced by the octet following the backslash. | Quoted-string decoder must unescape \X to X for every quoted-pair before exposing the value. |
| `9110 5-40` | informative | Grammar: quoted-pair = "\" followed by HTAB, SP, VCHAR, or obs-text. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | quoted-pair = "\" ( HTAB / SP / VCHAR / obs-text ) | Reject a backslash followed by any octet outside HTAB/SP/VCHAR/obs-text (i.e., CTLs) inside quoted-strings. |
| `9110 5-41` | SHOULD NOT | A sender should not generate quoted-pairs in a quoted-string except to escape DQUOTE and backslash. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A sender SHOULD NOT generate a quoted-pair in a quoted-string except where necessary to quote DQUOTE and backslash octets occurring within that string. | Quoted-string encoder escapes only " and \; all other characters are emitted literally. |
| `9110 5-42` | SHOULD NOT | A sender should not generate quoted-pairs in a comment except to escape parentheses and backslash. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A sender SHOULD NOT generate a quoted-pair in a comment except where necessary to quote parentheses ["(" and ")"] and backslash octets occurring within that comment. | If the client emits comments (e.g., in User-Agent), escape only ( ) and \ inside them. |
| `9110 5-43` | informative | Comments are permitted only in fields whose value definition includes "comment". <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Comments are only allowed in fields containing "comment" as part of their field value definition. | Do not emit parenthesized comments in arbitrary headers; parse comments only for fields defined with them (User-Agent, Server, Via). |
| `9110 5-44` | informative | Grammar: comment = "(" *( ctext / quoted-pair / comment ) ")" — comments nest; ctext = HTAB / SP / %x21-27 / %x2A-5B / %x5D-7E / obs-text. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | comment = "(" *( ctext / quoted-pair / comment ) ")" | Comment parser must handle nested parentheses recursively (with a depth bound) and quoted-pairs inside. |
| `9110 5-45` | informative | Grammar: parameters = *( OWS ";" OWS [ parameter ] ); parameter = parameter-name "=" parameter-value; parameter-name = token; parameter-value = token / quoted-string. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | parameters = *( OWS ";" OWS [ parameter ] ) | Shared parameter parser: split on ";" with OWS, tolerate empty parameters ([ parameter ] is optional), require token "=" (token/quoted-string). |
| `9110 5-46` | informative | Parameter names are case-insensitive; value case-sensitivity depends on the parameter's semantics. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Parameter names are case-insensitive. | Compare parameter names case-insensitively; preserve value case and let per-parameter logic decide sensitivity. |
| `9110 5-47` | informative | A parameter value matching token may be sent as a token or a quoted-string, and the two forms are equivalent. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A parameter value that matches the token production can be transmitted either as a token or within a quoted-string. | Client must treat charset=utf-8 and charset="utf-8" identically after quoted-string decoding. |
| `9110 5-48` | informative | Parameters allow no whitespace, not even BWS, around the "=" character. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Parameters do not allow whitespace (not even "bad" whitespace) around the "=" character. | Never emit spaces around "=" in parameters; strict parsing may reject "name = value" in parameter position. |
| `9110 5-49` | informative | Grammar: HTTP-date = IMF-fixdate / obs-date; three timestamp formats exist (IMF-fixdate preferred, RFC 850 and asctime obsolete). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | HTTP-date = IMF-fixdate / obs-date | Date parser must implement all three grammars behind one HTTP-date entry point. |
| `9110 5-50` | MUST | A recipient parsing a timestamp in an HTTP field must accept all three HTTP-date formats. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A recipient that parses a timestamp value in an HTTP field MUST accept all three HTTP-date formats. | Client must parse IMF-fixdate, rfc850-date, and asctime-date in Date/Last-Modified/Expires/Retry-After etc. |
| `9110 5-51` | MUST | A sender generating HTTP-date timestamps must emit them in IMF-fixdate format. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | When a sender generates a field that contains one or more timestamps defined as HTTP-date, the sender MUST generate those timestamps in the IMF-fixdate format. | All dates the client emits (e.g., If-Modified-Since) must be serialized as e.g. "Sun, 06 Nov 1994 08:49:37 GMT". |
| `9110 5-52` | informative | HTTP-date values represent UTC; GMT denotes UTC in the first two formats, and asctime-format values are assumed to be UTC. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | An HTTP-date value represents time as an instance of Coordinated Universal Time (UTC). | Parse all three formats to UTC; treat asctime values (which carry no zone) as UTC. |
| `9110 5-53` | informative | Grammar: IMF-fixdate = day-name "," SP date1 SP time-of-day SP GMT, with fixed-length 2DIGIT day, %s-case-sensitive month/day names, 4DIGIT year, and time 00:00:00-23:59:60 (leap second). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | IMF-fixdate = day-name "," SP date1 SP time-of-day SP GMT | Emit fixed-width fields with exact capitalization (Mon/Jan/GMT); accept second value 60 for leap seconds. |
| `9110 5-54` | informative | Grammar: obs-date = rfc850-date / asctime-date; rfc850-date = day-name-l "," SP date2 SP time-of-day SP GMT with date2 = day "-" month "-" 2DIGIT (two-digit year). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | rfc850-date = day-name-l "," SP date2 SP time-of-day SP GMT | Parse long day names (Monday..Sunday) and dd-Mon-yy dates; apply the two-digit-year rule (5-57). |
| `9110 5-55` | informative | Grammar: asctime-date = day-name SP date3 SP time-of-day SP year, where date3 = month SP ( 2DIGIT / ( SP 1DIGIT )) — single-digit days are SP-padded. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | date3 = month SP ( 2DIGIT / ( SP 1DIGIT )) | asctime parser must accept a space-padded day ("Jun 2") and the trailing 4-digit year with no zone. |
| `9110 5-56` | informative | HTTP-date is case-sensitive (day names, month names, and GMT use %s-tagged literals). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | HTTP-date is case sensitive. | Strict client parsing matches exact case ("Sun", "Nov", "GMT"); emit exact case always. (Only caches may relax per RFC 9111 §4.2.) |
| `9110 5-57` | MUST NOT | A sender must not generate whitespace in an HTTP-date beyond the SP octets the grammar specifies. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A sender MUST NOT generate additional whitespace in an HTTP-date beyond that specifically included as SP in the grammar. | Date serializer emits exactly the grammar's single-SP separators, nothing more. |
| `9110 5-58` | MUST | Recipients of rfc850-date two-digit years must interpret a timestamp appearing more than 50 years in the future as the most recent past year with the same last two digits. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | MUST interpret a timestamp that appears to be more than 50 years in the future as representing the most recent year in the past that had the same last two digits | rfc850 parser resolves yy by the 50-year sliding window relative to current time, never a fixed 19xx/20xx pivot. |
| `9110 5-59` | informative | Recipients are encouraged to parse timestamps robustly unless the field definition restricts the format. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Recipients of timestamp values are encouraged to be robust in parsing timestamps unless otherwise restricted by the field definition. | Client date parser may tolerate near-miss formats (e.g., other RFC 5322 variants) rather than hard-failing, where the field allows. |

### RFC 9110 6 - Message Abstraction

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|--------------|---------------------|
| `9110 6-1` | informative | An HTTP message is abstractly composed of control data, a headers lookup table, a potentially unbounded content stream, and a trailers lookup table. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A "message" consists of the following: | Model responses as control data + headers + content + trailers; keep trailers a distinct structure from headers. |
| `9110 6-2` | informative | Message parts are ordered: framing and control data first, then the header section, then content (if any), then an optional trailer section. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Framing and control data is sent first, followed by a header section containing fields for the headers table. | Emit requests as request-line, headers, body, then trailers; parse responses in the same fixed order. |
| `9110 6-3` | MUST | A client must retain knowledge of the request while parsing, interpreting, or caching the corresponding response (e.g., HEAD responses cannot be parsed like GET). <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | a client MUST retain knowledge of the request when parsing, interpreting, or caching a corresponding response | Associate each in-flight response with its request method/target so HEAD/CONNECT/status-dependent body rules are applied correctly. |
| `9110 6-4` | informative | Implicit framing (response ended by connection close) remains permitted in HTTP/1.1 for backwards compatibility. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | For backwards compatibility, this implicit framing is also allowed in HTTP/1.1. | Response reader must support read-until-EOF body framing when neither Content-Length nor chunked applies. |
| `9110 6-5` | informative | A message is complete only when all octets indicated by its framing have been received. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A message is considered "complete" when all of the octets indicated by its framing are available. | Mark a response complete only after consuming exactly the framing-indicated octets; otherwise report it truncated. |
| `9110 6-6` | informative | A close-delimited response is considered complete when the connection closes, unless a transport-level error indicates otherwise. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | a response message that is ended by the underlying connection's close is considered complete | Treat clean EOF on a close-delimited response as complete; treat transport errors (e.g., RST, TLS truncation) as incomplete. |
| `9110 6-7` | informative | Request control data comprises the request method, request target, and protocol version. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Request message control data includes a request method (Section 9), request target (Section 7.1), and protocol version (Section 2.5). | Request writer must emit method, target, and version as the control data of every request. |
| `9110 6-8` | informative | Response control data comprises a status code, an optional reason phrase, and the protocol version. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Response message control data includes a status code (Section 15), optional reason phrase, and protocol version. | Status-line parser must accept an absent/empty reason phrase and must not attach semantics to it. |
| `9110 6-9` | informative | In HTTP/1.1 and earlier, control data is carried as the first line of the message. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | In HTTP/1.1 ([HTTP/1.1]) and earlier, control data is sent as the first line of a message. | Emit the request-line first; parse the status-line as the first line of every response. |
| `9110 6-10` | SHOULD | A client should send the highest request version it conforms to, with a major version no higher than the server is known to support. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client SHOULD send a request version equal to the highest version to which the client is conformant | Send HTTP/1.1 on the H1 transport (highest conformant version, major <= known server support). |
| `9110 6-11` | MUST NOT | A client must never send a protocol version it does not conform to. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client MUST NOT send a version to which it is not conformant. | Never emit a version string (e.g., HTTP/2.0 on the wire, HTTP/1.2) the client does not fully implement. |
| `9110 6-12` | MAY | A client may downgrade the request version for a broken server, but only after at least one normal request showed the server mishandles higher versions. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client MAY send a lower request version if it is known that the server incorrectly implements the HTTP specification | Any version-downgrade logic must be gated on evidence from a prior response (status code or Server header), never preemptive. |
| `9110 6-13` | SHOULD | A server should respond with the highest version it conforms to whose major version does not exceed the request's. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A server SHOULD send a response version equal to the highest version to which the server is conformant | Server-side; no client action, but do not reject a valid lower-or-equal response version. |
| `9110 6-14` | MUST NOT | A server must not send a version it does not conform to. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A server MUST NOT send a version to which it is not conformant. | Server-side; no client implementation obligation. |
| `9110 6-15` | informative | A server can refuse service of the client's major protocol version by responding 505 (HTTP Version Not Supported). <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | A server can send a 505 (HTTP Version Not Supported) response if it wishes, for any reason, to refuse service of the client's major protocol version. | Treat a 505 response as the server refusing the major version; surface it and do not blindly retry with the same version. |
| `9110 6-16` | SHOULD | A recipient of a message with a supported major version but higher minor version should process it as the highest minor version it conforms to within that major version. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | SHOULD process the message as if it were in the highest minor version within that major version to which the recipient is conformant | Accept e.g. an HTTP/1.2 status line and process the response as HTTP/1.1; do not error on a higher minor version. |
| `9110 6-17` | informative | The header section is a sequence of header field lines; header fields are fields sent or received before the content. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The "header section" of a message consists of a sequence of header field lines. | Parse headers as an ordered sequence of field lines feeding the headers table; preserve order for same-name combination. |
| `9110 6-18` | informative | Content is the octet stream after transfer coding is decoded; it excludes chunk lengths, chunked framing syntax, and trailer fields. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | includes only the data stream after the transfer coding has been decoded; it does not include the chunk lengths, chunked framing syntax, nor the trailer fields | Deliver body to callers with chunked framing fully decoded; never surface chunk-size lines, chunk extensions, or trailer bytes as content. |
| `9110 6-19` | informative | Responses to HEAD never include content; response header fields describe what a GET response would have been. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Responses to the HEAD request method (Section 9.3.2) never include content | For HEAD requests, read no response body regardless of Content-Length or Transfer-Encoding headers. |
| `9110 6-20` | informative | A 2xx response to CONNECT switches the connection to tunnel mode instead of carrying content. <br>_(client,proxy,tunnel [CLIENT])_ | 2xx (Successful) responses to a CONNECT request method (Section 9.3.6) switch the connection to tunnel mode instead of having content. | On 2xx to CONNECT, stop HTTP message framing immediately and hand the raw connection over as a tunnel. |
| `9110 6-21` | informative | 1xx, 204, and 304 responses never include content. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | All 1xx (Informational), 204 (No Content), and 304 (Not Modified) responses do not include content. | Body-length algorithm: read zero body octets for 1xx/204/304, ignoring any Content-Length/Transfer-Encoding present. |
| `9110 6-22` | informative | All responses other than the listed exceptions include content, possibly of zero length. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | All other responses do include content, although that content might be of zero length. | For all other statuses, expect a (possibly empty) body per the framing; zero-length content is valid. |
| `9110 6-23` | informative | Content-Location in a request is a sender assertion that the content represents the identified resource; the assertion cannot be trusted without external verification. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | If the request has a Content-Location header field, then the sender asserts that the content is a representation of the resource identified by the Content-Location field value. | If the client emits Content-Location on uploads, treat it as an unverified assertion only; attach no framing semantics. |
| `9110 6-24` | informative | To identify what response content represents, the listed rules are applied in order until one matches. <br>_(client,user-agent,cache [CLIENT])_ | For a response message, the following rules are applied in order until a match is found: | Any content-identification logic (caching, bookmarking) must evaluate rules 1-7 strictly in order, first match wins. |
| `9110 6-25` | informative | Identification rule 1: HEAD requests and 204/304 responses have no content in the response. <br>_(client,user-agent,cache [CLIENT])_ | If the request method is HEAD or the response status code is 204 (No Content) or 304 (Not Modified), there is no content in the response. | Short-circuit content identification for HEAD/204/304: there is nothing to identify. |
| `9110 6-26` | informative | Identification rule 2: content of a 200 response to GET is a representation of the target resource. <br>_(client,user-agent,cache [CLIENT])_ | If the request method is GET and the response status code is 200 (OK), the content is a representation of the target resource | Attribute GET+200 content to the target URI (relevant for cache keys and representation tracking). |
| `9110 6-27` | informative | Identification rule 3: content of a 203 response to GET is a potentially modified or enhanced representation of the target resource provided by an intermediary. <br>_(client,user-agent,cache [CLIENT])_ | If the request method is GET and the response status code is 203 (Non-Authoritative Information), the content is a potentially modified or enhanced representation of the target resource | Treat GET+203 content as a transformed representation of the target resource, not authoritative origin data. |
| `9110 6-28` | informative | Identification rule 4: content of a 206 response to GET is one or more parts of a representation of the target resource. <br>_(client,user-agent,cache [CLIENT])_ | If the request method is GET and the response status code is 206 (Partial Content), the content is one or more parts of a representation of the target resource. | Treat GET+206 content as partial-representation parts, never as the full representation. |
| `9110 6-29` | informative | Identification rule 5: if the response's Content-Location references the same URI as the target URI, the content is a representation of the target resource. <br>_(client,user-agent,cache [CLIENT])_ | If the response has a Content-Location header field and its field value is a reference to the same URI as the target URI, the content is a representation of the target resource. | Compare response Content-Location against the target URI; on match, identify content as the target resource's representation. |
| `9110 6-30` | informative | Identification rule 6: a response Content-Location differing from the target URI is only an unverifiable sender assertion about which resource the content represents. <br>_(client,user-agent,cache [CLIENT])_ | the sender asserts that the content is a representation of the resource identified by the Content-Location field value | Do not trust a differing Content-Location without external verification; never use it as an authoritative cache key. |
| `9110 6-31` | informative | Identification rule 7: otherwise the content is unidentified by HTTP. <br>_(client,user-agent,cache [CLIENT])_ | Otherwise, the content is unidentified by HTTP, but a more specific identifier might be supplied within the content itself. | When no rule matches, record the content as unidentified rather than defaulting to the target URI. |
| `9110 6-32` | informative | Trailer fields ought to be processed and stored separately from header fields to avoid contradicting semantics established when the header section completed. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Trailer fields ought to be processed and stored separately from the fields in the header section to avoid contradicting message semantics known at the time the header section was complete. | Store received trailers in a structure separate from the response header map; never let trailers retroactively alter header-driven decisions. |
| `9110 6-33` | MUST NOT | A sender must not generate a trailer field unless it knows the field's definition permits it to be sent in trailers. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A sender MUST NOT generate a trailer field unless the sender knows the corresponding header field name's definition permits the field to be sent in trailers. | When the client sends chunked-request trailers, allow only field names whose definitions are known to permit trailer use (never framing/routing/auth fields). |
| `9110 6-34` | MUST NOT | A recipient must not merge a trailer field into the header section unless it understands the field definition and that definition explicitly permits and defines safe merging. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A recipient MUST NOT merge a trailer field into a header section unless the recipient understands the corresponding header field definition | Never fold received trailers into the response header map by default; merging requires an explicit per-field allowlist. |
| `9110 6-35` | informative | Sending "trailers" in the TE request header declares that the client (and any downstream clients) will accept, i.e. not drop, trailer sections. <br>_(client,user-agent [CLIENT])_ | The presence of the keyword "trailers" in the TE header field (Section 10.1.4) of a request indicates that the client is willing to accept trailer fields | Emit "TE: trailers" only if the client actually preserves and surfaces trailer sections instead of discarding them. |
| `9110 6-36` | SHOULD NOT | A server should not generate trailer fields it believes are necessary for the user agent to receive, because trailers may be discarded in transit. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | a server SHOULD NOT generate trailer fields that it believes are necessary for the user agent to receive. | Server-side; client corollary is to treat trailers as optional metadata that may be missing. |
| `9110 6-37` | informative | Same-named trailer field lines are processed in order received, with semantics equivalent to appending the values as list members. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | multiple trailer field lines with the same name have the equivalent semantics as appending the multiple values as a list of members | Preserve trailer line order and combine repeated names by list append, same as headers. |
| `9110 6-38` | MUST | A trailer field that may be generated more than once during a message must be defined as a list-based field. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Trailer fields that might be generated more than once during a message MUST be defined as a list-based field | Only emit a repeated trailer field name if that field is list-based; expect repeats only for list-based fields when receiving. |
| `9110 6-39` | MAY | At end of message, a recipient may treat the received trailer fields as a name/value data structure separate from the header fields. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | a recipient MAY treat the set of received trailer fields as a data structure of name/value pairs, similar to (but separate from) the header fields | Expose trailers to callers as a distinct name/value set available once the message completes. |
| `9110 6-40` | informative | Grammar: the Date header field value is a single HTTP-date. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Date = HTTP-date | Parse/emit Date strictly as one HTTP-date (Section 5.6.7 formats); no list syntax. |
| `9110 6-41` | SHOULD | A sender generating Date should set it to the best available approximation of the message generation time. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A sender that generates a Date header field SHOULD generate its field value as the best available approximation of the date and time of message generation. | If the client emits Date on requests, sample the clock at message origination time. |
| `9110 6-42` | MUST | An origin server with a clock must generate a Date header field in all 2xx, 3xx, and 4xx responses. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | An origin server with a clock (as defined in Section 5.6.7) MUST generate a Date header field in all 2xx (Successful), 3xx (Redirection), and 4xx (Client Error) responses | Server-side; client must still tolerate responses lacking Date (see 6-45). |
| `9110 6-43` | MAY | An origin server with a clock may generate a Date header field in 1xx and 5xx responses. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | MAY generate a Date header field in 1xx (Informational) and 5xx (Server Error) responses | Server-side; client must not require Date on 1xx/5xx responses. |
| `9110 6-44` | MUST NOT | An origin server without a clock must not generate a Date header field. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | An origin server without a clock MUST NOT generate a Date header field. | Server-side; reinforces that a missing Date is legal and must be handled by the client. |
| `9110 6-45` | MUST | A recipient with a clock that receives a response without Date must record the receipt time and append a corresponding Date header if the message is cached or forwarded downstream. <br>_(client,user-agent,cache,proxy [CLIENT])_ | MUST record the time it was received and append a corresponding Date header field to the message's header section if it is cached or forwarded downstream | If the client ever caches or forwards responses, stamp a receipt-time Date onto Date-less responses; otherwise record receipt time for age calculations. |
| `9110 6-46` | MAY | A recipient with a clock may replace an invalid Date header field value with the time the response was received. <br>_(client,user-agent,cache,proxy [CLIENT])_ | A recipient with a clock that receives a response with an invalid Date header field value MAY replace that value with the time that response was received. | On unparseable Date, optionally substitute the local receipt time; never hard-fail the response for a bad Date. |
| `9110 6-47` | MAY | A user agent may send a Date header field in a request, though generally will not. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | A user agent MAY send a Date header field in a request | Do not emit Date on requests by default; permit it as an opt-in caller-supplied header. |
| `9110 6-48` | informative | Grammar: the Trailer header field value is a comma-separated list of field names. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Trailer = #field-name | Parse/emit Trailer as a #-list of field-name tokens (RFC list syntax, zero or more members). |
| `9110 6-49` | SHOULD | A sender intending to generate trailer fields should send a Trailer header field in the header section naming the fields that might appear in the trailers. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A sender that intends to generate one or more trailer fields in a message SHOULD generate a Trailer header field in the header section of that message | When the client sends chunked-request trailers, emit a Trailer header listing the anticipated field names. |
| `9110 6-50` | informative | There is no guarantee that a sender of Trailer actually sends the named fields; the trailer section may also be discarded in transit. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | there is no guarantee that a sender of Trailer will always follow through by sending the named fields | Never block or error waiting for trailer fields promised by a Trailer header; treat them as best-effort hints. |

### RFC 9110 7 - Routing HTTP Messages (RFC 9110 §7.1–7.8)

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|--------------|---------------------|
| `9110 7-1` | informative | A client sends a request message containing enough components of the parsed target URI for recipients to identify the target resource. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | the client sends a request message containing enough components of its parsed target URI to enable recipients to identify that same resource | Build request target (origin-form path+query for h1, :scheme/:authority/:path for h2/h3) from the parsed target URI. |
| `9110 7-2` | informative | The target URI excludes any fragment component; fragments are client-side only and never sent on the wire. <br>_(client,user-agent [CLIENT])_ | The target URI excludes the reference's fragment component, if any, since fragment identifiers are reserved for client-side processing | Strip '#fragment' from the URI before constructing the request target; never emit a fragment in the request line or :path. |
| `9110 7-3` | informative | For CONNECT the request target is the tunnel destination's host name and port number separated by a colon (authority-form). <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | For CONNECT (Section 9.3.6), the request target is the host name and port number of the tunnel destination, separated by a colon. | If CONNECT is supported, emit 'host:port' as the request target, not a URI. |
| `9110 7-4` | informative | For OPTIONS the request target can be a single asterisk (asterisk-form). <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | For OPTIONS (Section 9.3.7), the request target can be a single asterisk ("*"). | Support emitting '*' as request target for server-wide OPTIONS; do not URI-normalize it. |
| `9110 7-5` | MUST NOT | Authority-form and asterisk-form request targets must not be used with any method other than CONNECT and OPTIONS respectively. <br>_(client,proxy [CLIENT])_ | These forms MUST NOT be used with other methods. | Reject or refuse to serialize a request combining authority-form with non-CONNECT or '*' with non-OPTIONS. |
| `9110 7-6` | informative | Grammar: the Host field value is uri-host optionally followed by ":" port. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Host = uri-host [ ":" port ] | Emit Host as uri-host[:port] taken from the target URI; no userinfo, no path, no trailing junk. |
| `9110 7-7` | MUST | A user agent must generate a Host header field in every request unless the same information is sent as the :authority pseudo-header. <br>_(user-agent,client [CLIENT])_ | A user agent MUST generate a Host header field in a request unless it sends that information as an ":authority" pseudo-header field. | http1 path: always synthesize Host from the target URI; h2/h3 path: :authority satisfies this. Never send an HTTP/1.1 request without Host. |
| `9110 7-8` | SHOULD | A user agent that sends Host should place it as the first field in the request header section. <br>_(user-agent,client [CLIENT])_ | A user agent that sends Host SHOULD send it as the first field in the header section of a request. | In the h1 serializer, write Host immediately after the request line, before all other fields. |
| `9110 7-9` | informative | If the client has a cache that can satisfy the request, the request is normally directed to the cache first. <br>_(client,cache [CLIENT])_ | If the client has a cache [CACHING] and the request can be satisfied by it, then the request is usually directed there first. | Only relevant if a cache layer is added; routing order would be cache -> proxy -> origin. No action for the cache-less client. |
| `9110 7-10` | informative | When routing via an http/https proxy, the client establishes or reuses a connection to the proxy and sends a request whose request target matches the client's target URI. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | sending it an HTTP request message containing a request target that matches the client's target URI | If forward-proxy support is added: send absolute-form request target to the proxy that reproduces the target URI. |
| `9110 7-11` | informative | When no proxy applies, the client obtains access to an http resource by connecting (or reusing a connection) to the identified origin server and sending a request target matching the target URI. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | establishing (or reusing) an inbound connection to the identified origin server and then sending it an HTTP request message containing a request target that matches the client's target URI | Pool keying and connection reuse must be per origin (scheme,host,port); the emitted request target must be derived from that same target URI. |
| `9110 7-12` | MUST | Unless the connection is from a trusted gateway, an origin server must reject a request that fails any scheme-specific requirement of the target URI. <br>_(origin,server [not-client])_ | Unless the connection is from a trusted gateway, an origin server MUST reject a request if any scheme-specific requirements for the target URI are not met. | Server-side only; client should expect rejection (e.g. 421) when its Host/SNI diverge from the connection. |
| `9110 7-13` | MUST | A request for an https resource must be rejected unless received over a connection secured via a certificate valid for the target URI's origin. <br>_(origin,server [not-client])_ | a request for an "https" resource MUST be rejected unless it has been received over a connection that has been secured via a certificate valid for that target URI's origin | Server-side; client consequence: don't coalesce https requests onto a connection whose certificate doesn't cover the new origin. |
| `9110 7-14` | informative | The 421 (Misdirected Request) status code indicates the origin server rejected the request as misdirected. <br>_(origin,client [CLIENT])_ | The 421 (Misdirected Request) status code in a response indicates that the origin server has rejected the request because it appears to have been misdirected | Treat 421 as retryable-on-a-different-connection (fresh conn to the correct origin), not as a permanent resource error. |
| `9110 7-15` | informative | Any response, including interim responses, can be sent at any time after the request is received, even before the request is complete. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | All responses, regardless of the status code (including interim responses) can be sent at any time after a request is received, even if the request is not yet complete. | The response reader must accept a full or interim response arriving while the request body is still being written; a response may complete before the request does. |
| `9110 7-16` | informative | Clients are not expected to wait any specific time and may abandon a request if no response arrives within a reasonable period. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | Clients (including intermediaries) might abandon a request if the response is not received within a reasonable period of time. | Response timeouts/deadline cancellation are permitted; expose per-request timeout and abort cleanly. |
| `9110 7-17` | SHOULD | A client that receives a response while still sending the request should continue sending it unless it receives an explicit contrary indication. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client that receives a response while it is still sending the associated request SHOULD continue sending that request unless it receives an explicit indication to the contrary | On early response (e.g. early 4xx), keep streaming the request body unless the server signals stop (h1 close, h2 RST_STREAM/END_STREAM per §6.4 of HTTP/2). Do not unilaterally abort the upload on a non-final/early response. |
| `9110 7-18` | informative | Intermediaries are expected to forward messages even when protocol elements (new methods, status codes, field names) are not recognized. <br>_(proxy,gateway [not-client])_ | Intermediaries are expected to forward messages even when protocol elements are not recognized | Intermediary-only; client analogue is generic tolerance of unknown fields/status codes (covered elsewhere). |
| `9110 7-19` | MUST | An intermediary not acting as a tunnel must implement the Connection header field and exclude connection-only fields from forwarding. <br>_(proxy,gateway [not-client])_ | An intermediary not acting as a tunnel MUST implement the Connection header field, as specified in Section 7.6.1, and exclude fields from being forwarded that are only intended for the incoming connection. | Not applicable to a pure client; relevant only if a proxying mode is ever added. |
| `9110 7-20` | MUST NOT | An intermediary must not forward a message to itself unless protected from an infinite request loop. <br>_(proxy,gateway [not-client])_ | An intermediary MUST NOT forward a message to itself unless it is protected from an infinite request loop. | Intermediary-only. |
| `9110 7-21` | informative | Senders and recipients cannot rely on incremental delivery of partial messages, since implementations may buffer or delay forwarding. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | senders and recipients cannot rely on incremental delivery of partial messages | API must not promise chunk-boundary-preserving delivery; streaming semantics are best-effort (relevant to DoStream docs and to anything resembling server-push/long-poll usage). |
| `9110 7-22` | informative | Grammar: Connection is a comma-separated list of connection-option tokens. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Connection = #connection-option connection-option = token | Parse Connection as a #list of tokens (handles multiple members, OWS, empty elements per list rules); each option must be a valid token to emit. |
| `9110 7-23` | informative | Connection options are compared case-insensitively. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Connection options are case-insensitive. | Match 'close', 'keep-alive', 'upgrade' etc. case-insensitively when parsing received Connection field values. |
| `9110 7-24` | MUST | When any field other than Connection supplies control information for the current connection, the sender must list that field's name in the Connection header field. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | the sender MUST list the corresponding field name within the Connection header field. | If the client emits a hop-by-hop field (e.g. Upgrade, TE), it must also emit 'Connection: <field-name>' naming it. Note HTTP/2+/3 forbid the Connection field entirely — h1-only behavior. |
| `9110 7-25` | MUST | Intermediaries must parse a received Connection field before forwarding, remove every header/trailer field named by a connection-option, and remove or replace Connection itself. <br>_(proxy,gateway [not-client])_ | Intermediaries MUST parse a received Connection header field before a message is forwarded and, for each connection-option in this field, remove any header or trailer field(s) from the message with the same name as the connection-option | Intermediary-only; client consequence is 7-28 (ignore improperly forwarded connection-specific fields). |
| `9110 7-26` | SHOULD | Intermediaries should remove or replace fields known to require removal before forwarding (Proxy-Connection, Keep-Alive, TE, Transfer-Encoding, Upgrade) whether or not listed as a connection-option. <br>_(proxy,gateway [not-client])_ | intermediaries SHOULD remove or replace fields that are known to require removal before forwarding, whether or not they appear as a connection-option | Intermediary-only; the enumerated list (Proxy-Connection, Keep-Alive, TE, Transfer-Encoding, Upgrade) is the canonical hop-by-hop set a client should never expect end-to-end. |
| `9110 7-27` | MUST NOT | A sender must not send a connection option that names a field intended for all recipients of the content (e.g. Cache-Control). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A sender MUST NOT send a connection option corresponding to a field that is intended for all recipients of the content. | Validate/deny caller-supplied Connection options naming end-to-end fields (e.g. reject 'Connection: cache-control'). |
| `9110 7-28` | informative | A connection-specific field received without a corresponding connection option indicates improper forwarding and ought to be ignored by the recipient. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | a connection-specific field received without a corresponding connection option usually indicates that the field has been improperly forwarded by an intermediary and ought to be ignored by the recipient | Ignore stray connection-specific response fields (e.g. Keep-Alive without 'Connection: keep-alive') rather than acting on them. |
| `9110 7-29` | informative | Grammar: Max-Forwards is one or more DIGITs. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Max-Forwards = 1*DIGIT | If the client (loadgen) emits Max-Forwards with TRACE/OPTIONS, serialize as plain decimal digits only. |
| `9110 7-30` | informative | The Max-Forwards value is a decimal integer giving the remaining number of times the request may be forwarded. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The Max-Forwards value is a decimal integer indicating the remaining number of times this request message can be forwarded. | Treat the value as a non-negative decimal integer; semantics matter only when emitting for TRACE/OPTIONS diagnostics. |
| `9110 7-31` | MUST | Each intermediary receiving a TRACE or OPTIONS request with Max-Forwards must check and update its value before forwarding. <br>_(proxy,gateway [not-client])_ | Each intermediary that receives a TRACE or OPTIONS request containing a Max-Forwards header field MUST check and update its value prior to forwarding the request. | Intermediary-only. |
| `9110 7-32` | MUST NOT | If the received Max-Forwards value is zero, the intermediary must not forward the request. <br>_(proxy,gateway [not-client])_ | If the received value is zero (0), the intermediary MUST NOT forward the request | Intermediary-only. |
| `9110 7-33` | MUST | When Max-Forwards is zero, the intermediary must respond as the final recipient instead of forwarding. <br>_(proxy,gateway [not-client])_ | instead, the intermediary MUST respond as the final recipient. | Intermediary-only; client consequence: with Max-Forwards: 0 the response comes from the first hop. |
| `9110 7-34` | MUST | If Max-Forwards is greater than zero, the forwarding intermediary must set it to the lesser of the received value minus one and its own maximum supported value. <br>_(proxy,gateway [not-client])_ | the intermediary MUST generate an updated Max-Forwards field in the forwarded message with a field value that is the lesser of a) the received value decremented by one (1) or b) the recipient's maximum supported value | Intermediary-only. |
| `9110 7-35` | MAY | A recipient may ignore a Max-Forwards header field received with request methods other than TRACE and OPTIONS. <br>_(server,proxy [not-client])_ | A recipient MAY ignore a Max-Forwards header field received with any other request methods. | Request-recipient rule; no client action. |
| `9110 7-36` | informative | Grammar: Via is a list of received-protocol RWS received-by with optional comment; received-protocol = [ protocol-name "/" ] protocol-version; received-by = pseudonym [ ":" port ]; pseudonym = token. <br>_(all [not-client])_ | Via = #( received-protocol RWS received-by [ RWS comment ] ) | Client never emits Via; received Via is treated as an ordinary list-valued field (no special parsing required unless the caller inspects it). |
| `9110 7-37` | MUST | A proxy must send an appropriate Via header field in each message it forwards. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | A proxy MUST send an appropriate Via header field, as described below, in each message that it forwards. | Proxy-only. |
| `9110 7-38` | MUST | An HTTP-to-HTTP gateway must send an appropriate Via header field in each inbound request message. <br>_(gateway [not-client])_ | An HTTP-to-HTTP gateway MUST send an appropriate Via header field in each inbound request message | Gateway-only. |
| `9110 7-39` | MAY | An HTTP-to-HTTP gateway may send a Via header field in forwarded response messages. <br>_(gateway [not-client])_ | MAY send a Via header field in forwarded response messages | Gateway-only; client must merely tolerate Via appearing in responses. |
| `9110 7-40` | informative | In Via, the protocol-name is omitted when the received protocol is HTTP (so "1.1" means HTTP/1.1). <br>_(all [not-client])_ | For brevity, the protocol-name is omitted when the received protocol is HTTP. | Only matters if the client ever interprets Via; bare versions imply the HTTP protocol family. |
| `9110 7-41` | MAY | A sender may replace the real received-by host with a pseudonym if the host is sensitive information. <br>_(proxy,gateway [not-client])_ | if the real host is considered to be sensitive information, a sender MAY replace it with a pseudonym. | Intermediary-only. |
| `9110 7-42` | MAY | If a Via received-by has no port, a recipient may interpret it as the default port for the received-protocol. <br>_(all [not-client])_ | If a port is not provided, a recipient MAY interpret that as meaning it was received on the default port, if any, for the received-protocol. | Only if the client interprets Via; otherwise no action. |
| `9110 7-43` | MAY | A sender may generate comments in Via to identify recipient software. <br>_(proxy,gateway [not-client])_ | A sender MAY generate comments to identify the software of each recipient | Intermediary-only; client parser must tolerate comments in Via values it receives. |
| `9110 7-44` | MAY | Via comments are optional and a recipient may remove them before forwarding. <br>_(proxy,gateway [not-client])_ | comments in Via are optional, and a recipient MAY remove them prior to forwarding the message. | Intermediary-only. |
| `9110 7-45` | SHOULD NOT | A firewall-portal intermediary should not forward the names and ports of hosts within the firewall region unless explicitly enabled. <br>_(proxy,gateway [not-client])_ | An intermediary used as a portal through a network firewall SHOULD NOT forward the names and ports of hosts within the firewall region unless it is explicitly enabled to do so. | Intermediary-only. |
| `9110 7-46` | SHOULD | If not enabled to forward internal names, such an intermediary should replace each received-by host behind the firewall with an appropriate pseudonym. <br>_(proxy,gateway [not-client])_ | such an intermediary SHOULD replace each received-by host of any host behind the firewall by an appropriate pseudonym for that host. | Intermediary-only. |
| `9110 7-47` | MAY | An intermediary may combine an ordered subsequence of Via list members into one member if their received-protocol values are identical. <br>_(proxy,gateway [not-client])_ | An intermediary MAY combine an ordered subsequence of Via header field list members into a single member if the entries have identical received-protocol values. | Intermediary-only. |
| `9110 7-48` | SHOULD NOT | A sender should not combine multiple Via list members unless all are under the same organizational control and hosts are already pseudonymized. <br>_(proxy,gateway [not-client])_ | A sender SHOULD NOT combine multiple list members unless they are all under the same organizational control and the hosts have already been replaced by pseudonyms. | Intermediary-only. |
| `9110 7-49` | MUST NOT | A sender must not combine Via members that have different received-protocol values. <br>_(proxy,gateway [not-client])_ | A sender MUST NOT combine members that have different received-protocol values. | Intermediary-only. |
| `9110 7-50` | MAY | A proxy may append its own domain to a received host name that is not fully qualified when forwarding. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | it MAY add its own domain to the host name it received when forwarding the request. | Proxy-only. |
| `9110 7-51` | MUST NOT | A proxy must not change the host name if the target URI contains a fully qualified domain name. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | A proxy MUST NOT change the host name if the target URI contains a fully qualified domain name. | Proxy-only. |
| `9110 7-52` | MUST NOT | A proxy must not modify the absolute-path and query of the received target URI when forwarding, except as required by the forwarding protocol (e.g. empty path becomes "/" or "*"). <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | A proxy MUST NOT modify the "absolute-path" and "query" parts of the received target URI when forwarding it to the next inbound server except as required by that forwarding protocol. | Proxy-only; client analogue (replace empty path with '/') is a §3.2.1 HTTP/1.1 rule, applied at request-line serialization. |
| `9110 7-53` | MUST NOT | A proxy must not transform the content of a response that contains a no-transform cache directive. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | A proxy MUST NOT transform the content (Section 6.4) of a response message that contains a no-transform cache directive | Proxy-only. |
| `9110 7-54` | MAY | A proxy may transform the content of a message lacking a no-transform directive; a transforming proxy can signal it by changing 200 to 203. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | A proxy MAY transform the content of a message that does not contain a no-transform cache directive. | Proxy-only; client should treat 203 as a transformed 200-class response, not an error. |
| `9110 7-55` | SHOULD NOT | A proxy should not modify header fields describing communication endpoints, resource state, or the selected representation unless the field definition allows it or privacy/security requires it. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | A proxy SHOULD NOT modify header fields that provide information about the endpoints of the communication chain, the resource state, or the selected representation (other than the content) | Proxy-only. |
| `9110 7-56` | MAY | A client may send a list of protocol names in Upgrade, in descending preference order, to invite the server to switch protocols. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client MAY send a list of protocol names in the Upgrade header field of a request to invite the server to switch to one or more of the named protocols | If Upgrade is exposed in the API: serialize offered protocols as a list ordered most-preferred first (h1 requests only). |
| `9110 7-57` | MAY | A server may ignore a received Upgrade header field and continue with the current protocol. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | A server MAY ignore a received Upgrade header field if it wishes to continue using the current protocol on that connection. | Client must handle the non-upgrade path: a normal final response (not 101) means the connection stays HTTP/1.1. |
| `9110 7-58` | informative | Upgrade cannot be used to insist on a protocol change; it is only an invitation. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | Upgrade cannot be used to insist on a protocol change. | Never assume the switch happened; only a 101 with matching Upgrade field commits the connection to the new protocol. |
| `9110 7-59` | informative | Grammar: Upgrade is a list of protocol elements; protocol = protocol-name ["/" protocol-version]; protocol-name = token; protocol-version = token. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | protocol = protocol-name ["/" protocol-version] | Emit/parse Upgrade members as token or token"/"token (e.g. 'websocket', 'IRC/6.9'); reject non-token protocol names on emission. |
| `9110 7-60` | SHOULD | Recipients should compare each Upgrade protocol-name to supported protocols case-insensitively, despite registered preferred case. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | recipients SHOULD use case-insensitive comparison when matching each protocol-name to supported protocols. | When validating a 101's Upgrade field against the protocols the client offered, match protocol-names case-insensitively. |
| `9110 7-61` | MUST | A server sending 101 (Switching Protocols) must send an Upgrade header field naming the protocol(s) the connection is switching to. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | A server that sends a 101 (Switching Protocols) response MUST send an Upgrade header field to indicate the new protocol(s) to which the connection is being switched | On receiving 101, require an Upgrade field; a 101 without one (or naming nothing usable) is a protocol violation — fail the exchange rather than guess. |
| `9110 7-62` | MUST | If multiple protocol layers are being switched, the 101 sender must list the protocols in layer-ascending order. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | if multiple protocol layers are being switched, the sender MUST list the protocols in layer-ascending order. | Interpret a multi-member Upgrade in a 101 as a layered stack, bottom layer first. |
| `9110 7-63` | MUST NOT | A server must not switch to a protocol the client did not indicate in the corresponding request's Upgrade header field. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | A server MUST NOT switch to a protocol that was not indicated by the client in the corresponding request's Upgrade header field. | Client-side check: if the 101 names a protocol the client never offered, treat the response as invalid and close the connection. |
| `9110 7-64` | MAY | A server may ignore the client's order of preference and choose the new protocol(s) on other factors. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | A server MAY choose to ignore the order of preference indicated by the client | Client must accept any offered protocol being selected, not only its top preference. |
| `9110 7-65` | MUST | A server sending 426 (Upgrade Required) must send an Upgrade header field listing acceptable protocols in descending preference. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | A server that sends a 426 (Upgrade Required) response MUST send an Upgrade header field to indicate the acceptable protocols, in order of descending preference. | Surface 426 plus its Upgrade list to the caller; the client may retry after upgrading. |
| `9110 7-66` | MAY | A server may send an Upgrade header field in any other response to advertise upgrade support for future requests. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A server MAY send an Upgrade header field in any other response to advertise that it implements support for upgrading to the listed protocols | Purely advisory; client may ignore Upgrade on non-101/426 responses. |
| `9110 7-67` | MUST NOT | A server must not switch protocols unless the received message's semantics can be honored by the new protocol. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A server MUST NOT switch protocols unless the received message semantics can be honored by the new protocol | Server-side; after 101 the client should expect the new protocol's equivalent response to the original request without resending it. |
| `9110 7-68` | MUST | Any sender of Upgrade must also send an "Upgrade" connection option in the Connection header field. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A sender of Upgrade MUST also send an "Upgrade" connection option in the Connection header field (Section 7.6.1) to inform intermediaries not to forward this field. | When the client emits Upgrade in an h1 request, it must also emit 'Connection: upgrade' (auto-add or validate in the serializer). |
| `9110 7-69` | MUST | A server receiving an Upgrade header field in an HTTP/1.0 request must ignore it. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A server that receives an Upgrade header field in an HTTP/1.0 request MUST ignore that Upgrade field. | Server-side; client corollary: sending Upgrade in an HTTP/1.0 request is pointless — only offer it on HTTP/1.1. |
| `9110 7-70` | informative | A client cannot begin using the upgraded protocol on the connection until it has completely sent the request message. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client cannot begin using an upgraded protocol on the connection until it has completely sent the request message | After a 101, finish writing the entire request before switching the connection's read/write path to the new protocol; never mid-message. |
| `9110 7-71` | MUST | If a server receives both Upgrade and Expect: 100-continue, it must send a 100 (Continue) response before sending 101 (Switching Protocols). <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | the server MUST send a 100 (Continue) response before sending a 101 (Switching Protocols) response. | Client sending both Expect: 100-continue and Upgrade must be prepared for interim 100 followed by 101 on the same exchange. |
| `9110 7-72` | informative | Upgrade only switches protocols on top of the existing connection; it cannot change the transport protocol or move to a different connection. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The Upgrade header field only applies to switching protocols on top of the existing connection | Never treat Upgrade as a redirect: the switched protocol runs over the same TCP/TLS connection; connection pool entry must be removed from HTTP reuse after a successful 101. |

### RFC 9110 8 - Representation Data and Metadata (Content-Type, Content-Encoding, Content-Language, Content-Length, Content-Location, Validators: Last-Modified, ETag) — RFC 9110, lines 2904-3725

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|--------------|---------------------|
| `9110 8-1` | informative | Representation data is a two-layer ordered encoding: Content-Type describes the data format, and Content-Encoding wraps it; decoders must reverse Content-Encoding before interpreting Content-Type. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | representation-data := Content-Encoding( Content-Type( data ) ) | When decoding a response body, strip Content-Encoding codings (in reverse listed order) before interpreting the media type. |
| `9110 8-2` | informative | In a HEAD response, representation header fields describe the data that a GET would have returned, not any enclosed content. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | In a response to a HEAD request, the representation header fields describe the representation data that would have been enclosed in the content if the same request had been a GET. | Treat Content-Type/Length/etc. on HEAD responses as metadata about the hypothetical GET body; never expect actual content. |
| `9110 8-3` | informative | The Content-Type field value is a single media-type. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Content-Type = media-type | Parse Content-Type as exactly one media-type; expose it as a singleton field. |
| `9110 8-4` | SHOULD | A sender generating a message with content SHOULD include a Content-Type header unless the media type is unknown to it. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A sender that generates a message containing content SHOULD generate a Content-Type header field in that message unless the intended media type of the enclosed representation is unknown to the sender. | When the client sends a request body, set Content-Type whenever the caller supplied or implied a media type. |
| `9110 8-5` | MAY | A recipient of a message without Content-Type MAY assume application/octet-stream or examine the data to determine its type. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | If a Content-Type header field is not present, the recipient MAY either assume a media type of "application/octet-stream" ([RFC2046], Section 4.5.1) or examine the data to determine its type. | On responses lacking Content-Type, default to application/octet-stream (or surface absence to the caller); do not error. |
| `9110 8-6` | informative | Implementations that perform MIME sniffing are encouraged to let the user disable it. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | When sniffing is implemented, implementers are encouraged to provide a means for the user to disable it. | Prefer not sniffing at all in a low-level client; if ever added, make it opt-out. |
| `9110 8-7` | informative | Content-Type is a singleton field; multiple occurrences are an error and recipients handling it (e.g., taking the last valid member) risk interop/security divergence. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Although Content-Type is defined as a singleton field, it is sometimes incorrectly generated multiple times, resulting in a combined field value that appears to be a list. | Define a deliberate policy for duplicate Content-Type (surface raw values or take last valid member); never silently merge as a list. |
| `9110 8-8` | informative | Media type grammar: type "/" subtype followed by parameters, where type and subtype are tokens. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | media-type = type "/" subtype parameters type = token subtype = token | Media-type parser must require token "/" token and accept trailing parameters per Section 5.6.6. |
| `9110 8-9` | informative | Media type and subtype tokens are compared case-insensitively. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The type and subtype tokens are case-insensitive. | Case-fold type/subtype when comparing media types (e.g., Text/HTML == text/html). |
| `9110 8-10` | MAY | The type/subtype MAY be followed by semicolon-delimited name/value parameters. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The type/subtype MAY be followed by semicolon-delimited parameters (Section 5.6.6) in the form of name/value pairs. | Parser must accept zero or more ";name=value" parameters (token or quoted-string values) after the subtype. |
| `9110 8-11` | informative | Media type parameter values may be case-sensitive depending on the parameter definition; do not case-fold them generically. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Parameter values might or might not be case-sensitive, depending on the semantics of the parameter name. | Preserve parameter value case when parsing/serializing; only fold case for parameters known to be case-insensitive (e.g., charset). |
| `9110 8-12` | informative | Charset names (Content-Type parameter or Accept-Encoding-style token) are matched case-insensitively. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | In both cases, charset names are matched case-insensitively. | Compare charset names case-insensitively (UTF-8 == utf-8). |
| `9110 8-13` | informative | All multipart media types use the common RFC 2046 syntax and carry a boundary parameter in the media type value. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | All multipart types share a common syntax, as defined in Section 5.1.1 of [RFC2046], and include a boundary parameter as part of the media type value. | When emitting multipart request bodies (e.g., multipart/form-data), always include a boundary parameter in Content-Type. |
| `9110 8-14` | MUST | A sender of a multipart body MUST use only CRLF for line breaks between body parts. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | a sender MUST generate only CRLF to represent line breaks between body parts. | Any multipart body the client generates must use CRLF (never bare LF) between parts. |
| `9110 8-15` | informative | HTTP message framing never uses the multipart boundary to determine message body length. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | HTTP message framing does not use the multipart boundary as an indicator of message body length | Frame bodies solely by Content-Length/Transfer-Encoding/close; never terminate reading at a multipart boundary. |
| `9110 8-16` | informative | Content-Encoding is a (possibly empty-element) comma-separated list of content-coding tokens. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Content-Encoding = #content-coding | Parse Content-Encoding with list (#) rules: split on commas, trim OWS, tolerate empty elements per field-list handling. |
| `9110 8-17` | MUST | A sender that applied content codings MUST list them in Content-Encoding in application order. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | the sender that applied the encodings MUST generate a Content-Encoding header field that lists the content codings in the order in which they were applied. | If the client compresses a request body, emit Content-Encoding listing codings in the order applied; decode responses in reverse list order. |
