| `9110 8-18` | SHOULD NOT | The "identity" coding is reserved for Accept-Encoding and SHOULD NOT appear in Content-Encoding. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | the coding named "identity" is reserved for its special role in Accept-Encoding and thus SHOULD NOT be included. | Never emit Content-Encoding: identity; tolerate it on receipt as a no-op. |
| `9110 8-19` | informative | Content-Encoding codings are a characteristic of the representation itself; other representation metadata (e.g., Content-Length, ETag) refers to the coded form. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | the codings listed in Content-Encoding are a characteristic of the representation; the representation is defined in terms of the coded form | Treat Content-Length/validators as describing the encoded bytes; do not adjust them after client-side decompression. |
| `9110 8-20` | MAY | An origin server MAY reject a request whose content coding is unacceptable with 415 (Unsupported Media Type). <br>_(origin [CLIENT])_ | An origin server MAY respond with a status code of 415 (Unsupported Media Type) if a representation in the request message has a content coding that is not acceptable. | Expect and surface 415 when sending encoded request bodies; do not automatically retry with the same coding. |
| `9110 8-21` | informative | A content-coding value is a token. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | content-coding = token | Validate content-coding names against token grammar when parsing/emitting. |
| `9110 8-22` | informative | Content coding names are case-insensitive. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | All content codings are case-insensitive | Match coding names case-insensitively (GZIP == gzip) when selecting a decoder. |
| `9110 8-23` | SHOULD | A recipient SHOULD treat "x-compress" as equivalent to "compress". <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A recipient SHOULD consider "x-compress" to be equivalent to "compress". | Alias x-compress to compress in the decoder registry (if compress is supported). |
| `9110 8-24` | informative | The deflate coding is the zlib-wrapped format (RFC 1950) containing a raw deflate stream (RFC 1951). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The "deflate" coding is a "zlib" data format [RFC1950] containing a "deflate" compressed data stream [RFC1951] | Decode Content-Encoding: deflate as zlib-wrapped; optionally fall back to raw deflate for the known non-conformant senders noted in the spec. |
| `9110 8-25` | informative | The gzip coding is LZ77 with a 32-bit CRC as produced by gzip (RFC 1952). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The "gzip" coding is an LZ77 coding with a 32-bit Cyclic Redundancy Check (CRC) that is commonly produced by the gzip file compression program [RFC1952]. | Implement gzip decoding per RFC 1952 (including CRC verification). |
| `9110 8-26` | SHOULD | A recipient SHOULD treat "x-gzip" as equivalent to "gzip". <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A recipient SHOULD consider "x-gzip" to be equivalent to "gzip". | Alias x-gzip to gzip in the decoder registry. |
| `9110 8-27` | informative | Content-Language is a comma-separated list of language-tags. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Content-Language = #language-tag | Parse Content-Language with list rules; accept one or more language tags. |
| `9110 8-28` | informative | Absent Content-Language means the content is intended for all language audiences. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | If no Content-Language is specified, the default is that the content is intended for all language audiences. | Do not infer or synthesize a language when the field is absent. |
| `9110 8-29` | MAY | Multiple languages MAY be listed in Content-Language for multi-audience content. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Multiple languages MAY be listed for content that is intended for multiple audiences. | Parser must accept multi-member Content-Language lists (e.g., "mi, en"). |
| `9110 8-30` | MAY | Content-Language MAY appear on any media type, not only textual documents. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Content-Language MAY be applied to any media type -- it is not limited to textual documents. | Never reject or warn on Content-Language for non-text media types. |
| `9110 8-31` | informative | The language-tag production is the RFC 5646 Language-Tag. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | language-tag = <Language-Tag, see [RFC5646], Section 2.1> | Validate Content-Language members against RFC 5646 Language-Tag syntax (or pass through opaquely). |
| `9110 8-32` | informative | A language tag is one or more case-insensitive subtags separated by hyphens. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A language tag is a sequence of one or more case-insensitive subtags, each separated by a hyphen character ("-", %x2D). | Compare language tags case-insensitively; split on "-" only. |
| `9110 8-33` | informative | Whitespace is forbidden inside a language tag. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Whitespace is not allowed within a language tag. | Reject (or refuse to emit) language tags containing whitespace; OWS is only permitted around list commas. |
| `9110 8-34` | informative | Content-Length is one or more DIGITs — a decimal non-negative integer count of octets. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Content-Length = 1*DIGIT | Parse Content-Length strictly as 1*DIGIT: no sign, no whitespace inside, no hex, non-empty. |
| `9110 8-35` | SHOULD | A user agent SHOULD send Content-Length on requests whose method defines content meaning when not using Transfer-Encoding (e.g., POST with empty body still gets Content-Length: 0). <br>_(user-agent,client [CLIENT])_ | A user agent SHOULD send Content-Length in a request when the method defines a meaning for enclosed content and it is not sending Transfer-Encoding. | Emit Content-Length (even 0) on POST/PUT/PATCH-style requests unless chunked Transfer-Encoding is used. |
| `9110 8-36` | SHOULD NOT | A user agent SHOULD NOT send Content-Length when the request has no content and the method does not anticipate any. <br>_(user-agent,client [CLIENT])_ | A user agent SHOULD NOT send a Content-Length header field when the request message does not contain content and the method semantics do not anticipate such data. | Do not emit Content-Length: 0 on bodyless GET/HEAD/DELETE/etc. requests. |
| `9110 8-37` | MAY | A server MAY send Content-Length in a response to HEAD. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | A server MAY send a Content-Length header field in a response to a HEAD request | Accept Content-Length on HEAD responses without ever reading a body from the connection. |
| `9110 8-38` | MUST NOT | A server MUST NOT send Content-Length on a HEAD response unless it equals what GET would have sent. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | a server MUST NOT send Content-Length in such a response unless its field value equals the decimal number of octets that would have been sent in the content of a response if the same request had used the GET method. | Server duty; client MAY rely on HEAD Content-Length as the GET representation size (e.g., for progress/planning), never for framing. |
| `9110 8-39` | MAY | A server MAY send Content-Length in a 304 (Not Modified) response to a conditional GET. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | A server MAY send a Content-Length header field in a 304 (Not Modified) response to a conditional GET request | Accept Content-Length on 304 responses and still treat 304 as bodyless for framing. |
| `9110 8-40` | MUST NOT | A server MUST NOT send Content-Length on a 304 unless it equals what the 200 (OK) content would have been. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | a server MUST NOT send Content-Length in such a response unless its field value equals the decimal number of octets that would have been sent in the content of a 200 (OK) response to the same request. | Server duty; client MAY use a 304's Content-Length to update stored representation metadata, never as body framing. |
| `9110 8-41` | MUST NOT | A server MUST NOT send Content-Length in any 1xx or 204 response. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | A server MUST NOT send a Content-Length header field in any response with a status code of 1xx (Informational) or 204 (No Content). | Client robustness: never derive body framing from a Content-Length seen on 1xx/204; treat those responses as bodyless. |
| `9110 8-42` | MUST NOT | A server MUST NOT send Content-Length in any 2xx response to CONNECT. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | A server MUST NOT send a Content-Length header field in any 2xx (Successful) response to a CONNECT request | After a 2xx to CONNECT, switch to tunnel mode immediately; ignore any Content-Length present. |
| `9110 8-43` | SHOULD | Absent Transfer-Encoding, an origin server SHOULD send Content-Length when the content size is known before the header section completes. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | in the absence of Transfer-Encoding, an origin server SHOULD send a Content-Length header field when the content size is known prior to sending the complete header section. | Server duty; client must still handle responses delimited by connection close when servers omit it. |
| `9110 8-44` | informative | Every Content-Length value >= 0 is valid, with no predefined upper limit. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Any Content-Length field value greater than or equal to zero is valid. | Do not impose an arbitrary maximum on parsed Content-Length values (resource limits are separate policy). |
| `9110 8-45` | MUST | A recipient MUST anticipate arbitrarily large Content-Length numerals and prevent integer overflow or precision loss when parsing. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | a recipient MUST anticipate potentially large decimal numerals and prevent parsing errors due to integer conversion overflows or precision loss due to integer conversion | Parse Content-Length with explicit overflow detection (e.g., checked uint64 accumulation); reject on overflow rather than wrapping. |
| `9110 8-46` | MUST NOT | A sender MUST NOT forward a message whose Content-Length is known to be incorrect (request smuggling / response splitting risk). <br>_(all,proxy [CLIENT])_ | a sender MUST NOT forward a message with a Content-Length header field value that is known to be incorrect. | Ensure emitted Content-Length exactly equals the body octets written; abort rather than send a known-wrong value. |
| `9110 8-47` | MUST NOT | A sender MUST NOT forward a message with a Content-Length that does not match the 1*DIGIT ABNF (single exception in 8-48). <br>_(all,proxy [CLIENT])_ | a sender MUST NOT forward a message with a Content-Length header field value that does not match the ABNF above | Never emit a non-1*DIGIT Content-Length; on receipt, treat malformed values as a framing error, not a best-effort parse. |
| `9110 8-48` | MAY | A recipient of a Content-Length that is the same decimal value repeated as a comma-separated list (e.g., "42, 42") MAY reject the message or collapse it to a single instance. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | MAY either reject the message as invalid or replace that invalid field value with a single instance of the decimal value | Implement a deliberate duplicate-Content-Length policy: identical repeated values may be collapsed to one; differing values must be rejected. |
| `9110 8-49` | informative | Content-Location is an absolute-URI or a partial-URI. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Content-Location = absolute-URI / partial-URI | Parse Content-Location as a URI reference without fragment handling beyond absolute-URI/partial-URI forms. |
| `9110 8-50` | informative | A partial-URI Content-Location is resolved relative to the target URI. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | In the latter case (Section 4), the referenced URI is relative to the target URI ([URI], Section 5). | Resolve relative Content-Location values against the request's target URI before exposing/using them. |
| `9110 8-51` | MAY | When a 2xx response's Content-Location (in absolute form) equals the target URI, the recipient MAY treat the content as a current representation of that resource as of the message origination date. <br>_(client,cache [CLIENT])_ | the recipient MAY consider the content to be a current representation of that resource at the time indicated by the message origination date. | Optional: after PUT/POST with matching Content-Location, allow callers/caches to update their local copy without a follow-up GET. |
| `9110 8-52` | informative | A user agent sending Content-Location in a request asserts the value is where it originally obtained the enclosed representation (a source back link). <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | A user agent that sends Content-Location in a request message is stating that its value refers to where the user agent originally obtained the content of the enclosed representation | Only emit Content-Location on requests when the caller intends the back-link semantics; never auto-generate it. |
| `9110 8-53` | MUST | An origin server receiving Content-Location in a request MUST treat it as transitory request context, not metadata to store verbatim. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | An origin server that receives a Content-Location field in a request message MUST treat the information as transitory request context rather than as metadata to be saved verbatim as part of the representation. | Server-only; no client action. |
| `9110 8-54` | MAY | An origin server MAY use request Content-Location context to guide processing or save it for other uses. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | An origin server MAY use that context to guide in processing the request or to save it for other uses, such as within source links or versioning metadata. | Server-only; no client action. |
| `9110 8-55` | MUST NOT | An origin server MUST NOT let request Content-Location alter the request semantics (e.g., PUT applies to the target URI, not the Content-Location URI). <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | an origin server MUST NOT use such context information to alter the request semantics. | Server-only; client note: to operate on a different resource, send the request to that URI, not via Content-Location. |
| `9110 8-56` | informative | In responses to safe requests, validator fields (ETag, Last-Modified) describe the selected representation chosen while handling the response. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | In responses to safe requests, validator fields describe the selected representation chosen by the origin server while handling the response. | Associate received validators from GET/HEAD with the selected representation for later conditional requests. |
| `9110 8-57` | informative | In a successful response to a state-changing request, validator fields describe the new representation replacing the prior one (e.g., ETag on 201 enables lost-update protection). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | In a successful response to a state-changing request, validator fields describe the new representation that has replaced the prior selected representation as a result of processing the request. | Capture ETag/Last-Modified from PUT/POST/201 responses and use them in subsequent If-Match preconditions. |
| `9110 8-58` | SHOULD | An origin server SHOULD change a weak entity tag whenever prior representations become unacceptable substitutes for the current one. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | An origin server SHOULD change a weak entity tag whenever it considers prior representations to be unacceptable as a substitute for the current representation. | Server-only; no client action. |
| `9110 8-59` | informative | Strong validators are usable for all conditional requests (including ranges and lost-update avoidance); weak validators only where exact byte equality is not required. <br>_(client,cache [CLIENT])_ | Weak validators are only usable when the client does not require exact equality with previously obtained representation data | Never use a weak validator (W/ ETag or implicitly-weak Last-Modified) in If-Range or byte-exact resume logic; strong only. |
| `9110 8-60` | informative | Last-Modified's field value is an HTTP-date timestamp of when the origin believes the selected representation was last modified. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Last-Modified = HTTP-date | Parse Last-Modified with the HTTP-date grammar (IMF-fixdate preferred, obsolete formats accepted per 5.6.7). |
| `9110 8-61` | SHOULD | An origin server SHOULD send Last-Modified whenever a modification date can be reasonably and consistently determined. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | An origin server SHOULD send Last-Modified for any selected representation for which a last modification date can be reasonably and consistently determined | Server-only; client just consumes it when present. |
| `9110 8-62` | SHOULD | An origin server SHOULD obtain the Last-Modified value as close as possible to when it generates the response's Date value. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | An origin server SHOULD obtain the Last-Modified value of the representation as close as possible to the time that it generates the Date field value for its response. | Server-only; underpins the client-side Date-vs-Last-Modified strength deduction in 8-66. |
| `9110 8-63` | MUST NOT | An origin server with a clock MUST NOT generate a Last-Modified date later than its message origination (Date) time. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | An origin server with a clock (as defined in Section 5.6.7) MUST NOT generate a Last-Modified date that is later than the server's time of message origination | Server-only; client MAY treat Last-Modified > Date as suspect (weak) input. |
| `9110 8-64` | MUST | If implementation metadata yields a future modification time, the origin server MUST replace it with the message origination date. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | the origin server MUST replace that value with the message origination date. | Server-only; no client action. |
| `9110 8-65` | MUST NOT | An origin server without a clock MUST NOT generate Last-Modified unless the date was assigned by another system with a clock. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | An origin server without a clock MUST NOT generate a Last-Modified date for a response unless that date value was assigned to the resource by some other system | Server-only; no client action. |
| `9110 8-66` | informative | A Last-Modified time used as a request validator is implicitly weak; a client may deduce it strong only when (for If-Modified-Since/If-Unmodified-Since/If-Range) its cache entry's Date is at least one second after Last-Modified with clock-sync confidence. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A Last-Modified time, when used as a validator in a request, is implicitly weak unless it is possible to deduce that it is strong | Before using Last-Modified in If-Range, verify the stored Date >= Last-Modified + 1s (same-clock heuristic); otherwise treat it as weak and do not use it for ranges. |
| `9110 8-67` | informative | Entity tag grammar: optional case-sensitive "W/" prefix plus a DQUOTE-delimited opaque-tag of etagc characters (VCHAR except DQUOTE, plus obs-text); ETag = entity-tag. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | entity-tag = [ weak ] opaque-tag weak = %s"W/" opaque-tag = DQUOTE *etagc DQUOTE etagc = %x21 / %x23-7E / obs-text | Parse ETag as [W/] "..." with etagc charset; empty opaque-tag ("") is valid; match the W/ prefix case-sensitively. |
| `9110 8-68` | informative | opaque-tag is not a quoted-string: recipients must not backslash-unescape entity tags (legacy RFC 2616 recipients might, so servers avoid backslashes). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | some recipients might perform backslash unescaping. Servers therefore ought to avoid backslash characters in entity tags. | Treat ETag content as opaque bytes; perform no backslash unescaping, and echo it back verbatim in If-Match/If-None-Match/If-Range. |
| `9110 8-69` | MUST | An origin server providing an entity tag that is not a strong validator MUST mark it weak with the case-sensitive "W/" prefix. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | the origin server MUST mark the entity tag as weak by prefixing its opaque value with "W/" (case-sensitive). | Server duty; client parse implication is covered by 8-67 (match W/ case-sensitively, never w/). |
| `9110 8-70` | MAY | A sender MAY deliver the ETag field in the trailer section instead of the header section. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A sender MAY send the ETag field in a trailer section | Client must be able to capture ETag arriving in trailers (chunked HTTP/1.1 trailer section) and surface it with the response. |
| `9110 8-71` | SHOULD | An origin server SHOULD send an ETag whenever change detection can be reasonably and consistently determined. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | An origin server SHOULD send an ETag for any selected representation for which detection of changes can be reasonably and consistently determined | Server-only; client just consumes it when present. |
| `9110 8-72` | informative | Strong comparison: entity tags are equivalent only if both are non-weak and their opaque-tags match character-by-character. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | two entity tags are equivalent if both are not weak and their opaque-tags match character-by-character. | Implement strong ETag comparison (byte-exact opaque-tags, neither W/) and use it wherever exact equality is required (If-Range, range resume). |
| `9110 8-73` | informative | Weak comparison: entity tags are equivalent if their opaque-tags match character-by-character, ignoring weakness flags on either side. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | two entity tags are equivalent if their opaque-tags match character-by-character, regardless of either or both being tagged as "weak". | Implement weak ETag comparison (byte-exact opaque-tags, W/ ignored) for cache validation contexts (If-None-Match). |
| `9110 8-74` | informative | A strong entity tag for a content-encoded representation has to differ from the entity tag of the unencoded representation (content codings are representation properties; transfer codings never affect entity tags). <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | a strong entity tag for a content-encoded representation has to be distinct from the entity tag of an unencoded representation to prevent potential conflicts during cache updates and range requests. | Server duty; client corollary: never combine ranges or cache entries across responses whose ETags differ, even if only Content-Encoding changed. |

### RFC 9110 9 - Methods

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|--------------|---------------------|
| `9110 9-1` | informative | The request method is grammatically a single token. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | method = token | Client must emit the method as a valid RFC 9110 token (no spaces/delimiters); validate user-supplied method strings against token charset. |
| `9110 9-2` | informative | Method names are compared and transmitted case-sensitively; standardized methods are uppercase. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The method token is case-sensitive | Never case-fold method names; send exactly the caller's bytes (use uppercase constants for standard methods). |
| `9110 9-3` | MUST | All general-purpose servers must support GET and HEAD. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | All general-purpose servers MUST support the methods GET and HEAD. | Server-only; client may assume GET/HEAD are universally supported. |
| `9110 9-4` | OPTIONAL | Support for all methods other than GET and HEAD is optional for servers. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | All other methods are OPTIONAL. | Server-only; client should surface 501/405 for non-GET/HEAD methods to the caller rather than treating them as transport errors. |
| `9110 9-5` | SHOULD | An origin server should answer an unrecognized or unimplemented method with 501. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | An origin server that receives a request method that is unrecognized or not implemented SHOULD respond with the 501 (Not Implemented) status code. | Server-only; client just needs to pass 501 through unchanged. |
| `9110 9-6` | SHOULD | An origin server should answer a recognized-but-disallowed method with 405. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | An origin server that receives a request method that is recognized and implemented, but not allowed for the target resource, SHOULD respond with the 405 (Method Not Allowed) status code. | Server-only; client just needs to pass 405 (and its Allow header) through unchanged. |
| `9110 9-7` | informative | GET, HEAD, OPTIONS, and TRACE are the safe methods. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Of the request methods defined by this specification, the GET, HEAD, OPTIONS, and TRACE methods are defined to be safe. | Client's method classification (used by retry/redirect/automation logic) must mark exactly GET/HEAD/OPTIONS/TRACE as safe. |
| `9110 9-8` | SHOULD | A user agent should distinguish safe from unsafe methods when presenting actions to a user. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | A user agent SHOULD distinguish between safe and unsafe methods when presenting potential actions to a user | Library has no UI; expose a safe/unsafe predicate so embedding applications can honor this. |
| `9110 9-9` | MUST | A resource owner must disable unsafe actions reachable via safe request methods. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | the resource owner MUST disable or disallow that action when it is accessed using a safe request method | Origin-side; no client code. |
| `9110 9-10` | informative | PUT, DELETE, and the safe methods are idempotent. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Of the request methods defined by this specification, PUT, DELETE, and safe request methods are idempotent. | Client's idempotency classification must be exactly {GET, HEAD, OPTIONS, TRACE, PUT, DELETE}; POST/CONNECT are not idempotent. |
| `9110 9-11` | informative | An idempotent request may be repeated automatically after a communication failure before the response was read. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | the request can be repeated automatically if a communication failure occurs before the client is able to read the server's response | Retry layer may auto-retry idempotent methods on connection failure before any response bytes; this is the permission basis for default retry policy. |
| `9110 9-12` | SHOULD NOT | A client should not auto-retry a non-idempotent request unless it knows the semantics are idempotent or can detect the original was never applied. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client SHOULD NOT automatically retry a request with a non-idempotent method unless it has some means to know that the request semantics are actually idempotent | Default retry policy must exclude POST/CONNECT/unknown methods; allow opt-in override only via explicit per-request idempotency flag. |
| `9110 9-13` | MUST NOT | A proxy must never auto-retry non-idempotent requests. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | A proxy MUST NOT automatically retry non-idempotent requests. | Proxy-only; no client code. |
| `9110 9-14` | SHOULD NOT | A client should not automatically retry a request that already failed an automatic retry. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client SHOULD NOT automatically retry a failed automatic retry. | Retry layer default must cap automatic retries of a given request at one; verify retry budget accounting does not re-retry a retried attempt. |
| `9110 9-15` | informative | A response can only be cached if the method definition explicitly allows caching. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | a method definition that does not do so cannot be cached | Only relevant if a client-side cache is added; then restrict cacheability to methods whose definitions allow it. |
| `9110 9-16` | informative | Only GET, HEAD, and POST have defined caching semantics in this specification. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | This specification defines caching semantics for GET, HEAD, and POST | Only relevant if a client-side cache is added; never cache PUT/DELETE/CONNECT/OPTIONS/TRACE responses. |
| `9110 9-17` | SHOULD NOT | A client should not send content in a GET request unless the origin server has previously indicated support. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client SHOULD NOT generate content in a GET request unless it is made directly to an origin server that has previously indicated | Do not attach a body to GET by default; if the API permits it, require an explicit opt-in and document the interop/smuggling risk. |
| `9110 9-18` | SHOULD NOT | An origin server should not rely on private agreements to receive GET content. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | An origin server SHOULD NOT rely on private agreements to receive content | Origin-side; no client code. |
| `9110 9-19` | MAY | GET responses are cacheable; a cache may reuse them for later GET and HEAD requests absent Cache-Control restrictions. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | The response to a GET request is cacheable; a cache MAY use it to satisfy subsequent GET and HEAD requests unless otherwise indicated by the Cache-Control header field | Only relevant if a client-side cache is added. |
| `9110 9-20` | MUST NOT | A server must not send content in a response to HEAD. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | The HEAD method is identical to GET except that the server MUST NOT send content in the response. | Response parser must treat HEAD responses as bodiless regardless of Content-Length/Transfer-Encoding; never block reading a body after HEAD (framing detail in RFC 9112 Section 6.3). |
| `9110 9-21` | SHOULD | A server should send the same header fields for HEAD as it would for GET. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | The server SHOULD send the same header fields in response to a HEAD request as it would have sent if the request method had been GET. | Client must tolerate Content-Length/Vary etc. on a HEAD response without expecting body bytes to follow. |
| `9110 9-22` | MAY | A server may omit from a HEAD response header fields whose values are only determined while generating content. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | a server MAY omit header fields for which a value is determined only while generating the content | Client must not require Content-Length (or other content-derived fields) to be present on HEAD responses. |
| `9110 9-23` | SHOULD NOT | A client should not send content in a HEAD request unless the origin server has previously indicated support. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client SHOULD NOT generate content in a HEAD request unless it is made directly to an origin server that has previously indicated | Do not attach a body to HEAD by default; explicit opt-in only. |
| `9110 9-24` | SHOULD NOT | An origin server should not rely on private agreements to receive HEAD content. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | An origin server SHOULD NOT rely on private agreements to receive content | Origin-side; no client code. |
| `9110 9-25` | MAY | HEAD responses are cacheable and may satisfy later HEAD requests absent Cache-Control restrictions. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | The response to a HEAD request is cacheable; a cache MAY use it to satisfy subsequent HEAD requests unless otherwise indicated by the Cache-Control header field | Only relevant if a client-side cache is added. |
| `9110 9-26` | SHOULD | On resource creation via POST, the origin server should send 201 with a Location header identifying the primary created resource. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | the origin server SHOULD send a 201 (Created) response containing a Location header field that provides an identifier for the primary resource created | Origin-side; client may expose Location from 201 responses but has no obligation. |
| `9110 9-27` | informative | POST responses are cacheable only with explicit freshness information plus a Content-Location matching the POST target URI. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | only cacheable when they include explicit freshness information (see Section 4.2.1 of [CACHING]) and a Content-Location header field that has the same value as the POST's target URI | Only relevant if a client-side cache is added; enforce both conditions before storing a POST response. |
| `9110 9-28` | informative | A cached POST response may satisfy later GET/HEAD requests, but a POST request can never be served from cache. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | A cached POST response can be reused to satisfy a later GET or HEAD request. | Only relevant if a client-side cache is added; never serve a POST from cache. |
| `9110 9-29` | MAY | An origin server may answer POST with 303 redirecting to an existing equivalent resource. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | an origin server MAY redirect the user agent to that resource by sending a 303 (See Other) response with the existing resource's identifier in the Location field | Origin-side; client redirect handling for 303 is specified in Section 15.4.4, not here. |
| `9110 9-30` | MUST | If PUT creates a representation where none existed, the origin server must respond 201. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | the origin server MUST inform the user agent by sending a 201 (Created) response | Origin-side; client may interpret 201-to-PUT as creation but implements nothing. |
| `9110 9-31` | MUST | If PUT successfully modifies an existing representation, the origin server must respond 200 or 204. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | the origin server MUST send either a 200 (OK) or a 204 (No Content) response to indicate successful completion of the request | Origin-side; no client code. |
| `9110 9-32` | SHOULD | An origin server should verify the PUT representation against the resource's configured constraints. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | An origin server SHOULD verify that the PUT representation is consistent with its configured constraints for the target resource. | Origin-side; no client code. |
| `9110 9-33` | SHOULD | When a PUT representation is inconsistent with the resource, the origin server should make them consistent or return an explanatory error. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | the origin server SHOULD either make them consistent, by transforming the representation or changing the resource configuration, or respond with an appropriate error message | Origin-side; client just surfaces 409/415. |
| `9110 9-34` | SHOULD | An origin server should ignore unrecognized header and trailer fields in a PUT request. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | an origin server SHOULD ignore unrecognized header and trailer fields received in a PUT request | Origin-side; no client code. |
| `9110 9-35` | MUST NOT | An origin server must not send a validator (ETag/Last-Modified) in a successful PUT response unless the content was saved untransformed and the validator reflects the new representation. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | An origin server MUST NOT send a validator field (Section 8.8), such as an ETag or Last-Modified field, in a successful response to PUT | Origin-side; a client MAY rely on a validator in a PUT success response as covering exactly the body it sent. |
| `9110 9-36` | SHOULD | A service that picks the URI on the client's behalf should use POST, not PUT. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | A service that selects a proper URI on behalf of the client, after receiving a state-changing request, SHOULD be implemented using the POST method rather than PUT. | Origin-side design guidance; no client code. |
| `9110 9-37` | MUST | If the origin server wants a PUT applied to a different resource, it must send a 3xx redirection instead of applying it. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | the origin server MUST send an appropriate 3xx (Redirection) response | Origin-side; pairs with 9-38 on the client side. |
| `9110 9-38` | MAY | The user agent may decide for itself whether to follow a redirect of a PUT request. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | the user agent MAY then make its own decision regarding whether or not to redirect the request | Do not silently auto-follow redirects for PUT; make redirect-following of state-changing methods an explicit policy choice. |
| `9110 9-39` | informative | Responses to PUT are not cacheable, and a successful PUT invalidates stored responses for the target URI. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | Responses to the PUT method are not cacheable. | Only relevant if a client-side cache is added; never store PUT responses and invalidate the URI on success. |
| `9110 9-40` | SHOULD | On successful DELETE the origin server should send 202, 204, or 200 depending on enactment status. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | If a DELETE method is successfully applied, the origin server SHOULD send | Origin-side; client should treat 200/202/204 as DELETE success variants (202 = not yet enacted). |
| `9110 9-41` | SHOULD NOT | A client should not send content in a DELETE request unless the origin server has previously indicated support. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client SHOULD NOT generate content in a DELETE request unless it is made directly to an origin server that has previously indicated | Do not attach a body to DELETE by default; explicit opt-in only. |
| `9110 9-42` | SHOULD NOT | An origin server should not rely on private agreements to receive DELETE content. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | An origin server SHOULD NOT rely on private agreements to receive content | Origin-side; no client code. |
| `9110 9-43` | informative | Responses to DELETE are not cacheable, and a successful DELETE invalidates stored responses for the target URI. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | Responses to the DELETE method are not cacheable. | Only relevant if a client-side cache is added; never store DELETE responses and invalidate the URI on success. |
| `9110 9-44` | informative | The CONNECT request target is a special form: only host and port of the tunnel destination, colon-separated. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | CONNECT uses a special form of request target, unique to this method, consisting of only the host and port number of the tunnel destination, separated by a colon | When emitting CONNECT, build the request target as host:port (authority-form) — never origin-form or absolute-form. |
| `9110 9-45` | MUST | A client must include the port in the CONNECT target even when the source URI elided it. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | a client MUST send the port number even if the CONNECT request is based on a URI reference that contains an authority component with an elided port | Always append the (default-resolved) port when constructing the CONNECT target; reject/complete targets that lack an explicit port. |
| `9110 9-46` | MUST | A server must reject a CONNECT request with an empty or invalid port, typically with 400. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A server MUST reject a CONNECT request that targets an empty or invalid port number, typically by responding with a 400 (Bad Request) status code. | Server-side; client-side port validation before sending (per 9-45) avoids triggering it. |
| `9110 9-47` | MAY | An origin server may accept CONNECT, but most do not implement it. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | An origin server MAY accept a CONNECT request, but most origin servers do not implement CONNECT. | Origin-side; client should only send CONNECT to configured proxies. |
| `9110 9-48` | informative | Any 2xx response to CONNECT switches the connection to tunnel mode immediately after the response header section; non-2xx means no tunnel was formed. <br>_(client,proxy [CLIENT])_ | Any 2xx (Successful) response indicates that the sender (and all inbound proxies) will switch to tunnel mode immediately after the response header section | On 2xx to CONNECT, stop HTTP response parsing at end of header section and hand remaining bytes to the tunnel (e.g., TLS); on non-2xx, keep normal HTTP framing. |
| `9110 9-49` | MUST | A tunnel intermediary detecting one side closed must flush outstanding data to the other side, close both connections, and discard the rest. <br>_(proxy,tunnel [not-client])_ | the intermediary MUST attempt to send any outstanding data that came from the closed side to the other side, close both connections, and then discard any remaining data left undelivered | Intermediary-only; client should expect abrupt tunnel closure when either end closes. |
| `9110 9-50` | SHOULD | Proxies supporting CONNECT should restrict it to known ports or a configurable safe-target list. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | Proxies that support CONNECT SHOULD restrict its use to a limited set of known ports or a configurable list of safe request targets. | Proxy-only; client should tolerate policy rejections (e.g., 403) for non-standard ports. |
| `9110 9-51` | MUST NOT | A server must not send Transfer-Encoding or Content-Length in a 2xx response to CONNECT. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A server MUST NOT send any Transfer-Encoding or Content-Length header fields in a 2xx (Successful) response to CONNECT. | Server-side; the client-side counterpart is 9-52. |
| `9110 9-52` | MUST | A client must ignore Content-Length and Transfer-Encoding in a successful CONNECT response. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client MUST ignore any Content-Length or Transfer-Encoding header fields received in a successful response to CONNECT. | In the 2xx-to-CONNECT path, skip all body-framing logic: never consume tunnel bytes as a message body even if CL/TE headers are present. |
| `9110 9-53` | informative | A CONNECT request message has no content; data after its header section is version-specific tunnel data. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A CONNECT request message does not have content. | Reject or ignore any request body supplied with CONNECT; send no CL/TE on the CONNECT request itself. |
| `9110 9-54` | informative | Responses to CONNECT are not cacheable. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | Responses to the CONNECT method are not cacheable. | Only relevant if a client-side cache is added; never store CONNECT responses. |
| `9110 9-55` | informative | An OPTIONS request with "*" as the request target applies to the server as a whole, not a specific resource. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | An OPTIONS request with an asterisk ("*") as the request target (Section 7.1) applies to the server in general rather than to a specific resource. | Support emitting asterisk-form request target ("OPTIONS * HTTP/1.1") as a server-wide ping distinct from origin-form OPTIONS. |
| `9110 9-56` | SHOULD | A server responding successfully to OPTIONS should send headers indicating implemented optional features (e.g., Allow). <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A server generating a successful response to OPTIONS SHOULD send any header that might indicate optional features implemented by the server and applicable to the target resource | Server-side; client may parse Allow from OPTIONS responses but has no obligation. |
| `9110 9-57` | MAY | A client may send Max-Forwards in an OPTIONS request to target a specific hop. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client MAY send a Max-Forwards header field in an OPTIONS request to target a specific recipient in the request chain | Permit callers to set Max-Forwards on OPTIONS; no automatic generation needed. |
| `9110 9-58` | MUST NOT | A proxy must not generate Max-Forwards while forwarding unless the incoming request carried it. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | A proxy MUST NOT generate a Max-Forwards header field while forwarding a request unless that request was received with a Max-Forwards field. | Proxy-only; no client code. |
| `9110 9-59` | MUST | A client sending an OPTIONS request with content must include a valid Content-Type describing it. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client that generates an OPTIONS request containing content MUST send a valid Content-Type header field describing the representation media type. | If a body is attached to OPTIONS, enforce (or auto-require) a Content-Type header before sending; error out if absent. |
| `9110 9-60` | informative | Responses to OPTIONS are not cacheable. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | Responses to the OPTIONS method are not cacheable. | Only relevant if a client-side cache is added; never store OPTIONS responses. |
| `9110 9-61` | SHOULD | The final recipient of a TRACE should reflect the received message back as the content of a 200 response. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | The final recipient of the request SHOULD reflect the message received, excluding some fields described below, back to the client as the content of a 200 (OK) response. | Server-side; client just reads the reflected message from the 200 body (e.g., message/http). |
| `9110 9-62` | MUST NOT | A client must not put fields containing sensitive data (credentials, cookies) into a TRACE request. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client MUST NOT generate fields in a TRACE request containing sensitive data that might be disclosed by the response. | Strip or refuse Authorization/Proxy-Authorization/Cookie (and other configured sensitive fields) when the method is TRACE. |
| `9110 9-63` | SHOULD | The final recipient should exclude likely-sensitive request fields when generating the TRACE response content. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | The final recipient of the request SHOULD exclude any request fields that are likely to contain sensitive data when that recipient generates the response content. | Server-side; no client code. |
| `9110 9-64` | MUST NOT | A client must not send content in a TRACE request. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client MUST NOT send content in a TRACE request. | Reject any request body when the method is TRACE (hard error, not opt-in). |
| `9110 9-65` | informative | Responses to TRACE are not cacheable. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | Responses to the TRACE method are not cacheable. | Only relevant if a client-side cache is added; never store TRACE responses. |

### RFC 9110 10 - Message Context: Expect, From, Referer, TE, User-Agent, Allow, Location, Retry-After, Server

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|--------------|---------------------|
| `9110 10-1` | informative | The Expect field value is a comma-separated list of expectation members (#expectation). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Expect = #expectation | Emit/parse Expect as a #-list; multiple members and multiple Expect field lines must combine. |
| `9110 10-2` | informative | Each expectation is a token optionally followed by =token or =quoted-string plus parameters. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | expectation = token [ "=" ( token / quoted-string ) parameters ] | Expectation grammar: token, optional =value (token/quoted-string), optional parameters — validate anything the client emits. |
| `9110 10-3` | informative | The Expect field value must be compared case-insensitively. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The Expect field value is case-insensitive. | Any client-side matching of Expect values (e.g. detecting its own 100-continue) must be case-insensitive. |
| `9110 10-4` | informative | 100-continue is the only expectation defined, and it has no parameters. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The only expectation defined by this specification is "100-continue" (with no defined parameters). | Client should only ever generate the bare token 100-continue; never attach parameters to it. |
| `9110 10-5` | MAY | A server may answer an unknown Expect member with 417 (Expectation Failed). <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | A server that receives an Expect field value containing a member other than 100-continue MAY respond with a 417 (Expectation Failed) status code | Client must tolerate 417 responses; surface them, and see 10-10 for the retry rule. |
| `9110 10-6` | MUST NOT | A client must not send a 100-continue expectation on a request that has no content. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client MUST NOT generate a 100-continue expectation in a request that does not include content. | Guard: strip/refuse Expect: 100-continue when the request has no body (nil body / zero framing). |
| `9110 10-7` | MUST | A client that intends to wait for 100 (Continue) before sending the body must actually send Expect: 100-continue. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client that will wait for a 100 (Continue) response before sending the request content MUST send an Expect header field containing a 100-continue expectation. | If the client implements a wait-for-100 mode, it must be gated on having emitted Expect: 100-continue in that request. |
| `9110 10-8` | MAY | A client that sent 100-continue may start sending the body without having received any response. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | such a client MAY proceed to send the content even if it has not yet received a response. | Sending the body before a 100 arrives is legal; do not treat it as a protocol violation and allow timeout-driven body start. |
| `9110 10-9` | SHOULD NOT | A client that sent 100-continue should not wait indefinitely before sending the body. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | such a client SHOULD NOT wait for an indefinite period before sending the content. | Implement a bounded 100-continue wait timeout (e.g. configurable, ~1s default), then send the body anyway. |
| `9110 10-10` | SHOULD | On receiving 417 for a request that carried 100-continue, the client should retry the request without the expectation. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client that receives a 417 (Expectation Failed) status code in response to a request containing a 100-continue expectation SHOULD repeat that request without a 100-continue expectation | Retry layer: on 417 where the request had Expect: 100-continue, resend once with the Expect field removed. |
| `9110 10-11` | MUST | A server must ignore a 100-continue expectation received in an HTTP/1.0 request. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A server that receives a 100-continue expectation in an HTTP/1.0 request MUST ignore that expectation. | Server-only; no client action (client never sends HTTP/1.0 requests here). |
| `9110 10-12` | MAY | A server may skip sending 100 (Continue) if it already received some or all of the content, or framing shows none. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | A server MAY omit sending a 100 (Continue) response if it has already received some or all of the content for the corresponding request | Client must not require a 100 before sending the body or before accepting the final response — 100 may legitimately never arrive. |
| `9110 10-13` | MUST | A server that sent 100 (Continue) must eventually send a final status code unless the connection closes prematurely. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | A server that sends a 100 (Continue) response MUST ultimately send a final status code, once it receives and processes the request content, unless the connection is closed prematurely. | Client must treat 100 as interim: after consuming it, keep reading the same response stream for the final status. |
| `9110 10-14` | SHOULD | A server responding with a final status before reading the whole request body should indicate whether it will close the connection. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A server that responds with a final status code before reading the entire request content SHOULD indicate whether it intends to close the connection | Server-only; client should still honor Connection: close on early final responses (covered by general connection handling). |
| `9110 10-15` | MUST | An origin server receiving an HTTP/1.1 request with 100-continue and content indicated must immediately send either a final response or a 100 (Continue). <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | a 100-continue expectation and an indication that request content will follow, an origin server MUST send either: | Origin-only; informs client 100-wait timeout sizing — a conforming origin responds immediately with 100 or a final status. |
| `9110 10-16` | MUST NOT | The origin server must not wait for the request content before sending the 100 (Continue) response. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | The origin server MUST NOT wait for the content before sending the 100 (Continue) response. | Origin-only; reinforces that a client waiting on 100 is not deadlocked against a conforming origin. |
| `9110 10-17` | MUST | A proxy receiving an HTTP/1.1 request with 100-continue and content indicated must either respond with a final status or forward the request-line and headers onward. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | a 100-continue expectation and indicates a request content will follow, a proxy MUST either: | Proxy-only; no client action. |
| `9110 10-18` | MAY | A proxy that believes the next hop is HTTP/1.0-only may generate an immediate 100 (Continue) itself. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | If the proxy believes (from configuration or past interaction) that the next inbound server only supports HTTP/1.0, the proxy MAY generate an immediate 100 (Continue) response | Proxy-only; consequence for clients: a received 100 does not guarantee the origin accepted the request. |
| `9110 10-19` | informative | The From field value is a single email mailbox. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | From = mailbox | If the client exposes a From option, emit exactly one mailbox value. |
| `9110 10-20` | informative | The mailbox production is imported from RFC 5322 Section 3.4. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | mailbox = <mailbox, see [RFC5322], Section 3.4> | Validate caller-supplied From values against RFC 5322 mailbox syntax, or pass through opaque and document the constraint. |
| `9110 10-21` | SHOULD NOT | A user agent should not send From unless the user explicitly configured it. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | A user agent SHOULD NOT send a From header field without explicit configuration by the user | Never auto-generate From; only emit it when the caller explicitly sets it. |
| `9110 10-22` | SHOULD | A robotic user agent should send a valid From so the operator can be contacted. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | A robotic user agent SHOULD send a valid From header field so that the person responsible for running the robot can be contacted | For load-generator use, document/support setting a contactable From address (robotic UA). |
| `9110 10-23` | SHOULD NOT | A server should not use From for access control or authentication. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A server SHOULD NOT use the From header field for access control or authentication | Server-only; no client action. |
| `9110 10-24` | MUST NOT | A user agent must strip fragment and userinfo components when generating a Referer value. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | A user agent MUST NOT include the fragment and userinfo components of the URI reference [URI], if any, when generating the Referer field value. | Any code that generates Referer (e.g. redirect-following) must remove #fragment and user:pass@ from the URI first. |
| `9110 10-25` | informative | The Referer value is either an absolute-URI or a partial-URI. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Referer = absolute-URI / partial-URI | Only emit Referer values matching absolute-URI or partial-URI (no fragment, per grammar). |
| `9110 10-26` | informative | A partial-URI Referer is interpreted relative to the target URI. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | In the latter case (Section 4), the referenced URI is relative to the target URI ([URI], Section 5). | If emitting a partial-URI Referer, ensure it resolves correctly against the request's target URI. |
| `9110 10-27` | MUST | When the target URI came from a source with no URI of its own, the user agent must omit Referer or send "about:blank". <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | the user agent MUST either exclude the Referer header field or send it with a value of "about:blank" | When no referring URI exists, either send no Referer at all or exactly about:blank — never fabricate one. |
| `9110 10-28` | MAY | A user agent may truncate a Referer value to just the referring origin. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | a user agent MAY truncate parts other than the referring origin | Origin-only Referer truncation is permitted; a privacy-trimming option may drop path/query. |
| `9110 10-29` | SHOULD NOT | A user agent should not send Referer cross-origin when the referring resource was fetched over a secure protocol, unless explicitly allowed. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | A user agent SHOULD NOT send a Referer header field if the referring resource was accessed with a secure protocol and the request target has an origin differing from that of the referring resource | Default policy: suppress Referer on cross-origin requests when the referrer was https, absent explicit opt-in. |
| `9110 10-30` | MUST NOT | A user agent must never send Referer on an unsecured HTTP request when the referring resource was accessed over a secure protocol. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | A user agent MUST NOT send a Referer header field in an unsecured HTTP request if the referring resource was accessed with a secure protocol. | Hard rule in redirect-following: drop Referer on any https-to-http downgrade. |
| `9110 10-31` | informative | Parties limiting Referer disclosure ought to make targeted edits (pseudonyms, truncating query/path) rather than indiscriminate removal. <br>_(proxy,user-agent [CLIENT])_ | Intermediaries and user agent extensions that wish to limit information disclosure in Referer ought to restrict their changes to specific edits | If the client offers Referer trimming, implement it as specific edits (drop query/path), not wholesale deletion that breaks CSRF defenses. |
| `9110 10-32` | SHOULD NOT | An intermediary should not modify or delete Referer when its value shares the target URI's scheme and host. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | An intermediary SHOULD NOT modify or delete the Referer header field when the field value shares the same scheme and host as the target URI. | Intermediary-only; no client action. |
| `9110 10-33` | informative | A TE field containing "trailers" declares that the client will not discard trailer fields. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | a TE field with a "trailers" member sent in a request indicates that the client will not discard trailer fields | Only send TE: trailers if the client actually retains and surfaces trailer fields to the caller. |
| `9110 10-34` | informative | Transfer codings (and thus non-trailers TE members) are only used by HTTP/1.1. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | As of publication, only HTTP/1.1 uses transfer codings | Only offer transfer-coding members in TE on HTTP/1.1 connections; on H2/H3 at most TE: trailers is meaningful (per those specs). |
| `9110 10-35` | informative | The TE field value is a comma-separated list of t-codings members. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | TE = #t-codings | Emit TE as a #-list; an empty TE (implying only chunked acceptable) is syntactically valid. |
| `9110 10-36` | informative | A t-codings member is "trailers" or a transfer-coding with an optional weight. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | t-codings = "trailers" / ( transfer-coding [ weight ] ) | Support the trailers keyword and transfer-coding-with-optional-q-weight forms when constructing TE. |
| `9110 10-37` | informative | A transfer-coding is a token followed by optional semicolon-separated transfer-parameters with OWS. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | transfer-coding = token *( OWS ";" OWS transfer-parameter ) | Parser/emitter for transfer-coding names must handle token + optional ;-separated parameters with optional whitespace. |
| `9110 10-38` | informative | A transfer-parameter is token BWS "=" BWS followed by a token or quoted-string. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | transfer-parameter = token BWS "=" BWS ( token / quoted-string ) | Accept bad whitespace around "=" when parsing; never emit BWS; values may be token or quoted-string. |
| `9110 10-39` | MUST | Any sender of TE must also list "TE" as a connection option in the Connection header field. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A sender of TE MUST also send a "TE" connection option within the Connection header field (Section 7.6.1) to inform intermediaries not to forward this field. | Whenever the HTTP/1.1 client emits a TE header, it must add TE to the Connection header of that request (auto-add or validate). |
| `9110 10-40` | SHOULD | A user agent should send a User-Agent header in every request unless configured not to. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | A user agent SHOULD send a User-Agent header field in each request unless specifically configured not to do so. | Send a default User-Agent (e.g. poseidon-http-client/x.y) on every request, with an explicit opt-out. |
| `9110 10-41` | informative | The User-Agent value is one or more product identifiers, each optionally followed by RWS-separated products or comments. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | User-Agent = product *( RWS ( product / comment ) ) | Default and caller-supplied User-Agent values should match product *( RWS ( product / comment ) ). |
| `9110 10-42` | informative | A product identifier is a token with an optional "/"-separated product-version. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | product = token ["/" product-version] | Build the default UA as name/version where both are valid tokens. |
| `9110 10-43` | informative | A product-version is a token. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | product-version = token | Version part of the product identifier must be a single token (no spaces, no separators outside tchar). |
| `9110 10-44` | SHOULD | A sender should limit product identifiers to what is necessary to identify the product. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A sender SHOULD limit generated product identifiers to what is necessary to identify the product | Keep the generated User-Agent minimal — library name and version only. |
| `9110 10-45` | MUST NOT | A sender must not put advertising or other nonessential information in a product identifier. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | a sender MUST NOT generate advertising or other nonessential information within the product identifier | The library's generated product token must contain no promotional or extraneous data. |
| `9110 10-46` | SHOULD NOT | A sender should not put non-version information in the product-version portion. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A sender SHOULD NOT generate information in product-version that is not a version identifier | product-version must carry only the version string; successive releases differ only there. |
| `9110 10-47` | SHOULD NOT | A user agent should not generate a needlessly fine-grained User-Agent value. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | A user agent SHOULD NOT generate a User-Agent header field containing needlessly fine-grained detail | Do not embed OS/build/host detail in the default UA (fingerprinting and latency). |
| `9110 10-48` | SHOULD | A user agent should limit the addition of third-party subproducts to the User-Agent value. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | SHOULD limit the addition of subproducts by third parties | Do not let plugins/wrappers append unbounded subproduct tokens to the UA string. |
| `9110 10-49` | informative | Implementations are discouraged from using other implementations' product tokens to declare compatibility. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | implementations are encouraged not to use the product tokens of other implementations in order to declare compatibility with them | Default UA must not masquerade as another client; UA spoofing only via explicit caller override. |
| `9110 10-50` | informative | The Allow field value is a comma-separated list of methods. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Allow = #method | If the client parses Allow (e.g. on 405), split as a #-list of method tokens across possibly multiple field lines. |
| `9110 10-51` | MUST | An origin server must include Allow in every 405 (Method Not Allowed) response. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | An origin server MUST generate an Allow header field in a 405 (Method Not Allowed) response | Origin-only; client may rely on Allow being present on 405 and expose it, but must not fail if a broken server omits it. |
| `9110 10-52` | MAY | An origin server may include Allow in any other response. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | MAY do so in any other response | Origin-only; client should not treat Allow on non-405 responses as an error. |
| `9110 10-53` | informative | An empty Allow field value means the resource currently allows no methods. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | An empty Allow field value indicates that the resource allows no methods | Accept an empty Allow value as valid and interpret it as the empty method set, not a parse error. |
| `9110 10-54` | MUST NOT | A proxy must not modify the Allow header field. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | A proxy MUST NOT modify the Allow header field | Proxy-only; no client action. |
| `9110 10-55` | informative | The Location field value is a single URI-reference. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Location = URI-reference | Parse Location as one URI-reference (relative references allowed); never list-split it. |
| `9110 10-56` | informative | A relative-reference Location value is resolved against the target URI to obtain the final value. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | When it has the form of a relative reference ([URI], Section 4.2), the final value is computed by resolving it against the target URI ([URI], Section 5). | Redirect-following must RFC 3986 resolve relative Location values against the request's target URI. |
| `9110 10-57` | informative | In a 201 (Created) response, Location identifies the primary resource created. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | For 201 (Created) responses, the Location value refers to the primary resource created by the request. | Do not auto-redirect on 201 Location; expose it as the created resource's URI. |
| `9110 10-58` | informative | In a 3xx response, Location identifies the preferred target for automatic redirection. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | For 3xx (Redirection) responses, the Location value refers to the preferred target resource for automatically redirecting the request. | Redirect logic uses 3xx Location as the next target URI. |
| `9110 10-59` | MUST | If a 3xx Location has no fragment, the user agent must apply the original reference's fragment to the redirect target. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | If the Location value provided in a 3xx (Redirection) response does not have a fragment component, a user agent MUST process the redirection as if the value inherits the fragment component | On redirect, carry the original request reference's #fragment onto the resolved Location when Location itself lacks one; keep Location's own fragment when present. |
| `9110 10-60` | informative | Location cannot be a comma-separated list; commas are valid URI data, so combined multiple Location lines are not reliably recoverable. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A Location field value cannot allow a list of members because the comma list separator is a valid data character within a URI-reference. | Never comma-split Location; treat multiple Location field lines as invalid/ambiguous rather than joining them (recovery is optional, not mandated). |
| `9110 10-61` | informative | Retry-After on a 503 indicates how long the service is expected to be unavailable. <br>_(server,user-agent [CLIENT])_ | When sent with a 503 (Service Unavailable) response, Retry-After indicates how long the service is expected to be unavailable to the client. | Retry/backoff layer should honor Retry-After on 503 as the minimum delay before retrying. |
| `9110 10-62` | informative | Retry-After on any 3xx indicates the minimum time to wait before issuing the redirected request. <br>_(server,user-agent [CLIENT])_ | When sent with any 3xx (Redirection) response, Retry-After indicates the minimum time that the user agent is asked to wait before issuing the redirected request. | Redirect-following should delay the follow-up request by Retry-After when present on a 3xx. |
| `9110 10-63` | informative | The Retry-After value is either an HTTP-date or delay-seconds. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Retry-After = HTTP-date / delay-seconds | Parser must accept both forms: an HTTP-date (absolute) and a plain integer (relative seconds). |
| `9110 10-64` | informative | delay-seconds is 1*DIGIT, a non-negative decimal integer representing seconds. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | delay-seconds = 1*DIGIT | Parse delay-seconds as digits only (no sign, no fraction); guard against overflow on huge values. |
| `9110 10-65` | MAY | An origin server may generate a Server header field in its responses. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | An origin server MAY generate a Server header field in its responses. | Server-only; client just passes the field through to callers. |
| `9110 10-66` | informative | The Server value is one or more product identifiers with optional RWS-separated products or comments (same grammar as User-Agent). <br>_(all [not-client])_ | Server = product *( RWS ( product / comment ) ) | Server emission grammar; client may treat received Server values as opaque bytes. |
| `9110 10-67` | SHOULD NOT | An origin server should not generate a needlessly fine-grained Server value. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | An origin server SHOULD NOT generate a Server header field containing needlessly fine-grained detail | Server-only; no client action. |
| `9110 10-68` | SHOULD | An origin server should limit third-party subproduct additions to the Server value. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | and SHOULD limit the addition of subproducts by third parties | Server-only; no client action. |

### RFC 9110 11 - HTTP Authentication

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|--------------|---------------------|
| `9110 11-1` | informative | The authentication scheme identifier is a token that is matched case-insensitively. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | It uses a case-insensitive token to identify the authentication scheme | Compare scheme names (e.g. "Basic" vs "basic") with ASCII case-insensitive comparison when matching challenges to configured credentials. |
| `9110 11-2` | informative | Grammar: auth-scheme is a token. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | auth-scheme = token | Parse the scheme name using the RFC 9110 token character set; reject/emit only token chars in the scheme position of Authorization/Proxy-Authorization. |
| `9110 11-3` | informative | After the scheme name, authentication information is either a comma-separated list of auth-params or a single token68 blob. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | as either a comma-separated list of parameters or a single sequence of characters capable of holding base64-encoded information | Challenge/credentials parser and serializer must support both shapes: scheme SP token68, and scheme SP name=value[, name=value...]. |
| `9110 11-4` | informative | Grammar: token68 is one or more ALPHA / DIGIT / "-" / "." / "_" / "~" / "+" / "/" followed by any number of "=". <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | token68 = 1*( ALPHA / DIGIT / "-" / "." / "_" / "~" / "+" / "/" ) *"=" | Implement token68 lexer exactly (padding "=" only at end, no whitespace); needed to hold base64/base64url/base32/base16 credential blobs like Basic. |
| `9110 11-5` | MUST | Auth-param names are matched case-insensitively and each parameter name must occur at most once per challenge. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | the name token is matched case-insensitively and each parameter name MUST only occur once per challenge | When emitting credentials with auth-params never repeat a name; when parsing challenges match param names case-insensitively (treat duplicate names in a received challenge as malformed or first-wins, but never emit them). |
| `9110 11-6` | informative | Grammar: auth-param is token BWS "=" BWS then token or quoted-string. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | auth-param = token BWS "=" BWS ( token / quoted-string ) | Parser must tolerate bad whitespace (BWS) around "=" and accept both token and quoted-string values; emitter should generate no BWS. |
| `9110 11-7` | informative | Both token and quoted-string notations for auth-param values must be accepted by generic parsing, for senders and recipients alike. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Authentication scheme definitions need to accept both notations, both for senders and recipients | Use one generic auth-param parser that handles token and quoted-string (with quoted-pair unescaping) regardless of scheme. |
| `9110 11-8` | informative | A 401 (Unauthorized) response is the origin server's challenge to a user agent and carries WWW-Authenticate with at least one applicable challenge. <br>_(origin,user-agent [CLIENT])_ | A 401 (Unauthorized) response message is used by an origin server to challenge the authorization of a user agent | On 401, parse WWW-Authenticate challenge list and surface it to auth logic (retry with credentials or fail); do not treat 401 as transport error. |
| `9110 11-9` | informative | A 407 (Proxy Authentication Required) response is a proxy's challenge to a client and carries Proxy-Authenticate with at least one applicable challenge. <br>_(proxy,client [CLIENT])_ | A 407 (Proxy Authentication Required) response message is used by a proxy to challenge the authorization of a client | On 407, parse Proxy-Authenticate and retry through the proxy with Proxy-Authorization; keep 401 and 407 handling paths distinct. |
| `9110 11-10` | informative | Grammar: a challenge is auth-scheme optionally followed by 1*SP and either token68 or a #-list of auth-params. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | challenge = auth-scheme [ 1*SP ( token68 / #auth-param ) ] | Challenge parser must accept scheme-only challenges, scheme+token68, and scheme+param-list, with one-or-more SP (not just one) between scheme and params. |
| `9110 11-11` | informative | Clients notoriously fail to parse challenge lists containing unknown schemes; a conforming parser must not choke on an unrecognized scheme. <br>_(client,user-agent [CLIENT])_ | Many clients fail to parse a challenge that contains an unknown scheme. | Skip unknown schemes gracefully while continuing to parse remaining challenges in the list; add a test with an unknown scheme listed before a known one. |
| `9110 11-12` | informative | A user agent may authenticate to an origin server by including Authorization, usually but not necessarily after receiving a 401 (preemptive auth is allowed). <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | can do so by including an Authorization header field with the request | Support attaching Authorization on the initial request (preemptive), not only in a 401-retry loop. |
| `9110 11-13` | informative | A client may authenticate to a proxy by including Proxy-Authorization, usually but not necessarily after receiving a 407. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | can do so by including a Proxy-Authorization header field with the request | Support preemptive Proxy-Authorization on requests sent through a proxy that is known to require authentication. |
| `9110 11-14` | informative | Grammar: credentials have the same shape as a challenge — auth-scheme optionally followed by 1*SP and token68 or a #-list of auth-params. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | credentials = auth-scheme [ 1*SP ( token68 / #auth-param ) ] | Serialize Authorization/Proxy-Authorization values exactly to this grammar; single SP between scheme and token68 is the conventional emission. |
| `9110 11-15` | informative | When creating credentials, the user agent ought to select the challenge with the most secure auth-scheme it understands. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | the user agent ought to do so by selecting the challenge with what it considers to be the most secure auth-scheme that it understands | When multiple challenges are offered, pick by a security preference order (e.g. prefer Digest/Bearer over Basic), not by list position. |
| `9110 11-16` | SHOULD | An origin server should answer a protected-resource request with missing, invalid, or partial credentials with a 401 carrying at least one (possibly new) challenge. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | an origin server SHOULD send a 401 (Unauthorized) response that contains a WWW-Authenticate header field with at least one (possibly new) challenge | Server-side duty; client must nonetheless be prepared for a fresh/different challenge after a failed attempt and re-run scheme selection rather than blindly resending the same credentials. |
| `9110 11-17` | SHOULD | A proxy requiring authentication should answer a request with missing/invalid/partial proxy credentials with a 407 carrying at least one (possibly new) challenge. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | a proxy that requires authentication SHOULD generate a 407 (Proxy Authentication Required) response that contains a Proxy-Authenticate header field | Proxy-side duty; client should expect a new challenge on 407 after failed proxy auth and retry accordingly. |
| `9110 11-18` | informative | A server receiving valid credentials that are not adequate for access ought to respond 403 (Forbidden), not 401. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A server that receives valid credentials that are not adequate to gain access ought to respond with the 403 (Forbidden) status code | Server-side; client should NOT re-prompt or retry credentials on 403 — only 401/407 mean "try (different) credentials". |
| `9110 11-19` | informative | The "realm" auth-param is reserved for schemes that indicate a scope of protection. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The "realm" authentication parameter is reserved for use by authentication schemes that wish to indicate a scope of protection. | Treat "realm" as a reserved, scheme-independent parameter; never repurpose it, and extract it during challenge parsing for credential scoping. |
| `9110 11-20` | informative | A protection space is defined by the origin of the server being accessed combined with the realm value if present. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A "protection space" is defined by the origin (see Section 4.3.1) of the server being accessed, in combination with the realm value if present. | Key any credential cache by (scheme://host:port origin, realm) tuple, never by hostname or URL prefix alone. |
| `9110 11-21` | informative | A single response can carry multiple challenges with the same auth-scheme but different realms. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | a response can have multiple challenges with the same auth-scheme but with different realms | Challenge parser must not deduplicate by scheme name; keep all (scheme, realm) pairs distinct. |
| `9110 11-22` | MAY | After a prior authorized request, the user agent may reuse the same credentials for other requests in that protection space for a scheme/preference-determined period. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | the user agent MAY reuse the same credentials for all other requests within that protection space | Credential caching is optional; if implemented, bound reuse to the protection space (origin+realm) and allow expiry/timeout configuration. |
| `9110 11-23` | informative | A protection space cannot extend outside the scope of its server unless the authentication scheme specifically allows it. <br>_(client,user-agent [CLIENT])_ | Unless specifically allowed by the authentication scheme, a single protection space cannot extend outside the scope of its server. | Never automatically apply cached credentials to a different origin (host, port, or scheme change), including across redirects. |
| `9110 11-24` | MUST | For the realm parameter, a sender must generate only the quoted-string syntax, never the token form. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | For historical reasons, a sender MUST only generate the quoted-string syntax. | If the client ever emits a realm auth-param (in credentials), always serialize it as a quoted-string (realm="..."), even when the value would be a valid token. |
| `9110 11-25` | informative | Recipients might have to accept realm in both token and quoted-string syntax for interoperability. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Recipients might have to support both token and quoted-string syntax for maximum interoperability with existing clients | Challenge parser must accept realm=token as well as realm="quoted" from servers, despite the sender-side quoted-string-only rule. |
| `9110 11-26` | informative | Grammar: WWW-Authenticate is a #-list of one or more challenges. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | WWW-Authenticate = #challenge | Parse WWW-Authenticate with HTTP list semantics: multiple challenges per field line and challenges split across multiple field lines must both combine. |
| `9110 11-27` | MUST | A server generating a 401 response must include a WWW-Authenticate header field containing at least one challenge. <br>_(server,origin [not-client])_ | A server generating a 401 (Unauthorized) response MUST send a WWW-Authenticate header field containing at least one challenge. | Server duty; client may rely on its presence for the auth-retry path but should tolerate (and simply fail auth on) a broken 401 without it. |
