| `9110 14-49` | SHOULD | A server generating a 416 to a byte-range request should send Content-Range with an unsatisfied-range value. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | A server generating a 416 (Range Not Satisfiable) response to a byte-range request SHOULD send a Content-Range header field with an unsatisfied-range value | On 416, parse Content-Range: bytes */N when present, but tolerate its absence. |
| `9110 14-50` | informative | The complete-length in a 416 response is the current length of the selected representation. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | The complete-length in a 416 response indicates the current length of the selected representation. | Use the 416 unsatisfied-range complete-length to recompute a satisfiable range before retrying. |
| `9110 14-51` | informative | Content-Range is meaningful only on status codes that define it; here only 206 and 416. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | only the 206 (Partial Content) and 416 (Range Not Satisfiable) status codes describe a meaning for Content-Range. | Ignore Content-Range semantics on any response status other than 206 and 416. |
| `9110 14-52` | SHOULD | An origin server should respond 400 to a PUT with Content-Range on a resource that does not support partial PUT. <br>_(origin [CLIENT])_ | An origin server SHOULD respond with a 400 (Bad Request) status code if it receives Content-Range on a PUT for a target resource that does not support partial PUT requests. | A client attempting partial PUT must handle 400 as the expected 'unsupported' outcome. |
| `9110 14-53` | informative | Partial PUT is not backwards compatible with the original PUT definition and may be applied as a complete replacement. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | Partial PUT is not backwards compatible with the original definition of PUT. It may result in the content being written as a complete replacement for the current representation. | Never send Content-Range on PUT to an unverified server: the partial body could be stored as the entire representation (data loss). |
| `9110 14-54` | informative | A 206 containing multiple ranges transmits them as body parts of a multipart/byteranges message body. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | When a 206 (Partial Content) response message includes the content of multiple ranges, they are transmitted as body parts in a multipart message body | If the client emits multi-range requests, it must implement a multipart/byteranges body parser (RFC 2046 Section 5.1 framing). |
| `9110 14-55` | informative | Each multipart/byteranges body part carries its own Content-Type and Content-Range; the boundary parameter is required. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The "multipart/byteranges" media type includes one or more body parts, each with its own Content-Type and Content-Range fields. The required boundary parameter specifies the boundary string used to separate each body part. | Extract the mandatory boundary parameter from the response Content-Type and read per-part Content-Range/Content-Type from each part's header section. |
| `9110 14-56` | informative | Additional CRLFs may precede the first boundary string in a multipart/byteranges body. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Additional CRLFs might precede the first boundary string in the body. | The multipart parser must skip leading CRLFs before the first boundary delimiter. |
| `9110 14-57` | informative | RFC 2046 permits a quoted boundary string, but some implementations mishandle it. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Although [RFC2046] permits the boundary string to be quoted, some existing implementations handle a quoted boundary string incorrectly. | Accept and correctly unquote a quoted boundary parameter when parsing Content-Type: multipart/byteranges. |
| `9110 14-58` | informative | Legacy implementations used the almost-compatible media type multipart/x-byteranges. <br>_(all [not-client])_ | used a media type of "multipart/x-byteranges", which is almost (but not quite) compatible with this type. | Optionally recognize multipart/x-byteranges from legacy servers; not required for conformance. |
| `9110 14-59` | informative | multipart/byteranges is not limited to byte ranges despite its name. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Despite the name, the "multipart/byteranges" media type is not limited to byte ranges. | The multipart parser must not assume the bytes unit in per-part Content-Range fields. |
| `9110 14-60` | informative | multipart/byteranges content permits only 7bit, 8bit, or binary encoding considerations. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Encoding considerations: only "7bit", "8bit", or "binary" are permitted | Parser may treat part bodies as raw octets; no base64/quoted-printable transfer decoding is needed for multipart/byteranges parts. |

### RFC 9110 15.1-15.3 - Status Codes: overview, Informational 1xx, Successful 2xx

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|--------------|---------------------|
| `9110 15.1-15.3-1` | informative | A response status code is a three-digit integer, and all valid status codes are in the range 100 to 599 inclusive. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | All valid status codes are within the range of 100 to 599, inclusive. | Response parser must accept exactly three digits and know 100..599 is the valid range (see -5/-6 for handling out-of-range). |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-2` | informative | The first digit of the status code defines the response class; the last two digits carry no categorization role. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The first digit of the status code defines the class of response. The last two digits do not have any categorization role. | Classify responses (1xx/2xx/3xx/4xx/5xx) by first digit only; never branch semantics on the last two digits of unknown codes. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-3` | informative | A client is not required to understand the meaning of every registered status code. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client is not required to understand the meaning of all registered status codes, though such understanding is obviously desirable. | No exhaustive status-code table is needed; unknown codes are legal input and must not be rejected as parse errors. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-4` | MUST | A client must understand the class of any status code from its first digit and treat an unrecognized code as the x00 code of that class. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | a client MUST understand the class of any status code, as indicated by the first digit, and treat an unrecognized status code as being equivalent to the x00 status code of that class. | Map unrecognized codes (e.g. 471) to class default (400) for all semantic decisions (retryability, error surfacing); check the fallback path in status handling. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-5` | informative | Status code values outside the range 100..599 are invalid. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Values outside the range 100..599 are invalid. | Parser must detect status < 100 or > 599 as invalid (do not silently accept 600..999 as normal responses); pair with -6 for required handling. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-6` | SHOULD | A client receiving a response with an invalid status code should process it as a 5xx (Server Error). <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client that receives a response with an invalid status code SHOULD process the response as if it had a 5xx (Server Error) status code. | On out-of-range status, do not hard-fail the parse: treat the response as 5xx (server-error semantics, retry policy of 500) rather than a connection error. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-7` | informative | A single request can elicit zero or more interim 1xx responses followed by exactly one final response. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | zero or more "interim" (non-final) responses with status codes in the "informational" (1xx) range, followed by exactly one "final" response | Response state machine must loop over any number of 1xx interim responses per request and only complete on the single non-1xx final response. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-8` | informative | Reason phrases are only recommendations and can be replaced or omitted without affecting the protocol. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The reason phrases listed here are only recommendations -- they can be replaced by local equivalents or left out altogether without affecting the protocol. | Never key any behavior on the reason phrase; accept an empty or arbitrary reason phrase in the status line. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-9` | informative | Responses with heuristically cacheable status codes (200, 203, 204, 206, 300, 301, 308, 404, 405, 410, 414, 501) can be reused by a cache with heuristic expiration; all others cannot. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | can be reused by a cache with heuristic expiration unless otherwise indicated by the method definition or explicit cache controls [CACHING]; all other status codes are not heuristically cacheable. | Only relevant if the client grows a cache layer; then restrict heuristic freshness to exactly this status-code list. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-10` | informative | Status codes beyond this specification ought to be registered in the HTTP Status Code Registry. <br>_(all [not-client])_ | All such status codes ought to be registered within the "Hypertext Transfer Protocol (HTTP) Status Code Registry" | No client action; registration guidance for extension authors. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-11` | informative | The 1xx class indicates an interim response communicating connection status or request progress before the final response. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The 1xx (Informational) class of status code indicates an interim response for communicating connection status or request progress prior to completing the requested action and sending a final response. | Treat every 1xx as non-final: keep the request/response exchange open and continue reading for the final response. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-12` | MUST NOT | A server must not send a 1xx response to an HTTP/1.0 client. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | a server MUST NOT send a 1xx response to an HTTP/1.0 client. | Server-side rule; irrelevant while the client only emits HTTP/1.1 requests (it never presents itself as HTTP/1.0). |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-13` | informative | A 1xx response is terminated by the end of its header section and cannot contain content or trailers. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A 1xx response is terminated by the end of the header section; it cannot contain content or trailers. | Framing: after a 1xx status line + header block + CRLF, read no body — immediately resume waiting for the next (interim or final) response. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-14` | MUST | A client must be able to parse one or more 1xx responses received before the final response, even when unexpected. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client MUST be able to parse one or more 1xx responses received prior to a final response, even if the client does not expect one. | Parser must consume any number of unsolicited 1xx responses (including 100 without Expect sent) without error before the final response; test multiple consecutive 1xx. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-15` | MAY | A user agent may ignore unexpected 1xx responses. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | A user agent MAY ignore unexpected 1xx responses. | Silently discarding unexpected 1xx (after parsing them) is conformant; optionally surface via hooks (cf. HTTP/2 stack's 1xx event handling). |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-16` | MUST | A proxy must forward 1xx responses unless the proxy itself requested their generation. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | A proxy MUST forward 1xx responses unless the proxy itself requested the generation of the 1xx response. | Proxy-only; no action for a request-sending client. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-17` | informative | 100 (Continue) indicates the initial part of the request was received and not yet rejected, and the server intends a final response. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The 100 (Continue) status code indicates that the initial part of a request has been received and has not yet been rejected by the server. | Treat 100 as interim only; never resolve the request on it. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-18` | informative | When the request carried Expect: 100-continue, on receiving 100 the client ought to continue sending the request content and discard the 100 response. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | The client ought to continue sending the request and discard the 100 response. | If Expect: 100-continue is supported, a 100 must unblock body transmission; the 100 itself is not delivered as the response result. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-19` | informative | If the request did not contain an Expect field with 100-continue, the client can simply discard a 100 interim response. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | If the request did not contain an Expect header field containing the 100-continue expectation, the client can simply discard this interim response. | Unsolicited 100 must be tolerated and dropped without altering request state; test 100 arriving when no Expect was sent. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-20` | informative | 101 (Switching Protocols) means the server agrees, via the Upgrade mechanism, to change the application protocol on this connection. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | indicates that the server understands and is willing to comply with the client's request, via the Upgrade header field (Section 7.8), for a change in the application protocol | On 101 (only valid if the client sent Upgrade), stop HTTP/1.1 parsing after the 101 header section and hand the connection to the new protocol; if Upgrade was never sent, treat as an error. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-21` | MUST | The server must generate an Upgrade header field in a 101 response indicating the protocol(s) in effect afterwards. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | The server MUST generate an Upgrade header field in the response that indicates which protocol(s) will be in effect after this response. | Client may rely on Upgrade being present in a 101 to select the follow-on protocol; validate it names a protocol the client offered. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-22` | informative | The 2xx class indicates the request was successfully received, understood, and accepted. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The 2xx (Successful) class of status code indicates that the client's request was successfully received, understood, and accepted. | Classify any 2xx (including unknown ones like 299, per -4 mapped to 200) as success for retry/metrics/error surfaces. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-23` | informative | 200 (OK) indicates the request succeeded; the content of a 200 response depends on the request method (Table 6). <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The 200 (OK) status code indicates that the request has succeeded. The content sent in a 200 response depends on the request method. | Deliver 200 as success; interpretation of the body (target representation vs. action status) is per-method and belongs to the caller. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-24` | informative | Except for responses to CONNECT, a 200 response is expected to contain content unless the framing explicitly indicates zero length. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Aside from responses to CONNECT, a 200 response is expected to contain message content unless the message framing explicitly indicates that the content has zero length. | Body presence on 200 is decided purely by framing (Content-Length/Transfer-Encoding/close), never assumed absent; do not special-case 200 in body-length logic. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-25` | informative | If the request indicates a preference for no content on success, the origin server ought to send 204 instead of 200. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | If some aspect of the request indicates a preference for no content upon success, the origin server ought to send a 204 (No Content) response instead. | Origin-server guidance; no client action. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-26` | informative | A 200 response to CONNECT has no content; the tunnel begins immediately after the 200 response header section. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | For CONNECT, there is no content because the successful result is a tunnel, which begins immediately after the 200 response header section. | If CONNECT is supported: on 2xx to CONNECT, switch to raw tunnel mode right after the header section and ignore any Content-Length/Transfer-Encoding for framing. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-27` | informative | A 200 response is heuristically cacheable unless otherwise indicated. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | A 200 response is heuristically cacheable; i.e., unless otherwise indicated by the method definition or explicit cache controls | Cache-layer rule only; not applicable to the cacheless client. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-28` | SHOULD | In 200 responses to GET or HEAD, an origin server should send available validator fields, preferring a strong entity tag plus Last-Modified. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | an origin server SHOULD send any available validator fields (Section 8.8) for the selected representation, with both a strong entity tag and a Last-Modified date being preferred. | Server emission rule; client just passes validators through to callers. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-29` | informative | In 200 responses to state-changing methods, validator fields convey the current validators of the new representation produced by the request. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | any validator fields (Section 8.8) sent in the response convey the current validators for the new representation formed as a result of successfully applying the request semantics. | If the client exposes ETag/Last-Modified helpers, on 200 to PUT/POST/DELETE interpret them as validators of the new representation, not of the request body echoed back. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-30` | informative | For 201 (Created), the primary created resource is identified by the Location header field, or by the target URI if no Location is received. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The primary resource created by the request is identified by either a Location header field in the response or, if no Location header field is received, by the target URI. | Any created-resource helper must fall back to the request target URI when 201 has no Location field. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-31` | informative | In a 201 response, validator fields convey the current validators for the new representation created by the request. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | Any validator fields (Section 8.8) sent in the response convey the current validators for a new representation created by the request. | Interpret ETag/Last-Modified on 201 as belonging to the newly created resource's representation. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-32` | informative | 202 (Accepted) indicates the request was accepted for processing but processing has not completed and may never be acted upon. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The 202 (Accepted) status code indicates that the request has been accepted for processing, but the processing has not been completed. | Treat 202 as success at the HTTP layer without implying the action completed; no special framing. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-33` | informative | 203 (Non-Authoritative Information) indicates a successful request whose content was modified by a transforming proxy from the origin's 200 response. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | the request was successful but the enclosed content has been modified from that of the origin server's 200 (OK) response by a transforming proxy | Treat 203 as 2xx success; optionally expose the non-authoritative flag to callers. No framing difference from 200. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-34` | informative | A 203 response is heuristically cacheable unless otherwise indicated. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | A 203 response is heuristically cacheable; i.e., unless otherwise indicated by the method definition or explicit cache controls | Cache-layer rule only. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-35` | informative | 204 (No Content) indicates success with no additional content to send; response header metadata refers to the target resource after the action. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | the server has successfully fulfilled the request and that there is no additional content to send in the response content. | Deliver 204 as success with an empty body; header fields (e.g. ETag) apply to the updated target resource. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-36` | informative | A 204 response is terminated by the end of its header section and cannot contain content or trailers. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A 204 response is terminated by the end of the header section; it cannot contain content or trailers. | Framing: on status 204, read zero body octets regardless of any Content-Length/Transfer-Encoding present; connection stays in sync for the next response. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-37` | informative | A 204 response is heuristically cacheable unless otherwise indicated. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | A 204 response is heuristically cacheable; i.e., unless otherwise indicated by the method definition or explicit cache controls | Cache-layer rule only. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-38` | informative | 205 (Reset Content) asks the user agent to reset the document view that caused the request to its original state. <br>_(user-agent [not-client])_ | the server has fulfilled the request and desires that the user agent reset the "document view", which caused the request to be sent, to its original state | UI-level semantics; a library/load-gen client has no document view. Treat 205 as ordinary 2xx with normal framing. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-39` | MUST NOT | A server must not generate content in a 205 response. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | a server MUST NOT generate content in a 205 response. | Server emission rule; client still frames a 205 body per normal message-length rules (205 is not in the header-terminated set like 204/1xx/304). |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-40` | informative | 206 (Partial Content) indicates the server is fulfilling a range request by transferring one or more parts of the selected representation. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | the server is successfully fulfilling a range request for the target resource by transferring one or more parts of the selected representation. | Treat 206 as success carrying partial data; never treat a 206 body as the full representation. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-41` | MUST | A client must inspect a 206 response's Content-Type and Content-Range field(s) to determine what parts are enclosed and whether more requests are needed. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client MUST inspect a 206 response's Content-Type and Content-Range field(s) to determine what parts are enclosed and whether additional requests are needed. | If the client issues Range requests, its 206 path must parse Content-Type (multipart detection) and Content-Range before consuming the body; the server may have satisfied only a subset. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-42` | MUST | A server generating 206 must also generate Date, Cache-Control, ETag, Expires, Content-Location, and Vary if the field would have been sent in a 200 to the same request. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | if the field would have been sent in a 200 (OK) response to the same request: Date, Cache-Control, ETag, Expires, Content-Location, and Vary. | Server emission rule; client should not require these fields to be present. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-43` | informative | Content-Length in a 206 response gives the octet count of this message's content, usually not the complete length of the selected representation. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A Content-Length header field present in a 206 response indicates the number of octets in the content of this message, which is usually not the complete length of the selected representation. | Frame the 206 body by its Content-Length as usual, but take the representation's complete length only from Content-Range's complete-length, never from Content-Length. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-44` | SHOULD NOT | A sender generating a 206 to a request with If-Range should not generate representation header fields beyond those required. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A sender that generates a 206 response to a request with an If-Range header field SHOULD NOT generate other representation header fields beyond those required | Server rule; corollary for client: when using If-Range, retain representation headers from the prior response since the 206 may omit them. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-45` | MUST | Otherwise (no If-Range), a 206 sender must generate all representation header fields that a 200 to the same request would have carried. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | Otherwise, a sender MUST generate all of the representation header fields that would have been sent in a 200 (OK) response to the same request. | Server emission rule; client may rely on full representation headers on non-If-Range 206s but should tolerate absence. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-46` | informative | A 206 response is heuristically cacheable unless otherwise indicated by explicit cache controls. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | A 206 response is heuristically cacheable; i.e., unless otherwise indicated by explicit cache controls | Cache-layer rule only. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-47` | MUST | For a single-part 206, the server must generate a Content-Range header field describing the enclosed range, with content consisting of that range. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | the server generating the 206 response MUST generate a Content-Range header field, describing what range of the selected representation is enclosed, and a content consisting of the range. | Client's single-part 206 path can require a top-level Content-Range; its absence on a non-multipart 206 is a peer violation worth flagging. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-48` | MUST | For a multi-part 206, the server must generate multipart/byteranges content and a Content-Type field carrying that media type with its required boundary parameter. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | MUST generate "multipart/byteranges" content, as defined in Section 14.6, and a Content-Type header field containing the "multipart/byteranges" media type and its required boundary parameter. | If the client sends multi-range requests, it must detect multipart/byteranges via Content-Type and parse the boundary parameter to split parts. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-49` | MUST NOT | A server must not generate a Content-Range header field in the HTTP header section of a multiple-part 206 response. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | a server MUST NOT generate a Content-Range header field in the HTTP header section of a multiple part response | Client multipart handling must take ranges from per-part headers only; top-level Content-Range plus multipart Content-Type is inconsistent input to reject or flag. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-50` | MUST | Within the header area of each body part of a multipart 206, the server must generate a Content-Range field for that part's range. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | the server MUST generate a Content-Range header field corresponding to the range being enclosed in that body part. | Client's multipart parser can require Content-Range in every part; a part without one is malformed (cf. fact -57 client MUST inspect). |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-51` | SHOULD | If the selected representation would have had a Content-Type in a 200 response, the server should repeat that Content-Type in each body part. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | the server SHOULD generate that same Content-Type header field in the header area of each body part. | Server rule; client should tolerate parts without Content-Type. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-52` | MAY | A server may coalesce requested ranges that overlap or are separated by a small gap, regardless of the order requested. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | a server MAY coalesce any of the ranges that overlap, or that are separated by a gap that is smaller than the overhead of sending multiple parts | Client must not assume returned parts equal requested range-specs in count, extent, or order; derive actual coverage from per-part Content-Range. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-53` | MUST NOT | A server must not generate a multipart response to a request for a single range. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | A server MUST NOT generate a multipart response to a request for a single range | After a single-range request the client may treat multipart/byteranges as a peer violation; only multi-range requests need the multipart parser. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-54` | MAY | A server may generate a multipart/byteranges response with only a single body part when multiple ranges were requested but only one is satisfiable or remains after coalescing. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | a server MAY generate a "multipart/byteranges" response with only a single body part if multiple ranges were requested and only one range was found to be satisfiable | Client's multipart parser must accept a one-part multipart body after a multi-range request. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-55` | MUST NOT | A client that cannot process a multipart/byteranges response must not send a request asking for multiple ranges. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client that cannot process a "multipart/byteranges" response MUST NOT generate a request that asks for multiple ranges. | If the client lacks a multipart/byteranges parser, its request builder must reject or refuse Range headers containing more than one range-spec. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-56` | SHOULD | A server generating a multipart response should send parts in the same order the range-specs appeared in the received Range field, minus unsatisfiable or coalesced ranges. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | A server that generates a multipart response SHOULD send the parts in the same order that the corresponding range-spec appeared in the received Range header field | Server rule only — and per fact -57 the client must NOT rely on this order. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-57` | MUST | A client receiving a multipart response must inspect each body part's Content-Range to determine which range it contains, and cannot rely on receiving the requested ranges or order. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client that receives a multipart response MUST inspect the Content-Range header field present in each body part in order to determine which range is contained in that body part | Multipart consumption must be driven by each part's Content-Range, never by positional matching against the sent Range header; test out-of-order and coalesced parts. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-58` | informative | Ranges received across multiple transfers can only be safely combined if they all share the same strong validator. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | These ranges can only be safely combined if they all have in common the same strong validator (Section 8.8.1). | Any range-combining logic must compare strong validators (strong ETag) across responses and refuse to merge on mismatch or weak/absent validators. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-59` | MAY | A client that received multiple partial responses to GET requests on a target resource may combine them into a larger continuous range if they share the same strong validator. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client that has received multiple partial responses to GET requests on a target resource MAY combine those responses into a larger continuous range if they share the same strong validator. | Combining is optional; if implemented, gate strictly on identical strong validators (fact -58) and follow facts -60..-64 for the merge. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-60` | informative | When combining, if the most recent response is an incomplete 200, its header fields are used for the combined response, replacing those of matching stored responses. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | If the most recent response is an incomplete 200 (OK) response, then the header fields of that response are used for any combined response and replace those of the matching stored responses. | In merge logic, an incomplete 200 as the newest response wins the header set outright. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-61` | informative | If the most recent response is a 206 and at least one matching stored response is a 200, the combined response uses the most recent 200 response's header fields. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | the combined response header fields consist of the most recent 200 response's header fields. | In merge logic, a stored 200's headers take precedence over a newer 206's headers. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-62` | MUST | If all matching stored responses are 206s, use the most recent header fields as the base, but the client must replace stored header fields with those in the new response, except Content-Range. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | the client MUST use other header fields provided in the new response, aside from Content-Range, to replace all instances of the corresponding header fields in the stored response. | When merging 206s, overlay every header from the new response onto the stored set (replacing all instances), excluding Content-Range which is per-message. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-63` | MUST | If the union of combined ranges covers the entire representation, the client must process the combined response as a complete 200, including a Content-Length reflecting the complete length. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | the client MUST process the combined response as if it were a complete 200 (OK) response, including a Content-Length header field that reflects the complete length. | Merge output covering the full range must be promoted to status 200 with a synthesized Content-Length equal to the representation's complete length. |
| `9110 15.1-15.3-64` | MUST | If the union is not the entire representation, the client must process the continuous ranges as an incomplete 200 (if a prefix), a single multipart/byteranges 206, or multiple single-range 206 responses. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | the client MUST process the set of continuous ranges as one of the following: an incomplete 200 (OK) response if the combined response is a prefix of the representation | Partial merge results must be represented only as one of the three sanctioned forms (prefix→incomplete 200; else single multipart 206 or multiple Content-Range-bearing 206s). |

### RFC 9110 15.4 - Redirection 3xx

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|--------------|---------------------|
| `9110 15.4-1` | informative | The 3xx class means the user agent must take further action to fulfill the request. <br>_(user-agent,client [CLIENT])_ | The 3xx (Redirection) class of status code indicates that further action needs to be taken by the user agent in order to fulfill the request. | Classify any 3xx status as redirection requiring follow-up action (or surfacing to caller) rather than a final result. |
| `9110 15.4-2` | MAY | When a Location header is present, the user agent may auto-redirect to that URI even for unrecognized 3xx codes. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | the user agent MAY automatically redirect its request to the URI referenced by the Location field value, even if the specific status code is not understood. | If auto-follow is enabled, treat any 3xx with Location as followable, including unknown codes like 3xx not in the 300-308 list; make auto-follow optional. |
| `9110 15.4-3` | informative | Automatic redirection of methods not known to be safe requires care because the user may not wish to redirect an unsafe request. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | Automatic redirection needs to be done with care for methods not known to be safe | For non-safe methods (POST, PUT, DELETE, etc.), gate auto-follow behind explicit configuration or restrict to method-rewriting codes (301/302/303). |
| `9110 15.4-4` | SHOULD | When automatically following a redirect, the user agent should resend the original request with the enumerated modifications (facts 15.4-5 through 15.4-14). <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | the user agent SHOULD resend the original request message with the following modifications | Redirect follower rebuilds the request from the original, applying the modification list, rather than constructing a fresh unrelated request. |
| `9110 15.4-5` | SHOULD | Replace the target URI with the Location value resolved relative to the original request's target URI. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | Replace the target URI with the URI referenced by the redirection response's Location header field value after resolving it relative to the original request's target URI. | Use RFC 3986 relative reference resolution against the original target URI; do not require Location to be absolute. |
| `9110 15.4-6` | SHOULD | Remove implementation-generated header fields and regenerate them with values appropriate to the new request. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | Remove header fields that were automatically generated by the implementation, replacing them with updated values as appropriate to the new request. | On redirect, strip all headers the client library injected itself and recompute them for the new target rather than copying blindly. |
| `9110 15.4-7` | SHOULD | Among auto-generated fields to remove/replace: connection-specific header fields. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | Connection-specific header fields (see Section 7.6.1) | Strip Connection and any fields it names (e.g., Keep-Alive, TE, Upgrade) before resending to the new target. |
| `9110 15.4-8` | SHOULD | Among auto-generated fields to remove/replace: proxy-configuration fields such as Proxy-Authorization. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | Header fields specific to the client's proxy configuration, including (but not limited to) Proxy-Authorization | Do not carry Proxy-Authorization (or other proxy-config headers) across a redirect; regenerate per the proxy applicable to the new target. |
| `9110 15.4-9` | SHOULD | Among auto-generated fields to remove/replace: origin-specific fields such as Host. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | Origin-specific header fields (if any), including (but not limited to) Host | Recompute Host (and :authority equivalents) from the new target URI; never reuse the old Host on a cross-origin redirect. |
| `9110 15.4-10` | SHOULD | Among auto-generated fields to remove/replace: cache-added validators like If-None-Match and If-Modified-Since. <br>_(user-agent,cache [CLIENT])_ | Validating header fields that were added by the implementation's cache (e.g., If-None-Match, If-Modified-Since) | If the client ever adds cache validators itself, drop them on redirect; validators supplied by the caller are governed by 15.4-12 instead. |
| `9110 15.4-11` | SHOULD | Among auto-generated fields to remove/replace: resource-specific fields including Referer, Origin, Authorization, and Cookie. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | Resource-specific header fields, including (but not limited to) Referer, Origin, Authorization, and Cookie. | Strip implementation-generated Referer/Origin/Authorization/Cookie on redirect and regenerate for the new resource (or omit). |
| `9110 15.4-12` | SHOULD | Consider removing caller-supplied header fields with security implications (notably Authorization and Cookie) before following the redirect. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | Consider removing header fields that were not automatically generated by the implementation (i.e., those present in the request because they were added by the calling context) where there are security implications | At minimum, drop caller-provided Authorization/Cookie on cross-origin redirects (or expose a policy hook); document the chosen policy. |
| `9110 15.4-13` | SHOULD | Change the request method according to the semantics of the specific redirecting status code, where applicable. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | Change the request method according to the redirecting status code's semantics, if applicable. | Per-code method table: 303 rewrites to GET/HEAD; 301/302 may rewrite POST to GET; 307/308 never change the method. |
| `9110 15.4-14` | SHOULD | If the method was changed to GET or HEAD, remove content-specific header fields. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | If the request method has been changed to GET or HEAD, remove content-specific header fields, including (but not limited to) Content-Encoding, Content-Language, Content-Location, Content-Type, Content-Length, Digest, Last-Modified. | When rewriting to GET/HEAD, drop the body and strip Content-* framing/representation headers (Content-Encoding, -Language, -Location, -Type, -Length, Digest, Last-Modified). |
| `9110 15.4-15` | SHOULD | A client should detect and stop cyclical (infinite) redirection loops. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client SHOULD detect and intervene in cyclical redirections (i.e., "infinite" redirection loops). | Enforce a maximum redirect count and/or visited-URI cycle detection; fail with a distinct error when exceeded. |
| `9110 15.4-16` | informative | A former spec version recommended a maximum of five redirections; some clients may still hard-limit at five. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | An earlier version of this specification recommended a maximum of five redirections ([RFC2068], Section 10.3). | No fixed limit is mandated; pick a sane default cap (>=5, commonly 10) and make it configurable. |
| `9110 15.4-17` | informative | 300 means the target resource has multiple representations and the agent may redirect to one of the provided identifiers (reactive negotiation). <br>_(user-agent,server [CLIENT])_ | The 300 (Multiple Choices) status code indicates that the target resource has more than one representation, each with its own more specific identifier | Treat 300 as a redirect only when Location is present; otherwise surface the response (choice list) to the caller. |
| `9110 15.4-18` | SHOULD | A server with a preferred choice should send a Location header carrying that choice's URI in a 300 response. <br>_(server,origin [not-client])_ | If the server has a preferred choice, the server SHOULD generate a Location header field containing a preferred choice's URI reference. | Server-side only; client just checks for Location presence on 300. |
| `9110 15.4-19` | MAY | The user agent may use a 300 response's Location value for automatic redirection. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | The user agent MAY use the Location field value for automatic redirection. | Optionally auto-follow 300 when Location is present; preserve the request method (300 has no method-rewrite provision). |
| `9110 15.4-20` | SHOULD | For non-HEAD requests, the server should include a list of representation metadata and URI references in the 300 content. <br>_(server,origin [not-client])_ | For request methods other than HEAD, the server SHOULD generate content in the 300 response containing a list of representation metadata and URI reference(s) | Server-side only; client may expose the 300 body to the caller for manual selection. |
| `9110 15.4-21` | MAY | The user agent may automatically select from the 300 choice list if it understands the media type. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | The user agent MAY make a selection from that list automatically if it understands the provided media type. | Optional; a low-level client can skip auto-selection and return the list body to the caller. |
| `9110 15.4-22` | informative | A 300 response is heuristically cacheable absent explicit cache controls. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | A 300 response is heuristically cacheable; i.e., unless otherwise indicated by the method definition or explicit cache controls | Cache-only; irrelevant unless the client grows a response cache. |
| `9110 15.4-23` | informative | 301 means the target resource has a new permanent URI and future references ought to use an enclosed URI. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | the target resource has been assigned a new permanent URI and any future references to this resource ought to use one of the enclosed URIs | Treat 301 as permanent: safe to have callers (or a redirect cache, if any) update stored URIs; distinguish from temporary 302/307. |
| `9110 15.4-24` | SHOULD | The server should send a Location header with the preferred new permanent URI in a 301 response. <br>_(server,origin [not-client])_ | The server SHOULD generate a Location header field in the response containing a preferred URI reference for the new permanent URI. | Server-side; client must tolerate a 301 lacking Location (no auto-follow possible, surface to caller). |
| `9110 15.4-25` | MAY | The user agent may use the 301 Location value for automatic redirection. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | The user agent MAY use the Location field value for automatic redirection. | Auto-follow of 301 is permitted, not required; implement behind the redirect-following option. |
| `9110 15.4-26` | MAY | On 301, the user agent may change the request method from POST to GET for the subsequent request. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | a user agent MAY change the request method from POST to GET for the subsequent request. | Rewriting POST to GET on 301 is allowed (and is prevailing practice); if rewritten, apply 15.4-14 header/body stripping. Never rewrite methods other than POST. |
| `9110 15.4-27` | informative | A 301 response is heuristically cacheable absent explicit cache controls. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | A 301 response is heuristically cacheable | Cache-only; irrelevant without a client cache. |
| `9110 15.4-28` | informative | 302 means the target resource resides temporarily at a different URI; the client ought to keep using the original target URI for future requests. <br>_(user-agent,client [CLIENT])_ | The 302 (Found) status code indicates that the target resource resides temporarily under a different URI. | Do not persist or cache the 302 target as a replacement URI; each future request uses the original URI. |
| `9110 15.4-29` | SHOULD | The server should send a Location header with the temporary URI in a 302 response. <br>_(server,origin [not-client])_ | The server SHOULD generate a Location header field in the response containing a URI reference for the different URI. | Server-side; client must tolerate 302 without Location and surface it to the caller. |
| `9110 15.4-30` | MAY | The user agent may use the 302 Location value for automatic redirection. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | The user agent MAY use the Location field value for automatic redirection. | Auto-follow of 302 is optional; implement behind the redirect-following option. |
| `9110 15.4-31` | MAY | On 302, the user agent may change the request method from POST to GET for the subsequent request. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | a user agent MAY change the request method from POST to GET for the subsequent request. | Same as 301: POST-to-GET rewrite permitted on 302 only for POST; apply 15.4-14 stripping when rewriting. |
| `9110 15.4-32` | informative | 303 directs the user agent to fetch a different resource with a retrieval request (GET or HEAD) as an indirect answer to the original request. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | A user agent can perform a retrieval request targeting that URI (a GET or HEAD request if using HTTP), which might also be redirected | When auto-following 303, rewrite any method (except HEAD) to GET, drop the body, and apply 15.4-14 header stripping; the result may itself redirect. |
| `9110 15.4-33` | informative | 303 is applicable to requests made with any HTTP method. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | This status code is applicable to any HTTP method. | Accept and handle 303 in response to any request method, not just POST. |
| `9110 15.4-34` | informative | The 303 Location URI is not considered equivalent to the original target URI. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | the new URI in the Location header field is not considered equivalent to the target URI. | Do not update stored references or cache identity to the 303 target; it identifies a different resource. |
| `9110 15.4-35` | informative | 304 means a conditional GET/HEAD evaluated false and the client's stored representation is still valid, to be used as if it were a 200 response. <br>_(client,server [CLIENT])_ | indicates that a conditional GET or HEAD request has been received and would have resulted in a 200 (OK) response | On 304, do not expect a body; report the stored/conditional-validation outcome to the caller distinctly from 200. |
| `9110 15.4-36` | MUST | A server generating 304 must include any of Content-Location, Date, ETag, Vary, Cache-Control, and Expires that a 200 to the same request would have carried. <br>_(server,origin [not-client])_ | The server generating a 304 response MUST generate any of the following header fields that would have been sent in a 200 (OK) response to the same request | Server obligation; client may rely on these fields for cache-update logic but must not hard-fail if absent. |
| `9110 15.4-37` | SHOULD NOT | A 304 sender should not include representation metadata beyond the listed fields unless it guides cache updates. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | a sender SHOULD NOT generate representation metadata other than the above listed fields unless said metadata exists for the purpose of guiding cache updates | Sender-side; client parser must still accept extra fields (e.g., Last-Modified) on 304 without error. |
| `9110 15.4-38` | SHOULD | A proxy receiving a 304 for a conditional request that originated from an outbound client should forward the 304 to that client. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | the proxy SHOULD forward the 304 response to that client. | Proxy-only; no client action. |
| `9110 15.4-39` | informative | A 304 response ends at the end of its header section and can never carry content or trailers. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | A 304 response is terminated by the end of the header section; it cannot contain content or trailers. | Message-framing rule: the response parser must treat 304 as bodiless regardless of Content-Length or Transfer-Encoding present, and must not wait for or read a body. |
| `9110 15.4-40` | informative | 305 (Use Proxy) is deprecated and its previous behavior must not be implemented. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The 305 (Use Proxy) status code was defined in a previous version of this specification and is now deprecated | Never honor 305 by switching to a proxy; treat it as a generic 3xx (followable only per 15.4-2 if Location present, otherwise surface). |
| `9110 15.4-41` | informative | 306 is unused and the code is reserved. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The 306 status code was defined in a previous version of this specification, is no longer used, and the code is reserved. | Treat 306 as an unrecognized 3xx: no special semantics, generic handling only. |
| `9110 15.4-42` | MUST NOT | On 307, the user agent must not change the request method when automatically redirecting. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | the user agent MUST NOT change the request method if it performs an automatic redirection to that URI. | Auto-follow of 307 resends with the identical method (and body); any POST-to-GET rewrite on 307 is a conformance bug. Test: 307 after POST stays POST. |
| `9110 15.4-43` | informative | After a 307, the client ought to continue using the original target URI for future requests. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | the client ought to continue using the original target URI for future requests. | Do not persist the 307 target; like 302, the redirect is temporary. |
| `9110 15.4-44` | SHOULD | The server should send a Location header with the temporary URI in a 307 response. <br>_(server,origin [not-client])_ | The server SHOULD generate a Location header field in the response containing a URI reference for the different URI. | Server-side; client must tolerate 307 without Location and surface it to the caller. |
| `9110 15.4-45` | MAY | The user agent may use the 307 Location value for automatic redirection. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | The user agent MAY use the Location field value for automatic redirection. | Auto-follow of 307 is optional; when following, 15.4-42 (method preservation) is mandatory. |
| `9110 15.4-46` | informative | 308 means the target resource has a permanent new URI; unlike 301, no POST-to-GET rewrite is sanctioned, so the method is preserved. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | The 308 (Permanent Redirect) status code indicates that the target resource has been assigned a new permanent URI | Follow 308 with the identical method and body (the section grants no method-change MAY for 308); permanence semantics as 301. Test: 308 after POST stays POST. |
| `9110 15.4-47` | SHOULD | The server should send a Location header with the preferred new permanent URI in a 308 response. <br>_(server,origin [not-client])_ | The server SHOULD generate a Location header field in the response containing a preferred URI reference for the new permanent URI. | Server-side; client must tolerate 308 without Location and surface it to the caller. |
| `9110 15.4-48` | MAY | The user agent may use the 308 Location value for automatic redirection. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | The user agent MAY use the Location field value for automatic redirection. | Auto-follow of 308 is optional; when following, preserve the method per 15.4-46. |
| `9110 15.4-49` | informative | A 308 response is heuristically cacheable absent explicit cache controls. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | A 308 response is heuristically cacheable | Cache-only; irrelevant without a client cache. |

### RFC 9110 15.5-15.6 - RFC 9110 — Status Codes: Client Error 4xx and Server Error 5xx

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|--------------|---------------------|
| `9110 15.5-15.6-1` | informative | The 4xx class signals that the fault is perceived to be on the client's side. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The 4xx (Client Error) class of status code indicates that the client seems to have erred. | Classify any 4xx response as a client-side error; default retry policy must not blindly re-send an unchanged request. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-2` | SHOULD | For 4xx responses, the server should include a representation explaining the error, except for HEAD. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | Except when responding to a HEAD request, the server SHOULD send a representation containing an explanation of the error situation | Client must be prepared to read/drain a body on any 4xx (and expect no body after HEAD). |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-3` | informative | 4xx status codes may be returned for any request method. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | These status codes are applicable to any request method. | Accept any 4xx code regardless of the request method sent; no method/status validity matrix for 4xx. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-4` | SHOULD | User agents should display an included 4xx error representation to the user. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | User agents SHOULD display any included representation to the user. | Library client: return the error body to the caller intact rather than discarding it. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-5` | informative | 400 means the server cannot or will not process the request due to a perceived client error. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | the server cannot or will not process the request due to something that is perceived to be a client error | Treat 400 as terminal for the request as sent; never auto-retry an identical request on 400. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-6` | informative | 401 means the request lacks valid authentication credentials for the target resource. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | the request has not been applied because it lacks valid authentication credentials for the target resource | Map 401 to an authentication-required condition, not a generic failure. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-7` | MUST | A server generating 401 must send a WWW-Authenticate header with at least one applicable challenge. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | The server generating a 401 response MUST send a WWW-Authenticate header field (Section 11.6.1) containing at least one challenge applicable to the target resource. | Client may rely on WWW-Authenticate being present on 401 to drive auth, but must not crash if a non-conforming server omits it. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-8` | MAY | After 401, the user agent may repeat the request with a new or replaced Authorization header. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | The user agent MAY repeat the request with a new or replaced Authorization header field | If auto-auth is implemented, retry on 401 only with new/replaced credentials, never identical ones in a loop. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-9` | SHOULD | If a 401 repeats the same challenge after at least one auth attempt, the user agent should present the enclosed representation to the user. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | the user agent SHOULD present the enclosed representation to the user, since it usually contains relevant diagnostic information | Stop auth retry loops when the challenge is unchanged after one attempt; surface the response body. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-10` | informative | 402 is reserved for future use. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | The 402 (Payment Required) status code is reserved for future use. | Treat 402 as a generic 4xx with no special semantics. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-11` | informative | 403 means the server understood the request but refuses to fulfill it. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | the server understood the request but refuses to fulfill it | Map 403 to a refusal distinct from auth-missing (401); credentials, if sent, were insufficient. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-12` | SHOULD NOT | On 403, the client should not automatically repeat the request with the same credentials. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | The client SHOULD NOT automatically repeat the request with the same credentials. | Retry logic must exclude 403 from automatic retry when the request would carry identical credentials. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-13` | MAY | On 403, the client may repeat the request with new or different credentials. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | The client MAY repeat the request with new or different credentials. | Permit caller-driven retry of a 403 with changed credentials; do not hard-fail the connection. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-14` | MAY | An origin server may answer 404 instead of 403 to hide a forbidden resource's existence. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | An origin server that wishes to "hide" the current existence of a forbidden target resource MAY instead respond with a status code of 404 (Not Found). | Server-side option; client must not infer resource non-existence guarantees from 404. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-15` | informative | 404 means the origin found no current representation or will not disclose that one exists. <br>_(origin [CLIENT])_ | the origin server did not find a current representation for the target resource or is not willing to disclose that one exists | Treat 404 as not-found without inferring whether the condition is temporary or permanent. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-16` | informative | 410 is preferred over 404 when the origin knows the condition is likely permanent. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | the 410 (Gone) status code is preferred over 404 if the origin server knows, presumably through some configurable means, that the condition is likely to be permanent | Origin guidance only; nothing to implement in the client. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-17` | informative | A 404 response is heuristically cacheable by default. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | A 404 response is heuristically cacheable | Applies only if a client-side cache exists (poseidon has none); a cache may store 404 absent explicit controls. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-18` | informative | 405 means the request-line method is known to the origin but not supported by the target resource. <br>_(origin [CLIENT])_ | the method received in the request-line is known by the origin server but not supported by the target resource | Treat 405 as method-not-allowed for this resource; do not retry with the same method. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-19` | MUST | The origin server must include an Allow header in a 405 listing the resource's supported methods. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | The origin server MUST generate an Allow header field in a 405 response containing a list of the target resource's currently supported methods. | Server obligation; client may optionally surface Allow to the caller but need not enforce anything. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-20` | informative | A 405 response is heuristically cacheable by default. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | A 405 response is heuristically cacheable | Cache-only rule; no action for a cacheless client. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-21` | informative | 406 means no current representation is acceptable per the request's proactive negotiation headers. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | the target resource does not have a current representation that would be acceptable to the user agent | Map 406 to content-negotiation failure; relevant only if the client sent Accept* fields. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-22` | SHOULD | On 406, the server should send a list of available representation characteristics and identifiers. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | The server SHOULD generate content containing a list of available representation characteristics and corresponding resource identifiers | Server obligation; client may pass the body through for caller-driven selection. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-23` | MAY | A user agent may automatically select the most appropriate choice from a 406 alternatives list. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | A user agent MAY automatically select the most appropriate choice from that list. | Optional; if implemented, note no standard selection algorithm is defined. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-24` | informative | 407 means the client must authenticate itself to use a proxy for this request. <br>_(proxy,client [CLIENT])_ | it indicates that the client needs to authenticate itself in order to use a proxy for this request | Distinguish 407 (proxy auth) from 401 (origin auth) in error mapping. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-25` | MUST | The proxy must send a Proxy-Authenticate header in a 407 with a challenge applicable to that proxy. <br>_(proxy [CLIENT])_ | The proxy MUST send a Proxy-Authenticate header field (Section 11.7.1) containing a challenge applicable to that proxy for the request. | A client implementing proxy auth parses Proxy-Authenticate from 407; tolerate absence without crashing. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-26` | MAY | After 407, the client may repeat the request with a new or replaced Proxy-Authorization header. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | The client MAY repeat the request with a new or replaced Proxy-Authorization header field | If proxy auth is supported, retry on 407 only with new/replaced Proxy-Authorization. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-27` | informative | 408 means the server did not receive a complete request within its wait time. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | the server did not receive a complete request message within the time that it was prepared to wait | Map 408 to a server-side idle/read timeout, a retriable condition. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-28` | MAY | A client with an outstanding request in transit may repeat that request after 408. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | If the client has an outstanding request in transit, it MAY repeat that request. | 408 is safe to auto-retry even for non-idempotent methods when the request was still in transit. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-29` | informative | If the connection is unusable after 408 (HTTP/1.1 request delimitation lost), a new connection is used for the retry. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | If the current connection is not usable (e.g., as it would be in HTTP/1.1 because request delimitation is lost), a new connection will be used. | After a 408 interrupting a partially sent HTTP/1.1 request, do not reuse the connection; retry on a fresh one and evict from pool. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-30` | informative | 409 means the request conflicts with the current state of the target resource and the user might resolve and resubmit. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | the request could not be completed due to a conflict with the current state of the target resource | Treat 409 as resolvable-by-caller; do not auto-retry an unchanged request. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-31` | SHOULD | On 409, the server should send content sufficient to recognize the conflict source. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | The server SHOULD generate content that includes enough information for a user to recognize the source of the conflict. | Server obligation; client just passes the body through. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-32` | informative | 410 means access to the resource is gone at the origin and the condition is likely permanent. <br>_(origin [CLIENT])_ | access to the target resource is no longer available at the origin server and that this condition is likely to be permanent | Treat 410 as permanent not-found; never auto-retry, and callers can drop stored links. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-33` | informative | If the origin cannot determine permanence, 404 ought to be used instead of 410. <br>_(origin [not-client])_ | the status code 404 (Not Found) ought to be used instead | Origin guidance; no client action. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-34` | informative | A 410 response is heuristically cacheable by default. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | A 410 response is heuristically cacheable | Cache-only rule; no action for a cacheless client. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-35` | informative | 411 means the server refuses the request without a defined Content-Length. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | the server refuses to accept the request without a defined Content-Length | Map 411 to missing-Content-Length; a retry must switch from chunked/unknown-length to a declared Content-Length. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-36` | MAY | After 411, the client may repeat the request if it adds a valid Content-Length. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | The client MAY repeat the request if it adds a valid Content-Length header field containing the length of the request content. | Only retry a 411 with a correct Content-Length added; identical resend is pointless. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-37` | informative | 412 means one or more request preconditions evaluated to false on the server. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | one or more conditions given in the request header fields evaluated to false when tested on the server | Map 412 to precondition failure (If-Match etc.); surface to caller, do not auto-retry unchanged. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-38` | informative | 413 means the server refuses the request because the request content is too large. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | the server is refusing to process a request because the request content is larger than the server is willing or able to process | Map 413 to request-body-too-large; do not auto-retry with the same body. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-39` | MAY | On 413, the server may terminate the request if the protocol allows, or may close the connection. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | The server MAY terminate the request, if the protocol version in use allows it; otherwise, the server MAY close the connection. | Client must tolerate an early 413 arriving and the connection closing while it is still sending the request body, without treating it as an I/O bug. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-40` | SHOULD | If a 413 condition is temporary, the server should send Retry-After, after which the client may try again. <br>_(server,client [CLIENT])_ | the server SHOULD generate a Retry-After header field to indicate that it is temporary and after what time the client MAY try again | Honor Retry-After on 413: only treat as retriable when present, and wait the indicated time before retrying. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-41` | informative | 414 means the target URI is longer than the server is willing to interpret. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | the server is refusing to service the request because the target URI is longer than the server is willing to interpret | Map 414 to URI-too-long; not retriable without a shorter URI (also guards against redirect loops growing the URI). |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-42` | informative | A 414 response is heuristically cacheable by default. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | A 414 response is heuristically cacheable | Cache-only rule; no action for a cacheless client. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-43` | informative | 415 means the origin refuses the request because the content format is unsupported for this method on this resource. <br>_(origin [CLIENT])_ | the origin server is refusing to service the request because the content is in a format not supported by this method on the target resource | Map 415 to unsupported media/coding; retry only after changing Content-Type or Content-Encoding. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-44` | informative | For an unsupported content coding, the 415 response ought to carry Accept-Encoding listing acceptable codings. <br>_(origin [CLIENT])_ | the Accept-Encoding response header field (Section 12.5.3) ought to be used to indicate which (if any) content codings would have been accepted | On 415, check the response's Accept-Encoding to pick a supported request content coding for a retry. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-45` | informative | For an unsupported media type, the 415 response can carry Accept listing acceptable media types. <br>_(origin [CLIENT])_ | the Accept response header field (Section 12.5.1) can be used to indicate which media types would have been accepted in the request | On 415, check the response's Accept field to pick a supported request media type for a retry. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-46` | informative | 416 means the Range header's range set was rejected as unsatisfiable or as an excessive/overlapping set. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | the set of ranges in the request's Range header field (Section 14.2) has been rejected either because none of the requested ranges are satisfiable | Map 416 to range failure; do not re-issue the same Range set. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-47` | SHOULD | A server generating 416 to a byte-range request should include Content-Range with the current representation length. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | A server that generates a 416 response to a byte-range request SHOULD generate a Content-Range header field specifying the current length of the selected representation | Parse an unsatisfied-range Content-Range (e.g. "bytes */47022") on 416 to learn the representation length for corrected ranges. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-48` | informative | Clients cannot depend on receiving 416; servers are free to ignore Range and return 200 with the full representation. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | clients cannot depend on receiving a 416 (Range Not Satisfiable) response even when it is most appropriate | Range-request code must handle a 200 full-body response in place of 206 or 416 without error. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-49` | informative | 417 means the request's Expect header expectation could not be met by at least one inbound server. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | the expectation given in the request's Expect header field (Section 10.1.1) could not be met by at least one of the inbound servers | If the client sends Expect (e.g. 100-continue), handle 417 by abandoning the expectation (per §10.1.1) rather than retrying identically. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-50` | informative | 418 is reserved in the IANA registry and unusable for any current application. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | the 418 status code is reserved in the IANA HTTP Status Code Registry | Treat 418 as an unrecognized 4xx (i.e., like 400 per generic class handling); assign no special semantics. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-51` | informative | 421 means the request reached a server unable or unwilling to produce an authoritative response for the target URI. <br>_(origin,gateway [CLIENT])_ | the request was directed at a server that is unable or unwilling to produce an authoritative response for the target URI | Map 421 to misdirected-request, typically caused by connection reuse/coalescing to the wrong origin. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-52` | MAY | A client receiving 421 may retry the request over a different connection, even for non-idempotent methods. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | A client that receives a 421 (Misdirected Request) response MAY retry the request, whether or not the request method is idempotent, over a different connection | Retry policy: 421 is retriable regardless of idempotency, but only on a fresh connection specific to the target origin (never the same pooled conn). |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-53` | MUST NOT | A proxy must not generate a 421 response. <br>_(proxy [not-client])_ | A proxy MUST NOT generate a 421 response. | Proxy-only prohibition; a client may treat any received 421 as originating from the origin or its gateway. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-54` | informative | 422 means the content type and syntax were understood/correct but the contained instructions could not be processed. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | the syntax of the request content is correct, but it was unable to process the contained instructions | Map 422 to semantic content error; terminal for the request as sent, no auto-retry. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-55` | informative | 426 means the server refuses the request on the current protocol but may accept it after a protocol upgrade. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | the server refuses to perform the request using the current protocol but might be willing to do so after the client upgrades to a different protocol | Map 426 to upgrade-required; do not retry on the same protocol version. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-56` | MUST | The server must send an Upgrade header in a 426 response indicating the required protocol(s). <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | The server MUST send an Upgrade header field in a 426 response to indicate the required protocol(s) | Client should read the Upgrade field from 426 to learn the required protocol; surface it to the caller (poseidon does no in-place upgrade). |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-57` | informative | The 5xx class signals the server knows it erred or cannot perform the requested method. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | the server is aware that it has erred or is incapable of performing the requested method | Classify 5xx as server-side failure; eligible for retry policies (unlike most 4xx). |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-58` | SHOULD | For 5xx responses, the server should include a representation explaining the error, except for HEAD. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | Except when responding to a HEAD request, the server SHOULD send a representation containing an explanation of the error situation | Client must be prepared to read/drain a body on any 5xx (and expect no body after HEAD). |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-59` | SHOULD | A user agent should display an included 5xx error representation to the user. <br>_(user-agent [CLIENT])_ | A user agent SHOULD display any included representation to the user. | Library client: return the 5xx error body to the caller intact. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-60` | informative | 5xx status codes may be returned for any request method. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | These status codes are applicable to any request method. | Accept any 5xx code regardless of the request method sent. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-61` | informative | 500 means the server hit an unexpected condition preventing fulfillment. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | the server encountered an unexpected condition that prevented it from fulfilling the request | Map 500 to generic server failure; retriable only per idempotency-aware retry policy. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-62` | informative | 501 means the server does not support the functionality required, e.g. it does not recognize the request method. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | the server does not support the functionality required to fulfill the request | Treat 501 as not-retriable with the same method; the server lacks the capability entirely. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-63` | informative | A 501 response is heuristically cacheable by default. <br>_(cache [not-client])_ | A 501 response is heuristically cacheable | Cache-only rule; no action for a cacheless client. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-64` | informative | 502 means a gateway/proxy received an invalid response from an inbound server. <br>_(gateway,proxy [CLIENT])_ | the server, while acting as a gateway or proxy, received an invalid response from an inbound server it accessed while attempting to fulfill the request | Map 502 to upstream failure at an intermediary; a common auto-retry candidate under idempotency rules. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-65` | informative | 503 means the server is temporarily unable to handle the request due to overload or maintenance. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | the server is currently unable to handle the request due to a temporary overload or scheduled maintenance | Treat 503 as explicitly temporary; prime retry-with-backoff candidate. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-66` | MAY | With 503, the server may send Retry-After suggesting how long the client should wait before retrying. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | The server MAY send a Retry-After header field (Section 10.2.3) to suggest an appropriate amount of time for the client to wait before retrying the request. | Retry logic must parse and honor Retry-After (both delay-seconds and HTTP-date forms) on 503 when present. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-67` | informative | Servers are not obliged to use 503 when overloaded; some simply refuse the connection. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | The existence of the 503 status code does not imply that a server has to use it when becoming overloaded. Some servers might simply refuse the connection. | Client retry/backoff must treat connection refusal as a possible overload signal equivalent to 503, not only the status code. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-68` | informative | 504 means a gateway/proxy did not receive a timely response from an upstream server. <br>_(gateway,proxy [CLIENT])_ | the server, while acting as a gateway or proxy, did not receive a timely response from an upstream server it needed to access in order to complete the request | Map 504 to upstream timeout at an intermediary; retry only per idempotency-aware policy (request may have been processed upstream). |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-69` | informative | 505 means the server does not support, or refuses to support, the request's major HTTP version. <br>_(server [CLIENT])_ | the server does not support, or refuses to support, the major version of HTTP that was used in the request message | On 505, do not retry with the same major protocol version; surface as a version-negotiation failure. |
| `9110 15.5-15.6-70` | SHOULD | The server should generate a 505 representation describing why the version is unsupported and what protocols it supports. <br>_(server [not-client])_ | The server SHOULD generate a representation for the 505 response that describes why that version is not supported and what other protocols are supported by that server. | Server obligation; client may pass the body through to the caller. |

### RFC 9110 17 - Security Considerations (RFC 9110)

| ID | Level | Requirement (scope) | RFC verbatim | Impl check / status |
|----|-------|---------------------|--------------|---------------------|
| `9110 17-1` | informative | Security considerations for HTTP/1.1 message syntax and parsing are defined elsewhere, in RFC 9112 Section 11, not in this section. <br>_(all [CLIENT])_ | considerations related to HTTP/1.1 message syntax and parsing are discussed in Section 11 of [HTTP/1.1] | No parsing grammar or syntax-security rules live here; extract RFC 9112 Section 11 separately for request-smuggling/chunked-parsing requirements binding the client parser. |
| `9110 17-2` | informative | The https scheme only defeats authority attacks if the client verifies over a secured connection that the server's identity matches the target URI's authority component. <br>_(client [CLIENT])_ | provided that the negotiated connection is secured and the client properly verifies that the communicating server's identity matches the target URI's authority component | For https targets, perform full TLS certificate verification against the URI host (per Section 4.3.4) by default; any skip-verify knob must be explicit caller opt-in (repo already treats G402 as caller opt-in). |
| `9110 17-3` | informative | Responses from non-authoritative sources (e.g., shared proxy caches) are useful only to the extent the source is trusted or the distrusted response can be safely used. <br>_(client,proxy,cache [CLIENT])_ | but only to the extent that the source can be trusted or the distrusted response can be safely used | Treat any configured intermediary as trusted-by-configuration only; never silently route through unconfigured proxies or accept responses from unexpected sources. |
| `9110 17-4` | informative | User agents can reduce phishing impact by letting users easily inspect the target URI before acting. <br>_(user-agent [not-client])_ | enabling users to easily inspect a target URI prior to making an action | Browser-UI concern; not applicable to a programmatic client library. Nothing to implement. |
